# The End of the Cold War and the New World Order - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches the period between August 1988 and the middle of the 1990s as four separate endings that happened to overlap - the ending of a rivalry, the ending of a bloc, the ending of a multinational state, and the attempted beginning of an order - and it insists throughout that running them together is the commonest way to lose marks on this part of the syllabus. It begins from the structural pressures inside the late Soviet system: a command economy that could not convert vast resources into consumer welfare, a leadership that admitted the absurdity in its own words, an imperial and military burden that included Afghanistan, and a federation of fifteen republics whose parliaments could become instruments of exit the moment the centre weakened. It then teaches Gorbachev's project exactly as the record states it - that he came to power in March 1985 intending to replace a still basically Stalinist system with a humane and democratic socialism inside the Marxist-Leninist one-party framework, and that his policies failed at home while succeeding abroad. It teaches glasnost and perestroika as two different mechanisms with two different speeds, and it teaches the sequencing problem that follows: criticism became legal faster than the shops filled. It teaches what went wrong through the record's own four headings - the split between radicals like Yeltsin and conservatives like Ligachev, the economic reforms that did not deliver, the nationalist pressures from Nagorno-Karabakh to the Baltic republics, and the rivalry between Gorbachev and Yeltsin - and it teaches the July 1989 Siberian miners' strike, the first major strike since 1917, as the moment at which reform lost control of its own constituency. It teaches 1989 in Eastern Europe as one regional wave with five different national routes: Poland's negotiated exit through Solidarity's August 1988 strikes, the April 1989 constitutional agreement and the June 1989 elections; Hungary's reform-communist surrender and the opening of the Austrian frontier; East Germany's demonstrations, elite split and the breach of the Berlin Wall on 9 November 1989; Czechoslovakia's Velvet Revolution and Havel's election on 29 December 1989; and Romania's short and bloody overthrow ending in the execution of the Ceausescus on 25 December 1989. It teaches German reunification as a revolution completed by diplomacy - the Two Plus Four settlement signed in Moscow on 12 September 1990, legal reunification at midnight on 3 October 1990 - and it teaches the Soviet dissolution as a state process rather than a resignation, running from Lithuania's declaration of March 1990 through the failed August 1991 coup to the republican decisions of December 1991 and Gorbachev's resignation on 25 December 1991. It teaches the aftermath honestly: shock therapy from January 1992, prices on average thirty times higher by the end of that year, the financiers who had acquired most of former state industry by the end of 1995, and the recorded judgement that the collapse of communism did not automatically create either a healthy democracy or a fair market economy. It then turns to the order that was claimed to be replacing bipolarity. It teaches the phrase "new world order" with its speaker and date, the Gulf War of 1990-91 as Security Council-authorised coalition enforcement rather than blue-helmet peacekeeping, and the aftermath in which the same coalition left Saddam Hussein in power. It teaches Yugoslavia as nationalism produced by politics rather than inherited from antiquity, with Milosevic and Tudjman named as agents, with every mortality figure carried with the name of whoever produced it, and with the Dayton conference of November 1995 as a settlement that ended one war without settling Kosovo. It teaches Kosovo in 1999 as the case on which the whole post-Cold-War argument about humanitarian intervention, authorisation and sovereignty turns. It teaches European integration as a bargain repeatedly renegotiated between peace, prosperity, sovereignty and geopolitical anchoring, from the European Coal and Steel Community of 1951 to the eastern enlargement of 1 May 2004 and beyond. It teaches NATO's enlargement as the other half of that institutional settlement, and it states the dispute rather than resolving it. It teaches globalisation, the Global South's continuing agency and India's simultaneous strategic and economic recalibration of 1991 as the correctives to a convergence story. And it closes by teaching the interpretations themselves - structuralist, agency-centred, Western-triumphalist, social-historical and postcolonial - without teleology, because the examiner's real test on this topic is whether a candidate can explain a collapse without implying that it had to happen. Five boundaries are held throughout and stated in the lessons: the long Cold War is studied as a topic in its own right, United Nations design and institutional performance are studied under the United Nations, China's revolution and reform are studied under China, the thematic world economy is studied under the world economy, and present-day international relations and India's domestic reform package are studied under international relations and the economy.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of any single chapter, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates the Soviet leadership, then the satellites, then the wars, then the institutions, each in its own place. That arrangement produces the two commonest failing scripts on this topic: a Gorbachev biography with the satellites appended, or a list of dated events between 1989 and 2004 with no mechanism joining them. Here the structural pressures come first, then the reform project, then what the reform released, so that by the time the Berlin Wall appears the reader already possesses the two variables whose interaction the whole period is about - a system losing the capacity to deliver, and a centre choosing not to shoot. The Eastern European cases are then not a list but a controlled comparison: the same external permission produced five different national outcomes, and the difference is the analysis. The dissolution of the USSR is placed after Eastern Europe, not before, because the satellites' success is one of the things that showed the Soviet republics what was possible. The order question comes after the collapse, because "was there a new world order?" cannot be assessed until the reader knows precisely what disappeared. Yugoslavia and Kosovo come after the Gulf War, because the Gulf case is the strongest evidence for the order claim and the Balkan cases are the strongest evidence against it, and reversing them makes the argument feel like pessimism rather than evidence. European integration comes late because its meaning changes retrospectively: a device for containing Germany becomes a device for absorbing the post-communist east, and that second function only makes sense once the east has collapsed. The interpretations lesson comes last, because a theory offered before the evidence becomes a template, and a template is exactly what this topic punishes.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session. Separating them matters here because this is a part of the syllabus where confident sentences are cheap - about the triumph of the West, about the failure of socialism, about ancient Balkan hatreds - and where almost none of the confident sentences carry a source.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two knowledge files held for this topic, the single core world-history account read for this session, the neighbouring-topic files consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or one of the standard connecting facts checked and listed in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, named individuals, institutions, recorded policies and stated outcomes can be written flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated official item, used exactly once in this session and limited there in the text itself | One dated sentence at most, and never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Attributed estimate** | A figure for deaths, casualties, refugees or displacement, always carried with the name of whoever produced it | Attribute it in the sentence. Never state such a figure as an unowned fact |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself. ⚠️ This topic contains a war in which the core account itself uses the words "perhaps the ultimate act of barbarism", and in which the figures in circulation come from courts, tribunals, governments and journalists counting different things over different periods. An answer that writes "hundreds of thousands died in the Balkans" has asserted something no careful source states in that form; an answer that writes "the account read for this session records about 8,000 Muslims killed at Srebrenica in July 1995, and the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia characterised that killing as genocide in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004" has made a checkable statement and has demonstrated exactly the discipline a 20-mark question rewards.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where the temptation to write a verdict before the evidence is unusually strong.

**The four-endings discipline.** ⚠️ "The end of the Cold War" names four different things that are usually merged. ✅ The **rivalry** ended by agreement, and the record dates it: in November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were "no longer adversaries". ✅ The **bloc** ended between August 1988 and the end of 1991 as the satellite regimes fell one by one. ✅ The **state** ended in December 1991 when the union dissolved into separate republics. ⚠️ The **order** was announced rather than achieved, and whether it arrived is the topic's central analytical question. ⚠️ An answer that fuses the four has not compressed the topic; it has lost the distinctions on which every close question is built.

**The rescue-not-demolition discipline.** ✅ The record is explicit: Gorbachev "did not want to end communism; he wanted to replace the existing system, which was still basically Stalinist, with a socialist system which was humane and democratic", and he "sincerely believed that this could be achieved within the framework of the Marxist-Leninist one-party state". ⚠️ Every sentence in an answer that treats him as a liberaliser who set out to dismantle Soviet communism is factually wrong and analytically self-defeating, because the interest of the case lies precisely in a rescue that destroyed what it was rescuing.

**The permission-and-agency discipline.** ⚠️ Two halves make one explanation, and naming only one produces a weak answer. ✅ Moscow's restraint created the opportunity: critics "became more daring as they realized that Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them". ✅ Organised society supplied the agency that used it: the process "began in Poland in August 1988 when the 'Solidarity' trade union organized huge anti-government strikes". ⚠️ Naming only the first makes 1989 a Kremlin decision; naming only the second makes it inevitable. The record contains both, and so must the answer.

**The category discipline.** ⚠️ Three legal and institutional categories are routinely confused on this topic, and each confusion has a standard close option attached. ✅ Security Council-**authorised coalition enforcement** by member states, as in the Gulf in 1991, is not ✅ consent-based **peacekeeping** by United Nations contingents, as UNPROFOR attempted in Bosnia, and neither is ✅ **alliance action without express Council authorisation**, as NATO's Kosovo air campaign was in 1999. ⚠️ Writing "the UN went to war with Iraq" or "the UN bombed Serbia" forfeits the marks that the distinction was set to test.

**The attribution discipline.** ⚠️ Every mortality, casualty and refugee figure in this session travels with the name of whoever produced it and the period it covers. ❌ No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated or combined across sources. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to named governments, leaders, armies and militias - never to a nation or a people. ⚠️ Where the core account itself gives an estimate or a range, it is preserved as one and labelled as one.

**The non-teleology discipline.** ⚠️ The most damaging sentence a candidate can write on this topic is one that treats 1991 as proof that history had a destination. ✅ The evidence held here refutes it directly: the same collapse produced a European Union enlargement in 2004 and a war in Bosnia; a negotiated German settlement and a violent Romanian overthrow; a Czech-Slovak separation agreed peacefully in 1992 and a Serb-Croat war fought over a comparable question. ⚠️ Divergent outcomes from a shared cause are the strongest available evidence that no single destination was in operation.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics cover material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. The long Cold War - containment, Berlin in 1948 and 1961, Korea, Cuba, Vietnam, deterrence, non-alignment and detente - is studied as the Cold War topic in its own right; this session begins where that rivalry enters its terminal phase. United Nations structure, the veto, the peacekeeping record as an institutional question and reform logic are studied under the United Nations; this session uses the Council only to fix the legal category of particular actions. China's revolution, Maoist consolidation and post-1978 reform are studied under China; this session uses China only for the one comparison the account's own end-of-chapter questions invite. The thematic world economy - Bretton Woods, the North-South divide, oil, debt and the 2008 crisis - is studied under the world economy; this session teaches globalisation only as a component of the order claim. And present-day international relations, India's 1991 reform package and India's contemporary strategic posture are studied under international relations and the economy; this session establishes the changed systemic context and stops.

## Roadmap

Twenty-one lessons. The count is set by the number of distinct causal and institutional problems this topic contains, not by a template, and the lessons are deliberately unequal: Lesson 13 on explanation and Lesson 21 on interpretation and answer architecture are long because the evidence is contested, while Lesson 10 on the institutional termination of the rivalry is tight because it teaches four dated instruments with unusual clarity.

| # | Stage | Lesson | What it settles |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Foundation | Four endings inside one phrase | What the topic is actually about, and what it is not |
| 2 | Foundation | The late-Soviet structural pressures | Why reform had become urgent by 1985 |
| 3 | Foundation | Legitimacy, nationalities and federal design | Why a multinational party-state was structurally exposed |
| 4 | Core | Gorbachev I: glasnost and perestroika | The rescue project, in its own terms |
| 5 | Core | Gorbachev II: new thinking abroad | Why the satellites stopped expecting rescue |
| 6 | Core | Why the reforms failed at home | Four recorded mechanisms of loss of control |
| 7 | Core | 1989 I: Poland and Hungary | Negotiated exit, and what made it possible |
| 8 | Core | 1989 II: the other four routes | One wave, five national outcomes |
| 9 | Core | German reunification | Revolution completed by diplomacy |
| 10 | Core | The institutional termination, 1990-91 | How a rivalry is formally ended |
| 11 | Core | Soviet dissolution, 1990-91 | Why a state, not just a government, ended |
| 12 | Core | The aftermath in Russia | What collapse did not automatically produce |
| 13 | Core | Explaining 1989-91 | Six causal families, four readings, one contingency test |
| 14 | Core | The unipolar moment | The order claim, its author and its three tests |
| 15 | Core | The Gulf War, 1990-91 | The strongest evidence for the claim, and its limits |
| 16 | Core | Yugoslavia | Nationalism as political production, strictly attributed |
| 17 | Core | Kosovo 1999 | Humanitarian intervention, authorisation and sovereignty |
| 18 | Advanced | European integration | Peace project into enlargement project |
| 19 | Advanced | NATO enlargement | The other half of the institutional settlement |
| 20 | Advanced | Globalisation, the Global South and India | Convergence claimed, divergence recorded |
| 21 | Advanced | Continuity, rupture and the answer architecture | Interpretation without teleology, and how to write it |

Practice is embedded: every lesson ends with a concept check, and cumulative checks follow each major block. Verified question routing, original Mains practice with model answers, remediation, master maps and consolidated register notes come after the lesson sequence.

## Lesson 1 - Four endings inside one phrase, and the scope this topic actually owns

⚠️ The diagram is the whole lesson in one image. Four processes with four different kinds of evidence were completed within about three years of each other, and because they overlapped in time, candidates treat them as one event. They are not. Each has its own actors, its own dates and its own examinable distinction, and the most common close option on this topic is built by swapping one for another.

### 1. Start from the learner's real difficulty

The difficulty is not remembering that the Berlin Wall opened in 1989 and the USSR dissolved in 1991. Almost everyone remembers that. The difficulty is that these two facts sit in the mind as a single blur called "the collapse of communism", and a blur cannot be argued with.

Ask a precise question and the blur dissolves. When exactly did the Cold War end? ⚠️ If the Cold War is a **rivalry between two states and two alliance systems**, then it ended when those states and alliances said so. ✅ The record is unambiguous: "In November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were 'no longer adversaries', and that none of their weapons would ever be used except in self-defence. The Cold War was over." ⚠️ Note what that sentence does **not** say. It does not say that the Soviet Union had ended - it had not, and would not for another thirteen months. It does not say that communism had ended in Russia - it had not. It says a rivalry was formally terminated by the parties to it.

Now ask a second question. When did the Soviet bloc end? ✅ The account fixes its own window: "Remarkable events happened in eastern Europe in the period August 1988 to December 1991. Communism was swept away by a rising tide of popular opposition and mass demonstrations, far more quickly than anybody could ever have imagined." ⚠️ That is a different process with a different mechanism: not negotiation between alliances, but the sequential failure of six or seven national regimes.

A third question. When did the Soviet state end? ✅ "By the end of December 1991, the USSR itself had split up into separate republics and Gorbachev had resigned. Communist rule in Russia was over after 74 years." ⚠️ That is a third process again: a federation dissolving into its constituent units.

And a fourth. When did the new order begin? ⚠️ It did not begin on any date, because it was a claim rather than an event - and evaluating claims is what the 15- and 20-mark questions on this topic actually ask for.

### 2. Why the separation earns marks rather than merely being tidy

⚠️ Consider what happens when the four are merged. A candidate writes: "The fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989 ended the Cold War and dissolved the Soviet Union, after which the United States became the sole superpower." Four errors in one sentence. ❌ The Wall's breach did not end the rivalry - the November 1990 declaration did. ❌ It did not dissolve the USSR - republican decisions in December 1991 did. ❌ It did not even reunify Germany - a treaty in September 1990 and a legal act in October 1990 did. ⚠️ And the last clause, which is the only defensible part, needs the qualification the whole session is built around.

⚠️ Now consider the alternative. "The rivalry was terminated by agreement in November 1990; the communist monopoly in the six non-Soviet Warsaw Pact states had already been broken during 1989; the union state dissolved in December 1991; and the order that was announced during the Gulf crisis of 1990 was tested almost immediately in Yugoslavia and found wanting." That is the same knowledge, sequenced, and it is the difference between a description and an argument.

⚠️ One precision inside that sentence has to be held. ✅ The window the account gives for eastern Europe as a whole is **August 1988 to December 1991**. ✅ What was completed during **1989** is narrower: the communist monopoly was broken in the six non-Soviet Warsaw Pact states - Poland, Hungary, East Germany, Czechoslovakia, Bulgaria and Romania - although several of their first free elections followed in 1990, in Hungary in March, Romania in May and Bulgaria in June. ✅ Albania, which had left the Pact and whose communism was not Soviet-installed, held multi-party elections only in the spring of 1991, and the Soviet Union itself dissolved in December 1991. ⚠️ Write "the six European satellites lost their communist monopoly during 1989" and the sentence is exact; write "the bloc collapsed in 1989" and it is not, because it silently annexes Albania and the union itself to a year in which neither fell.

### 3. What this topic owns, and where it stops

⚠️ Scope discipline matters more here than almost anywhere else in world history, because the material is adjacent to four neighbouring topics and the temptation to annex them is constant.

| Neighbouring topic | What it covers | What this session takes from it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ The Cold War topic | Origins, ideology, the security dilemma, containment, Berlin 1948 and 1961, Korea, Cuba, Vietnam, deterrence, non-alignment, detente and the long chronology to the late 1980s | ⚠️ Only the terminal condition: that by the mid-1980s there existed a rivalry, an alliance system and a satellite sphere whose maintenance had become expensive |
| ✅ The United Nations topic | Charter design, the six principal organs, Security Council composition and the veto, the peacekeeping record as an institutional question, reform logic | ⚠️ Only the legal category of particular post-1990 actions, used to prevent the blue-helmet error |
| ✅ The China topic | The Chinese revolution, Maoist consolidation, post-1978 reform without democratisation, the Sino-Soviet fracture | ⚠️ Only one comparison, and only because the account's own end-of-chapter question asks whether the USSR could have survived "in the same way that communism survived in China" |
| ✅ The world-economy topic | Bretton Woods, the North-South divide, oil, debt, globalisation as economic history, the 2008 crisis | ⚠️ Only globalisation's role as an **ideological component** of the order claim |
| ✅ International relations and the economy | India's 1991 reform package, strategic autonomy, present-day groupings and diplomacy | ⚠️ Only the changed systemic context that India had to respond to |

⚠️ The period boundary is equally firm. ✅ The core account read for this session was published in a fifth edition whose narrative runs to 2013, and nothing past that boundary may be presented as part of the historical narrative. ⚠️ Events after 2001 therefore appear in this session only where they serve as a **test** of the order claim - the disputed status of intervention, the enlargement rounds, the eurozone strain - and never as a continuing story. ⚠️ The war on terrorism, the Iraq war of 2003 and everything that follows are handed to the topics that cover them and to current international relations.

### 4. The organising question, stated once so that every later lesson can refer to it

⚠️ This topic has one organising question with two halves, and every lesson answers part of it.

```text
     WHY DID A SUPERPOWER SYSTEM DISSOLVE WITHOUT BEING DEFEATED,
     AND WHAT REPLACED IT?
                    |
        +-----------+-----------+
        |                       |
   HALF ONE (L2-L13)       HALF TWO (L14-L20)
   Structural weakness     Capability was
   + reform + mobilisation concentrated; rules,
   + the choice not to     legitimacy and consent
   coerce                  were not
        |                       |
        +-----------+-----------+
                    |
             L21: HOW TO SAY BOTH
             WITHOUT IMPLYING THAT
             EITHER HAD TO HAPPEN
```

### Comparative note: why "collapse" is a better word than "defeat", and what it still conceals

⚠️ **Defeat** implies an opponent who won on the field. No Soviet army was destroyed; no Soviet territory was occupied; no surrender was signed. ⚠️ **Collapse** is closer, because it points to a structure failing under its own weight. ⚠️ But collapse conceals something too: it suggests an absence of decisions, and the decisive moments in this story are all decisions. ✅ Honecker "wanted to order the army to open fire on the demonstrators, but other leading communists were not prepared to cause widespread bloodshed". ✅ Jaruzelski "decided not to risk using force". ✅ Gorbachev, "having only just succeeded in withdrawing from Afghanistan", "had no desire for an even greater involvement". ⚠️ Each is a choice, and each could have gone the other way - as the Romanian army's initial firing on crowds and the Chinese government's use of troops in June 1989 both demonstrate. ⚠️ The best single word is therefore neither defeat nor collapse but **dismantling**: a structure taken apart, partly by intention and partly by consequence, by people inside it.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The General Studies Paper I world-history clause covers the redrawing of national boundaries and political philosophies and their effect on society; this topic supplies the late-twentieth-century case of both. ✅ The Paper II clause on important international institutions, their structure and mandate becomes relevant here through the Security Council, NATO and the European Union.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Any question of the form "why did the Cold War end", "assess the new world order", "analyse the collapse of communism" or "discuss the redrawal of boundaries after 1991" opens on this lesson's distinctions, whether or not the stem mentions them.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the opening of the Berlin Wall as the end of the Cold War. ✅ The rivalry was terminated by the November 1990 declaration; the Wall's breach on 9 November 1989 ended enforced separation in one city and one country.
2. ⚠️ Treating the end of the USSR as a change of government in Moscow. ✅ A federation of separate republics dissolved; that is a different category of event.
3. ⚠️ Importing the whole Cold War into an answer about its ending. ⚠️ Name the terminal condition in one sentence and move on; the marks here are for the end phase.
4. ⚠️ Extending the answer into the war on terrorism. ⚠️ This session stops at the point where the order claim can be tested, rather than narrating forward.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four endings: rivalry (November 1990 declaration), bloc (August 1988 to end of 1991), state (December 1991), order (claimed from 1990, contested thereafter).
2. ✅ The account's own window for the eastern European transformation is **August 1988 to December 1991**.
3. ✅ Communist rule in Russia ended "after 74 years", counting from 1917.
4. ⚠️ Rivalries can end by agreement; spheres can end by sequential regime failure; states can end by internal dissolution. None of the three requires a military defeat.
5. ⚠️ "Dismantling" is a more accurate organising word than either "defeat" or "collapse", because the decisive moments were decisions taken by people inside the system.
6. ✅ Evidence that decisions mattered: Honecker overruled by colleagues unwilling to cause widespread bloodshed; Jaruzelski deciding not to risk force in 1988; Gorbachev unwilling to take on a larger commitment after Afghanistan.
7. ⚠️ Five boundaries: the long Cold War, United Nations design, China, the thematic world economy, and present-day Indian foreign and economic policy all belong elsewhere.
8. ⚠️ The period boundary of the core account is its 2013 publication horizon; post-2013 claims must be separately dated current items.
9. ⚠️ The organising question: why did a superpower system dissolve without being defeated, and what replaced it?

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Cold War ended in 1989 when the Berlin Wall fell and the Soviet Union collapsed." Identify every distinct error in that sentence and replace it with a defensible version of the same length.

**Model answer:** There are three errors. First, the rivalry did not end in 1989: the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were no longer adversaries in November 1990, and that is the act that terminated the confrontation between the alliance systems. Second, the Berlin Wall did not "fall" as a political settlement - it was breached on 9 November 1989, which ended enforced separation but settled nothing about Germany's borders, sovereignty or alliance status, both of which required the Two Plus Four treaty of 12 September 1990 and the legal reunification of 3 October 1990. Third, the Soviet Union did not collapse in 1989: it dissolved into separate republics in December 1991, and Gorbachev resigned on 25 December 1991, two years after the Wall. A defensible version of the same length reads: "The Cold War was ended by agreement in November 1990, after the communist monopoly in the six non-Soviet Warsaw Pact states had been broken during 1989, and the Soviet state itself dissolved in December 1991." The middle clause has to stay that precise: the wider eastern European window runs from August 1988 to December 1991, because Albania's multi-party elections came only in the spring of 1991 and the union dissolved at the end of that year.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that this is pedantry about dates. It is not: it is the difference between three causal processes. Merging them forces a candidate to explain everything by one mechanism - usually "people wanted freedom" - whereas separating them allows each to be explained by the mechanism that actually produced it: negotiation between alliances, sequential national regime failure, and republican exit from a federation.

```text
    "no room for local        -> "the world's biggest producer of
    individual initiative"       steel, fuel and energy" suffering
    state ownership and          shortages through waste and
    control; weighted to         inefficiency: an "absurd situation"
    defence and heavy
    industry; consumer
    goods "in short supply"

[2] TECHNOLOGICAL AND        -> the claim to be the future
    SOCIAL BACKWARDNESS      -> a BBC correspondent's judgement on
    living standards,            the bloc: the communist record on
    health, housing,             health, education, housing "and a
    environment                  range of other social services has
                                 been atrocious"

[3] ARMS AND MILITARY        -> resources available for renewal
    COMPETITION              -> Brezhnev "presided over a rapid
    SS-20s, a naval              increase in Soviet armed forces"
    build-up, Star Wars          while advocating disarmament

[4] IMPERIAL AND AFGHAN      -> prestige, and the appetite for
    BURDENS                      further commitment
    over 100,000 troops;     -> "a drain on their resources and a
    "impossible to subdue        blow to their prestige"
    the ferocious Islamic
    guerrillas"

[5] CONTACT WITH THE WEST    -> the attribution of blame to
    in the 1980s                 anything except the system itself
                             -> it "showed also that it must be
                                their own leaders and the communist
                                system that were the cause of all
                                their problems"
```

⚠️ Read the right-hand column as a single sentence and the lesson is done: the system lost the capacity to deliver, the claim to be the future, the resources to renew itself, the appetite for further commitment, and finally the ability to blame anyone else.

### 1. The learner's problem: stagnation is not the same as collapse

Every account of this period begins with economic failure, and every candidate therefore writes "the Soviet economy was weak" - which is true, and which explains almost nothing on its own. ⚠️ Armed states survive weak economies routinely; several were doing so at the same moment. The analytical question is not whether the economy was failing but **what specifically the failure removed**, and the answer is a form of legitimacy.

⚠️ A revolutionary state begins with revolutionary legitimacy: it is entitled to rule because it made the revolution. As the revolutionary generation dies, that entitlement has to be replaced, and the standard replacement is **performance**: we are entitled to rule because we deliver industrialisation, security, education, housing and rising living standards. ⚠️ Once performance visibly fails, the replacement legitimacy fails with it, and nothing is left except coercion and inertia. ⚠️ That is the precise thing economic stagnation removed, and stating it in those terms is what converts a description into a cause.

### 2. What "command economy" meant in practice, in the leadership's own words

✅ Gorbachev set out the diagnosis himself in a speech to the Party Conference in 1988, and the record preserves its four elements: the system was **too centralized**, "leaving no room for local individual initiative"; it was a **command economy**, "based almost completely on state ownership and control"; it was **weighted strongly towards defence and heavy industry**; and it therefore left **consumer goods for ordinary people in short supply**.

⚠️ Each element is a mechanism, not an adjective.

- ⚠️ **Over-centralisation** means that information about what was needed had to travel up a hierarchy and instructions had to travel back down. Every step lost accuracy, and no one at the bottom could act on what they could see.
- ⚠️ **State ownership and control** means that the entity deciding what to produce was also the entity judging whether the decision had been good, which removes the corrective that failure normally supplies.
- ⚠️ **The defence and heavy-industry weighting** means that the sector best able to attract resources was the one furthest from consumption, so growth in the measured economy did not translate into anything visible in a shop.
- ⚠️ **Shortage of consumer goods** is therefore not a separate problem but the output of the first three, and it is the one the population experiences directly.

✅ The account preserves Gorbachev's own summary of the resulting paradox: it was "an absurd situation" that the USSR, "the world's biggest producer of steel, fuel and energy, should be suffering shortages because of waste and inefficiency". ⚠️ That sentence is worth quoting in an answer precisely because it is the leadership's own admission: the failure is not attributed here by a hostile Western observer but conceded by the general secretary.

### 3. The comparative shock: what contact with the West did

✅ The account is specific about the information mechanism: "Increasing contact with the west in the 1980s showed people how backward the east was in comparison with the west, and suggested that their living standards were falling even further. It showed also that it must be their own leaders and the communist system that were the cause of all their problems."

⚠️ Note the structure of that sentence. It contains a comparison, a trend and an **attribution**. The comparison supplies the gap; the trend supplies the direction; and the attribution is the politically decisive step, because a population that attributes its hardship to war, to encirclement or to enemies is a governable population, while a population that attributes it to the system is not.

✅ On the bloc's social record the account quotes a named observer, Misha Glenny, a correspondent in eastern Europe: the party leaderships "refused to admit that the working class lived in more squalid conditions, breathing in more damaged air and drinking more toxic water, than western working classes ... the communist record on health, education, housing, and a range of other social services has been atrocious". ⚠️ The label matters: this is a journalist's judgement, quoted by the account, and should be attributed in an answer rather than asserted as an unowned fact.

### 4. The military and imperial burden, stated without exaggeration

✅ The Brezhnev record contains the contradiction in one line: he "continued to advocate disarmament but presided over a rapid increase in Soviet armed forces, particularly the navy and the new SS-20 missiles". ✅ Soviet aid to Cuba was stepped up and aid offered to Angola, Mozambique and Ethiopia. ⚠️ A state that simultaneously proposes disarmament and expands its forces is not being hypocritical so much as revealing that it cannot afford the competition it cannot abandon.

✅ On the American side the account records that by the later 1980s the intermediate-range treaty of 1987 required the scrapping of 436 American and 1,575 Soviet warheads, that there were strict verification provisions, that this "amounted, at most, to only 4 per cent of existing stocks", and that there remained "the stumbling block of Reagan's Star Wars, which he was still not prepared to give up, even though it was only at the planning stage". ⚠️ The analytical point is not that the strategic-defence programme worked - it was at the planning stage - but that the prospect of a further technological round was being weighed by a leadership that already knew it could not fund the current one.

✅ Afghanistan is stated plainly: "By 1985 the USSR was seriously embarrassed by its involvement in Afghanistan. Although there were over 100 000 Soviet troops in the country, they found it impossible to subdue the ferocious Islamic guerrillas; it was a drain on their resources and a blow to their prestige." ✅ Three external pressures are named as convincing Gorbachev to withdraw: "the hostility of China, the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world and repeated condemnations by the UN". ✅ The account records the terms as a **timetable**: that the Russians "would begin withdrawing their troops from Afghanistan on 1 May 1988, provided the Americans stopped sending military aid to the Afghan resistance movement". ⚠️ Two dates are firmer than that timetable and should be the ones written: the settlement was signed at **Geneva on 14 April 1988**, and the withdrawal actually began on **15 May 1988**, finishing on 15 February 1989. ❌ Do not assert that the columns moved on 1 May; that is the date the agreement set, not the date the movement started.

⚠️ For this topic the Afghan war matters less as a campaign than as a **precedent**: a leadership that has just extracted itself, with difficulty, from one costly intervention has a strong and stated disinclination to begin a larger one. ✅ The account makes that link explicitly when it turns to eastern Europe: "Having only just succeeded in withdrawing from Afghanistan, Gorbachev had no desire for an even greater involvement."

### 5. The inheritance: what the three preceding leaderships had and had not done

⚠️ It is tempting to treat 1985 as year zero. It was not, and the two short leaderships before Gorbachev show that the diagnosis was already internal party knowledge.

| Leadership | ✅ What the record states | ⚠️ What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Brezhnev, to 1982 | Dissidents confined in psychiatric hospitals; Helsinki monitoring groups arrested, imprisoned, exiled or dissolved; Sakharov exiled to Gorky and later Siberia; forces expanded while disarmament was advocated | ⚠️ Repression was still working, which is why stagnation had not yet become crisis |
| ✅ Andropov, November 1982 to February 1984 | Head of the security service until May 1982; "immediately launched a vigorous campaign to modernize and streamline the Soviet system"; an anti-corruption drive; economic reform through decentralization; younger officials promoted | ⚠️ The reform agenda predates Gorbachev; what Andropov lacked was time, since he "was dogged by ill health and died after little more than a year in office" |
| ✅ Chernenko, February 1984 to March 1985 | Aged 72, conventional, Brezhnev's former personal assistant, "already terminally ill when he was chosen"; no relaxation for human-rights activists; unofficial trade unionists and religious groups arrested | ⚠️ The account preserves a named historian's verdict: Dmitri Volkogonov wrote that Chernenko's rise "symbolized the deepening of the crisis in society, the total lack of positive ideas in the party, and the inevitability of the convulsions to come" |

⚠️ The selection of Chernenko is itself evidence. ✅ The record notes that "clearly the majority wanted somebody who would abandon the anti-corruption campaign and leave them in peace". ⚠️ A ruling elite that chooses a terminally ill caretaker in order to avoid being reformed has told you what its priority is, and it is not renewal.

### Criticism and reply: is this just "the economy failed, so the system fell"?

⚠️ **The objection.** Listing pressures is not explanation. Every large state has structural problems. Naming five of them and pointing at 1991 is post hoc reasoning: the outcome is used to select the causes.

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** First, this lesson does not claim that the pressures caused the collapse; it claims that they removed specific capacities, and each removal is separately evidenced by the leadership's own statements rather than inferred backwards from the outcome. Second, the counter-case is available inside the same period and is used deliberately: ✅ China faced comparable economic problems and a mass demonstration in the same year, ✅ used troops to disperse it in June 1989, and ✅ its communist system survived - which proves that these pressures do not entail this outcome. Third, the account itself frames its own end-of-chapter question in exactly these terms, asking whether the USSR could have survived "in the same way that communism survived in China", which is a question that only makes sense if the outcome was not determined by the pressures alone.

⚠️ **The residual.** What the pressures do explain is **urgency**. They explain why a reforming leadership arrived in 1985 rather than 1975, why it believed it had little time, and why it attempted simultaneous political and economic change rather than the sequenced economic-first change attempted elsewhere. ⚠️ That is a real explanatory contribution, and it is also the limit of what this lesson can claim.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The political-philosophies clause: this is the evidence base for any assessment of the effect of a centrally planned system on the society that lived under it.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Analyse the causes of the collapse of communism in the USSR and eastern Europe" always opens here, and the opening paragraph should convert stagnation into **loss of performance legitimacy** within two sentences.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the Soviet economy "collapsed" in the 1980s. ✅ It stagnated and then contracted under reform: growth in 1988 and 1989 "stayed exactly the same as it had been in previous years", national income fell in 1990 and fell by about 15 per cent in 1991. Collapse is a later and partly reform-induced phenomenon, taught in Lesson 6.
2. ⚠️ Treating the arms race as the decisive cause. ⚠️ It narrowed the resources available for renewal; it does not explain the timing or the peaceful character of the withdrawal.
3. ⚠️ Presenting Afghanistan as a military defeat that broke the USSR. ✅ The record's words are "a drain on their resources and a blow to their prestige", and the withdrawal was negotiated with a reciprocal American condition.
4. ⚠️ Quoting the social-record judgement as an unowned fact. ✅ Attribute it to Misha Glenny, whom the account names.
5. ⚠️ Beginning the story in 1985. ✅ Andropov's modernisation drive and Volkogonov's verdict on Chernenko show the diagnosis was already internal.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Gorbachev's 1988 diagnosis has four parts: over-centralisation with no room for local initiative; a command economy based almost completely on state ownership and control; a strong weighting towards defence and heavy industry; consumer goods in short supply.
2. ✅ His own phrase for the paradox: "an absurd situation" that the world's biggest producer of steel, fuel and energy suffered shortages through waste and inefficiency.
3. ✅ Contact with the West in the 1980s supplied a comparison, a trend **and** an attribution - that the leaders and the system were the cause.
4. ✅ Misha Glenny, quoted by the account, judged the bloc's record on health, education, housing and other social services "atrocious"; attribute it.
5. ✅ Brezhnev advocated disarmament while expanding the forces, particularly the navy and the SS-20 missiles, and stepped up aid to Cuba, Angola, Mozambique and Ethiopia.
6. ✅ The 1987 intermediate-range treaty scrapped 436 American and 1,575 Soviet warheads with strict verification, amounted to at most 4 per cent of stocks, and left the planning-stage strategic-defence programme unresolved.
7. ✅ Afghanistan: over 100,000 Soviet troops; guerrillas impossible to subdue; Chinese hostility, Islamic suspicion and repeated United Nations condemnation; settlement signed at **Geneva on 14 April 1988** on a reciprocal condition, withdrawal begun on **15 May 1988** and completed on 15 February 1989. ⚠️ The account's 1 May 1988 is the agreed timetable, not the start.
8. ✅ Andropov (November 1982 - February 1984) had already begun modernisation, anti-corruption and decentralisation before ill health ended it.
9. ✅ Volkogonov on Chernenko: his rise symbolised "the deepening of the crisis in society, the total lack of positive ideas in the party, and the inevitability of the convulsions to come".
10. ⚠️ The analytical conversion to memorise: stagnation removed **performance legitimacy**, which had replaced revolutionary legitimacy. That is the cause; "the economy was weak" is the description.
11. ⚠️ The disciplining counter-case is China in 1989: comparable pressures, a different decision, a different outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it stronger to write that economic stagnation removed the regime's performance legitimacy than to write that the Soviet economy was failing, and what single piece of evidence in this lesson makes the stronger version checkable?

**Model answer:** Because "the economy was failing" is a description that has no political consequence attached to it, and many states survive it. The stronger version names the mechanism: a revolutionary state that has outlived its revolutionary generation rules on the claim that it delivers - industrialisation, security, welfare, rising living standards - and once delivery visibly fails, that claim fails with it, leaving only coercion and inertia. The single checkable piece of evidence is Gorbachev's own admission, recorded in the account, that it was "an absurd situation" that the USSR, the world's biggest producer of steel, fuel and energy, should be suffering shortages because of waste and inefficiency. That sentence is decisive because it is not an external accusation but the leadership's own concession that output and welfare had become disconnected, which is precisely the disconnection that destroys performance legitimacy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the economic pressures made the collapse inevitable. The same lesson supplies the refutation: China faced comparable problems, met a mass demonstration in June 1989 with troops, and its communist system survived. The pressures explain urgency and vulnerability; they do not explain the timing, the peaceful character or the outcome, and an answer that lets them do so has smuggled in a determinism the evidence does not support.

---

## Lesson 3 - Legitimacy, nationalities and federal design: why this particular state was exposed

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the fifteen union republics and their parliaments; tight control since Stalin's time; Nagorno-Karabakh and the Armenia-Azerbaijan conflict; the Baltic republics and the 1940 annexation; why federal units become state-building platforms; and the ideological binding that nationality claims replace

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's numbered treatment of nationalist pressures as the third thing that went wrong with Gorbachev's policies, which sets out the federal structure of fifteen republics, the Nagorno-Karabakh dispute of February 1988 and the Baltic independence declarations from March 1990
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a federal design, and the exit it made available

```text
  THE DESIGN                                THE CONSEQUENCE
  ==========                                ===============

  "The Soviet Union was a federal           Each republic already possessed:
   state consisting of 15 separate            - a territory with a border
   republics, each with its own               - a titular nationality
   parliament."                               - a parliament
                                              - an administrative apparatus
  The Russian republic "was just one            - a capital city
   of the 15, with its parliament
   in Moscow" - which was also where        i.e. every institutional
   the federal Supreme Soviet and           component of a sovereign state
   Congress of People's Deputies met        EXCEPT permission

                    |
                    v
  WHAT HELD IT TOGETHER              WHAT HAPPENED WHEN THAT WEAKENED
  -----------------------            --------------------------------
  [a] the Party's overall control    -> glasnost and perestroika
      ("kept under tight control        "encouraged them to hope for more
      since Stalin's time")              powers for their parliaments and
  [b] an official ideology that         more independence from Moscow"
      claimed to transcend           -> Nagorno-Karabakh asks to transfer
      nationality                       to Armenia, Feb 1988: refused;
  [c] the credible threat of            fighting follows; "Moscow had
      force                             clearly lost control"
                                     -> Lithuania declares independence,
                                        March 1990; Latvia and Estonia
                                        follow "more gradually"
                                     -> Moscow refuses recognition, and
                                        the refusal does not stop it
```

⚠️ The left-hand column is a constitutional fact; the right-hand column is a political consequence. The lesson is the arrow between them.

### 1. The learner's problem: why did nationalism appear so suddenly?

The standard impression is that suppressed national feelings burst out once repression lifted, like water through a cracked dam. ⚠️ That image is half right and badly misleading. It is right that control had been tight: ✅ "the republics had been kept under tight control since Stalin's time". It is misleading because it suggests that nationalism was a pre-existing quantity waiting to be released, when what the record actually shows is **institutions being repurposed**.

⚠️ Consider what a union republic was. It had a defined territory with an international-looking border, a titular nationality after which it was named, its own parliament, its own administrative machinery and its own capital. ⚠️ In other words it already had every component of a sovereign state except the permission to behave like one. ⚠️ When the permission weakened, nothing had to be invented. The state was already built; it only had to be claimed.

⚠️ That is why the sequence in the record is not "feeling, then demand" but "opening, then demand, then escalation": ✅ "glasnost and perestroika encouraged them to hope for more powers for their parliaments and more independence from Moscow", and ✅ "Gorbachev himself seemed sympathetic, provided that the Communist Party of the Soviet Union (CPSU) remained in overall control. However, once started, demands got out of hand."

### 2. What the ideology had been doing, and what its weakening exposed

⚠️ A multinational state needs something that makes the shared state more important than the several nations. Empires use dynastic loyalty; liberal states use citizenship; the Soviet Union used an **official ideology** that claimed to be more fundamental than nationality - class before nation, and a future in which national difference would matter less.

⚠️ Two things follow, and both are examinable.

- ⚠️ First, when the ideology loses credibility, the state loses its only universal loyalty and nationality becomes the **best available alternative** - not because it is primordial but because it is the loyalty already institutionalised in the republic that the citizen lives in.
- ⚠️ Second, nationalism is therefore better read as a **consequence** of weakening central authority as much as a cause of it. ⚠️ The control to hold is exactly that: nationalism strengthened because central authority and ideology were already weakening, and treating it as an independent first cause reverses the sequence.

⚠️ The same mechanism is already familiar from the multinational states that broke up after the First World War, and it recurs in Yugoslavia in Lesson 16. ⚠️ Stating it once, generally, and then applying it twice is a far better use of an answer than describing two break-ups separately.

### 3. Nagorno-Karabakh: the first refusal, and why it was refused

✅ "Trouble began in Nagorno-Karabakh, a small Christian autonomous republic within the Soviet republic of Azerbaijan, which was Muslim. The parliament of Nagorno-Karabakh asked to become part of neighbouring Christian Armenia (February 1988), but Gorbachev refused."

⚠️ The reason for the refusal is the analytically interesting part, and the account gives it: "He was afraid that if he agreed, this would upset the conservatives (who opposed internal frontier changes) and turn them against his entire reform programme."

⚠️ Read that as a trap with two jaws. Conceding the transfer would have cost him the conservative wing whose acquiescence his reform programme required. Refusing it demonstrated that the new openness had a hard limit on exactly the question that mattered most to the republics. ✅ The outcome is recorded flatly: "Fighting broke out between Azerbaijan and Armenia, and Moscow had clearly lost control."

⚠️ The general principle, worth carrying into any answer about reforming authoritarian systems, is that a reforming centre cannot be selectively generous. Each concession is read as a precedent and each refusal as a revelation, and the two readings together erode authority faster than either would alone.

### 4. The Baltic republics: a different claim, and why it was harder to answer

✅ "Worse was to follow in the three Baltic soviet republics of Lithuania, Latvia and Estonia, which had been taken over against their will by the Russians in 1940. Independence movements, denounced by Gorbachev as 'national excesses', had been growing in strength. In March 1990, encouraged by what was happening in the satellite states of eastern Europe, Lithuania took the lead by declaring itself independent. The other two soon followed, though they voted to proceed more gradually. Moscow refused to recognize their independence."

⚠️ Four distinct things are doing work in that passage.

1. ⚠️ **The nature of the claim.** The Baltic case was not a demand for more autonomy but a claim that the 1940 incorporation had been illegitimate. ⚠️ A claim of that form cannot be answered by devolution, because it denies that there was ever a lawful union to devolve from.
2. ⚠️ **The demonstration effect.** The account states it explicitly: they were "encouraged by what was happening in the satellite states of eastern Europe". ⚠️ This is one of the two great transmission channels in the whole topic - eastern European success teaching Soviet republics what was possible - and it is the reason this session teaches Eastern Europe before the Soviet dissolution rather than after.
3. ⚠️ **The graduated response.** Lithuania led; the other two "voted to proceed more gradually". ⚠️ Even within a single region, timing and tactics differed, which is a useful corrective to writing "the Baltics" as one actor.
4. ⚠️ **The refusal that changed nothing.** ✅ Moscow refused recognition. ⚠️ A refusal that does not alter the behaviour it refuses is a public demonstration that the centre's writ has narrowed, and every other republic watched it.

### 5. Where the third source of authority came from

✅ The third element of the nationalist section in the record is not a nationality at all: "Boris Yeltsin, who had been excluded from the new Supreme Soviet by the conservatives, made a dramatic comeback when he was elected president of the parliament of the Russian republic (Russian Federation) in May 1990."

⚠️ Placing Yeltsin inside the nationalities problem rather than outside it is the account's own arrangement, and it is correct. ⚠️ The Russian republic was the largest unit of the union and the one in which the union's own capital sat. ⚠️ Once it acquired a separately elected leadership with its own popular mandate, the union contained two authorities in the same city claiming different sources of legitimacy - one from the Party and the union structure, one from an election in the largest republic. ⚠️ That is the structural condition usually called **dual power**, and Lesson 11 shows what it did in August 1991.

### Doubt and resolution: "wasn't the USSR always going to break up along national lines?"

⚠️ This is the most common live doubt on this lesson, and it deserves a direct answer rather than a dismissal.

⚠️ **What supports it.** The federal design did supply ready-made units; the Baltic incorporation of 1940 was recent and contested; the account records that national feeling had been suppressed rather than dissolved.

⚠️ **What refutes it.** Three things. First, the same design had held for decades under conditions of tight control, which means the design alone is not sufficient. Second, the break did not follow national lines cleanly: ✅ fighting broke out **between** two union republics over an autonomous region inside one of them, and ✅ further conflicts followed in Georgia "where the people of the north wanted to form a separate state" - that is, national claims nested inside national claims, not a tidy fracture along pre-drawn seams. Third, the outcomes diverged sharply: some successor questions were settled peacefully and others by war, which is inconsistent with a single inevitable logic.

⚠️ **The resolution to write.** The design made national exit **available**; the failure of ideology and performance made it **attractive**; the decision not to coerce made it **achievable**. ⚠️ All three are required, and an answer that names only the first has described a constitution rather than explained a dissolution.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The redrawal of national boundaries clause: the fifteen successor states of 1991 are, with the Yugoslav successors and German reunification, the syllabus's principal late-twentieth-century case.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Why did the Soviet Union disintegrate?" and "Examine the role of nationality politics in the collapse of the USSR" both sit here; so does any comparative question on multinational states.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating nationalism as the primary cause. ⚠️ Preserve the canonical control: it was a consequence of weakening central authority as much as a cause of it.
2. ⚠️ Writing "ethnic tensions exploded" without an institution attached. ✅ The account's first case is a **parliament** making a formal request, not a crowd.
3. ⚠️ Saying the USSR simply became Russia. ✅ Fifteen union republics existed; the Russian republic was one of them, and it acquired its own separately elected leadership in May 1990.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the demonstration effect. ✅ The Baltic declarations were "encouraged by what was happening in the satellite states of eastern Europe".
5. ⚠️ Treating the Baltic claim as a demand for autonomy. ⚠️ It rested on the illegitimacy of the 1940 takeover, which is a different kind of claim and admits a different kind of answer.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Fifteen union republics, each with its own parliament; the Russian republic was one of the fifteen and its parliament sat in Moscow alongside the federal bodies.
2. ✅ Republics had been "kept under tight control since Stalin's time"; glasnost and perestroika encouraged hopes of more powers and more independence.
3. ✅ Gorbachev was sympathetic provided the CPSU remained in overall control; "once started, demands got out of hand".
4. ✅ Nagorno-Karabakh: a Christian autonomous republic inside Muslim Azerbaijan; its parliament asked to join Armenia in February 1988; Gorbachev refused for fear of losing the conservatives; fighting followed and "Moscow had clearly lost control".
5. ✅ Baltic republics: taken over against their will in 1940; Gorbachev denounced the movements as "national excesses"; Lithuania declared independence in March 1990; Latvia and Estonia followed more gradually; Moscow refused recognition.
6. ✅ The Baltic declarations were encouraged by events in the eastern European satellites - the key transmission channel of the whole topic.
7. ✅ Yeltsin was elected president of the parliament of the Russian republic in May 1990, after conservatives had excluded him from the new Supreme Soviet.
8. ⚠️ Mechanism to memorise: federal design made exit **available**; ideological and performance failure made it **attractive**; non-coercion made it **achievable**.
9. ⚠️ A reforming centre cannot concede selectively: concessions become precedents and refusals become revelations.
10. ⚠️ National claims nested inside national claims - Nagorno-Karabakh inside Azerbaijan, northern Georgia inside Georgia - which is why "a tidy fracture along national lines" is not an accurate description.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Nagorno-Karabakh request of February 1988 damaged Gorbachev's authority whichever way he answered it, and state the general principle the case illustrates.

**Model answer:** The parliament of Nagorno-Karabakh, a Christian autonomous republic inside Muslim Azerbaijan, asked to be transferred to Armenia. Agreeing would have alienated the conservatives, who opposed internal frontier changes, and the account records that this was precisely Gorbachev's fear - that they would turn against his entire reform programme, whose survival depended on their acquiescence. Refusing, which is what he did, demonstrated publicly that the new openness stopped at the one question the republics cared about most, and the result was that fighting broke out between Azerbaijan and Armenia and, in the account's words, Moscow had clearly lost control. The general principle is that a reforming authoritarian centre cannot concede selectively: every concession is read elsewhere as a precedent and every refusal is read as a revelation of the reform's limits, so the two readings together erode authority faster than either would on its own.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the case as evidence that ancient religious or ethnic hatred was resurfacing. The first actor in the record is a parliament making a formal constitutional request, and the violence follows the political refusal rather than preceding it. Treating the sequence in the other order produces the same error that Lesson 16 shows to be fatal in Yugoslavia, and it removes from the answer the only actors whose decisions can actually be assessed.

## Lesson 4 - Gorbachev I: glasnost and perestroika as a rescue project
