### Visual: two instruments, two speeds, one gap

```text
                        MARCH 1985
              Gorbachev, aged 54, comes to power
        "determined to transform and revitalize the country"
                             |
          +------------------+------------------+
          |                                     |
    GLASNOST (openness)               PERESTROIKA (restructuring
    Instrument: permission            of the Party, the economy
    Speed: IMMEDIATE                   and the government)
                                      Instrument: institutional
    Dec 1986 Sakharovs return          redesign
    from Gorky                        Speed: SLOW, and reversible
    Bukharin and others declared       in its effects
    innocent of all crimes
    Pravda prints criticism of        1988-89 growth "stayed
    Brezhnev's treatment of            exactly the same"
    dissidents                        1990 national income FALLS
    Jan 1988 law against sending      1991 falls again, by about
    dissidents to mental                15 per cent
    institutions                      Basic goods - soap, washing
    May 1986 film-makers' and          powder, razor blades, cups,
    writers' unions elect new          saucers, TV sets, food -
    leaderships                        "in very short supply"
    Banned anti-Stalin films and      Queues in the towns get
    novels released; Mandelstam        longer
    prepared for publication
    1988 Party Conference and
    May 1989 Congress TELEVISED
          |                                     |
          +------------------+------------------+
                             |
                    THE GAP IS THE STORY
      Criticism became legal, organised and visible years
      before restructuring produced anything a citizen could
      buy. Expectations were raised, then disappointed, and
      the disappointment now had a legal vocabulary and a
      televised stage.
```

### 1. Simple intuition before the terminology

Imagine a manager who inherits a failing factory. She does two things on the same day. She announces that any worker may now say publicly what is wrong, and she begins a redesign of the production line. The first change takes effect that afternoon. The second will take three years, if it works.

⚠️ For those three years she will be criticised daily, in public, by people using the permission she granted, about a factory that has not yet improved. ⚠️ Her critics are not ungrateful; they are behaving exactly as her first reform invited them to. But her authority is being consumed at the speed of the first reform while her results arrive at the speed of the second - if they arrive.

⚠️ That asymmetry is the whole of Gorbachev's domestic tragedy, and it is why this lesson teaches the two instruments as **two different speeds** rather than as two policies with two translations.

### 2. What he intended, stated in the record's own words

✅ "Mikhail Gorbachev, who came to power in March 1985, was, at 54, the most gifted and dynamic leader Russia had seen for many years. He was determined to transform and revitalize the country after the sterile years following Khrushchev's fall. He intended to achieve this by modernizing and streamlining the Communist Party with new policies of glasnost (openness) and perestroika (restructuring - of the Party, the economy and the government)."

✅ And the decisive sentence, which controls every answer on this topic: "Gorbachev did not want to end communism; he wanted to replace the existing system, which was still basically Stalinist, with a socialist system which was humane and democratic. He sincerely believed that this could be achieved within the framework of the Marxist-Leninist one-party state."

✅ The account's own summary of the outcome follows immediately: "He did not have the same success at home as abroad. His policies failed to provide results quickly enough, and led to the collapse of communism, the break-up of the USSR, and the end of his own political career."

⚠️ Three things to extract. ⚠️ First, the object of reform was the **Party**, not the state's replacement - he intended to modernise and streamline the instrument he was using. ⚠️ Second, the target system was **socialist**, humane and democratic, inside a one-party framework; whether those terms are compatible is a genuine question, and it is the question the outcome answers in the negative. ⚠️ Third, the account states the asymmetry between his foreign and domestic records in its own voice, and that asymmetry is the bridge to Lesson 5.

### 3. Glasnost: what "openness" actually consisted of

⚠️ Glasnost is often reduced to "freedom of speech", which is both too strong and too vague. What the record shows is a series of specific permissions and reversals, each of which can be dated and named.

| Domain | ✅ What was done | ⚠️ Why it mattered politically |
|---|---|---|
| Human rights | ✅ Several well-known dissidents released; the Sakharovs allowed to return to Moscow from internal exile in Gorky in **December 1986** | ⚠️ The most internationally visible prisoner of conscience was restored to the capital, which signalled to every domestic critic that the rules had changed |
| Historical rehabilitation | ✅ Leaders such as **Bukharin**, disgraced and executed in the purges of the 1930s, "were declared innocent of all crimes" | ⚠️ Rehabilitation is not merely historical: it concedes that the Party had committed judicial murder, which transfers the burden of proof in every later argument about the Party's record |
| Press | ✅ *Pravda* "was allowed to print an article criticizing Brezhnev for overreacting against dissidents" | ⚠️ Criticism of a predecessor in the Party's own newspaper legitimises criticism as a genre |
| Legal protection | ✅ A new law **January 1988** "to prevent dissidents from being sent to mental institutions" | ⚠️ This closes, by statute, the specific repressive technique the account records the security service as having used since around 1970 |
| Televised politics | ✅ The **Nineteenth Party Conference in 1988** and the first session of the new **Congress of People's Deputies in May 1989** were televised | ⚠️ This is the single most underrated item on the list: it converted internal Party argument into a mass spectator event and created a national audience for dissent |
| Cultural institutions | ✅ **May 1986**: both the Union of Soviet Film-makers and the Union of Writers "were allowed to sack their reactionary heads and elect more independent-minded leaders" | ⚠️ Elective turnover inside official bodies demonstrated that incumbency was no longer protected |
| Publication | ✅ Long-banned anti-Stalin films and novels shown and published; preparations made to publish the poet **Osip Mandelstam**, who died in a labour camp in 1938 | ⚠️ The regime's own victims became publicly readable, which is a permanent change in the available vocabulary of criticism |
| News reporting | ✅ **April 1986**: when the nuclear reactor at **Chernobyl** in Ukraine exploded, "killing hundreds of people and releasing a massive radioactive cloud which drifted across most of Europe, the disaster was discussed" | ⚠️ Attribute the casualty wording exactly as the account gives it; do not substitute any other figure |

⚠️ Note that every item is an act of **permission**, and permission is the fastest instrument a government possesses. It costs nothing to grant and takes effect the moment it is announced.

### 4. Perestroika: why restructuring is intrinsically slow

⚠️ Restructuring the Party, the economy and the government means changing who decides what, on what information, with what incentives. ⚠️ Every one of those changes has to pass through the institutions being changed, which are staffed by the people whose positions depend on the arrangement being replaced.

✅ The measured results are recorded without softening: growth in 1988 and 1989 "stayed exactly the same as it had been in previous years"; ✅ "In 1990 national income actually fell, and continued to fall - by about 15 per cent - in 1991"; ✅ and the account notes that "some economists think that the USSR was going through an economic crisis as serious as the one in the USA in the early 1930s". ⚠️ That last clause is an attributed comparison and must be written as one.

⚠️ Lesson 6 takes the specific mechanism of the failure - the Law on State Enterprises - in detail, because it is the clearest single demonstration in the whole topic that a partial reform can be worse than either the old system or a complete change.

### 5. The gap, stated as a formula worth memorising

⚠️ Put the two columns of the visual together and the formula is this:

```text
  SPEED OF PERMISSION  >>  SPEED OF DELIVERY
            |
            v
  EXPECTATIONS RISE FAST, RESULTS ARRIVE SLOWLY OR NOT AT ALL
            |
            v
  DISAPPOINTMENT NOW HAS A LEGAL VOCABULARY, ORGANISED BODIES
  AND A TELEVISED STAGE
            |
            v
  CRITICISM TURNS FROM "FIX THIS" TO "WHO GAVE YOU THE RIGHT
  TO DECIDE?"
```

✅ The account's own version of the last step is exact: glasnost - "encouraging people to voice their criticisms - was now in full flow, and was beginning to turn against the Communist Party". ⚠️ And on the general mechanism it offers a sentence that transfers to every comparable case: "The most dangerous time for any repressive regime is when it begins to try and reform itself by making concessions. These are never enough to satisfy the critics."

⚠️ The same mechanism appears in the South African transition, and the comparison is worth carrying: an authoritarian regime that starts reforming cannot stop halfway.

### Criticism and reply: should he have done perestroika first and glasnost later?

⚠️ **The objection.** This is the standard counterfactual, and it usually cites China: economic restructuring first, political opening deferred indefinitely, communist rule preserved. ✅ The account's own end-of-chapter question asks whether the USSR could have survived "in the same way that communism survived in China", so the comparison is invited rather than imported.

⚠️ **The reply, and it has two parts.** First, the instruments were not independent. ⚠️ Gorbachev's diagnosis was that the system was too centralized, "leaving no room for local individual initiative"; the cure for that diagnosis requires people lower down to act on information they possess and superiors do not, which requires that they be permitted to say what they know. ⚠️ Openness was not decoration attached to restructuring; on his own analysis it was a precondition of it. Second, the Chinese comparison carries a cost that must be stated: ✅ in June 1989 the Chinese government "used troops to disperse a student demonstration in Tiananmen Square", the account records that at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed, and this brought worldwide condemnation. ⚠️ The Chinese route was available to Gorbachev too; the difference is a decision about force, not a technical sequencing choice.

⚠️ **The residual.** The account also preserves the opposite charge in its own end-of-chapter questions: that "it was Gorbachev's reluctance to commit himself to sufficiently radical changes that led to the break-up of the Soviet Union". ⚠️ The two criticisms - too fast politically, too slow economically - are both defensible on the evidence, and the honest answer says so rather than choosing. ⚠️ That combination is itself the strongest available verdict: he was reforming a system whose components could not be changed at compatible speeds.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Political philosophies and their effect on society: this is the clearest available case of a socialist system attempting self-reform from within.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Gorbachev tried to reform communism but ended by dissolving it" invites comment; so does the account's own "if Gorbachev had followed different policies, the USSR could have survived, in the same way that communism survived in China".

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying Gorbachev intended to end communism. ✅ The record says the opposite in terms, and the error destroys the answer's central irony.
2. ⚠️ Treating glasnost and perestroika as synonyms or as one policy with two names. ✅ Openness and restructuring were different instruments with different speeds.
3. ⚠️ Translating glasnost as "democracy". ⚠️ It was permission to speak, granted inside a one-party framework whose monopoly was not initially in question.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the televising of the 1988 Conference and the May 1989 Congress. ⚠️ It is the mechanism that gave criticism a national audience.
5. ⚠️ Giving casualty figures for Chernobyl other than the account's own wording. ✅ Use "killing hundreds of people" and attribute it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ March 1985: Gorbachev, aged 54, comes to power, determined to transform and revitalise after "the sterile years following Khrushchev's fall".
2. ✅ Glasnost = openness; perestroika = restructuring of the Party, the economy and the government.
3. ✅ He "did not want to end communism"; he wanted a humane and democratic socialist system inside the Marxist-Leninist one-party framework.
4. ✅ The account's verdict: less success at home than abroad; results too slow; the outcome was the collapse of communism, the break-up of the USSR and the end of his career.
5. ✅ December 1986: the Sakharovs return from Gorky. ✅ Bukharin and others declared innocent. ✅ January 1988: law against sending dissidents to mental institutions.
6. ✅ May 1986: film-makers' and writers' unions elect new leaderships. ✅ Banned anti-Stalin films and novels released; Mandelstam, who died in a labour camp in 1938, prepared for publication.
7. ✅ Televised: the Nineteenth Party Conference in 1988 and the first session of the Congress of People's Deputies in May 1989.
8. ✅ April 1986: Chernobyl exploded, "killing hundreds of people" and releasing a cloud across most of Europe; the disaster was discussed openly.
9. ✅ Growth unchanged in 1988-89; national income fell in 1990 and by about 15 per cent in 1991; an attributed comparison with the American crisis of the early 1930s.
10. ⚠️ The formula: speed of permission exceeds speed of delivery, so expectation outruns result and criticism acquires legality, organisation and an audience.
11. ✅ The transferable sentence: "The most dangerous time for any repressive regime is when it begins to try and reform itself by making concessions. These are never enough to satisfy the critics."
12. ⚠️ The Chinese counterfactual is invited by the account's own question, and its cost is the June 1989 decision to use troops.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it more accurate to describe glasnost and perestroika as instruments of different speeds than as two halves of a single liberalising programme, and what political consequence follows from the difference?

**Model answer:** Because they operated through different mechanisms. Glasnost worked by permission - releasing dissidents, returning the Sakharovs from Gorky in December 1986, declaring Bukharin innocent, legislating in January 1988 against confinement in mental institutions, allowing cultural unions to elect new leaderships in May 1986, and televising the 1988 Party Conference and the May 1989 Congress. Permission costs nothing to grant and takes effect immediately. Perestroika worked by institutional redesign of the Party, the economy and the government, which has to pass through the very institutions being redesigned and can only show results over years; the record shows growth unchanged in 1988 and 1989 and national income falling in 1990 and by about 15 per cent in 1991. The consequence is that expectations rose at the speed of the first instrument while results arrived at the speed of the second, so that disappointment accumulated in a society that now possessed a legal vocabulary of criticism, elected cultural bodies and a televised national stage - and the account records the result exactly: glasnost "was now in full flow, and was beginning to turn against the Communist Party".

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the sequencing was simply a mistake that a cleverer leader would have avoided by copying China. Gorbachev's own diagnosis was that the system was too centralised and left no room for local initiative, and curing that requires people lower down to be able to say what they know, so openness was a precondition of restructuring rather than an optional extra. The genuine difference from China is not sequencing but the decision about force, and the account records what that decision looked like when it was taken the other way: troops used in Tiananmen Square in June 1989, with at least a thousand students killed.

## Lesson 5 - Gorbachev II: new thinking abroad, and the decisive omission

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the impact of new thinking on foreign affairs; the intermediate-range treaty of 1987 and its verification provisions; the Geneva settlement of April 1988 and the Afghan withdrawal from May 1988; reconciliation with China in May 1989; the engineered replacement of hardline satellite leaders; the renunciation of intervention and its standard designation; the Malta meeting of December 1989; and why the satellites' loss of the expectation of rescue is the topic's single most important political fact

### 1. The learner's problem: why did foreign policy succeed where domestic policy failed?

✅ The account states the asymmetry as a fact: "He did not have the same success at home as abroad." ⚠️ The reason is structural and worth naming, because it also explains why the successes abroad accelerated the failure at home.

⚠️ Foreign policy in a centralised state is the domain in which a leader's decisions translate most directly into outcomes. ⚠️ Signing a treaty, ordering a withdrawal, visiting Beijing or declining to intervene are acts a general secretary can simply perform. ⚠️ Raising living standards is not: it requires millions of decisions by people whose incentives he can only alter indirectly. ⚠️ So the same leader appeared decisive abroad and impotent at home - and the appearance was accurate.

⚠️ The irony that follows is the heart of this lesson. ⚠️ Each foreign success removed a support from the domestic structure. Ending the arms competition removed the external threat that had justified the defence weighting and the discipline. Ending the Afghan commitment removed a demonstration of will. Declining to intervene in eastern Europe removed the guarantee on which the entire bloc rested. ⚠️ He was, in the most literal sense, dismantling the external scaffolding of the system he was trying to repair from inside.

### 2. Arms: what the 1987 treaty did and did not do

✅ The terms recorded: "All land-based intermediate-range (300 to 3000 miles) nuclear weapons were to be scrapped over the next three years. This meant 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads, and would include all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia." ✅ "There were strict verification provisions so that both sides could check that the weapons were actually being destroyed."

✅ The qualifications are equally recorded and must be carried: the treaty amounted "at most, to only 4 per cent of existing stocks of nuclear weapons"; ✅ the American strategic-defence programme remained a stumbling block, "even though it was only at the planning stage"; ✅ British and French weapons were not included, and the British prime minister "was determined that Britain should keep its own nuclear arsenal, and planned to develop Trident missiles".

⚠️ Why it still mattered: ✅ "this INF Treaty was an important turning point in the nuclear arms race, since it was the first time any weapons had been destroyed." ⚠️ The distinction between limiting future growth and destroying existing weapons is the examinable point, and it is also why the treaty functioned as a **signal** far beyond its 4 per cent.

⚠️ One detail is easy to miss and is worth marks: the treaty removed **all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia**. ⚠️ A satellite leadership watching Soviet missiles leave its own territory is receiving information about its place in Moscow's priorities.

### 3. Afghanistan and China: reducing commitments in every direction

✅ Afghanistan: over 100,000 Soviet troops; guerrillas impossible to subdue; a drain on resources and a blow to prestige; Chinese hostility, Islamic suspicion and repeated United Nations condemnations convincing Gorbachev to withdraw; the settlement signed at **Geneva on 14 April 1988** on the reciprocal condition that American military aid to the resistance stop, with withdrawal beginning on **15 May 1988** and completed on 15 February 1989 - ⚠️ the account's "1 May 1988" being the timetable the agreement set rather than the day the movement began; ✅ and in June 1988 Reagan went to Moscow to discuss the timetable for implementing the intermediate-range treaty.

✅ China: relations had deteriorated steadily after 1956 over "revisionism", the frontier and the rival claim to leadership of world communism, reaching "rock bottom" when China attacked Vietnam in February 1979. ✅ In 1984 the Chinese set out three grievances: Soviet troops in Afghanistan, Soviet backing for Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea, and the Soviet build-up along the Chinese frontiers of Mongolia and Manchuria. ✅ "Mikhail Gorbachev was determined to begin a new era in Sino-Russian relations." ✅ Five-year trade and economic agreements were signed in July 1985; ✅ "a formal reconciliation took place in May 1989 when Gorbachev visited Beijing"; ✅ and in the same year Vietnam withdrew its troops from Kampuchea.

⚠️ Notice that the Chinese grievances of 1984 and the Soviet moves of 1985-89 correspond almost item for item. ⚠️ That is not a coincidence but a strategy: a state reducing its burdens can convert each reduction into a diplomatic gain if it sequences them where they are wanted. ⚠️ It is also the reason the reconciliation and the Tiananmen crackdown occur within weeks of each other in the same city, which is a chronological trap the alert examiner enjoys setting.

### 4. The decisive omission, stated precisely

⚠️ Every satellite regime in eastern Europe rested, in the last resort, on the same guarantee: that if its own coercive apparatus failed, Soviet force would arrive. ⚠️ Withdrawing that guarantee does not require an announcement. It requires only that the guarantee stop being believed.

✅ The account records both the mechanism and its effect: "The critics became more daring as they realized that Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them. With no help to be expected from Moscow, when it came to the crisis, none of the communist governments was prepared to use sufficient force against the demonstrators (except in Romania)."

✅ It also records the reasoning: "When they came, the rebellions were too widespread, and it would have needed a huge commitment of tanks and troops to hold down the whole of eastern Europe simultaneously. Having only just succeeded in withdrawing from Afghanistan, Gorbachev had no desire for an even greater involvement."

✅ And it supplies the verdict on who converted the opportunity into an outcome: "In the end it was a triumph of 'people power': demonstrators deliberately defied the threat of violence in such huge numbers that troops would have had to shoot a large proportion of the population in the big cities to keep control."

⚠️ Three analytical points follow.

1. ⚠️ **Non-intervention is a choice, not an absence.** That is why it is best described as the decisive omission. ⚠️ A choice can be assessed, praised, criticised and counterfactually reversed; an absence cannot.
2. ⚠️ **It has a standard designation.** The renunciation of the right to intervene in the bloc was summarised at the time by a Soviet foreign-ministry spokesman, Gennadi Gerasimov, in October 1989, in a formulation the press named after a popular song - the "Sinatra Doctrine", meaning that each state could now do it its own way. ⚠️ The label is useful in an answer because it names the reversal of the earlier doctrine of limited sovereignty, itself studied as part of the Cold War.
3. ⚠️ **Scale mattered as much as principle.** ✅ The account's own reasoning is arithmetical: simultaneous risings across the whole region would have required a commitment beyond what a state just extricating itself from Afghanistan would contemplate. ⚠️ An answer that presents the omission as purely moral has missed half the evidence.

### 5. Moscow chooses the leaders: the active half of the policy

⚠️ It is a mistake to read new thinking as pure withdrawal. ✅ The account records active intervention of a different kind: Gorbachev "also helped to engineer the overthrow of the old-fashioned, hardline communist leaders in Czechoslovakia, and he was probably involved in plotting the overthrow of the East German, Romanian and Bulgarian leaders. His hope was that more progressive leaders would increase the chances of saving communism in Russia's satellite states."

⚠️ Two qualifications. ⚠️ First, note the account's own hedge - "probably involved" - and preserve it; this is an attributed probability, not an established fact, and writing it as certainty is a source error. ⚠️ Second, note the purpose: replacing hardliners with reformers was intended to **save** communism in the satellites, not to end it. ⚠️ This is the same rescue logic as at home, applied abroad, and it failed for the same reason.

✅ The account also records the resulting inversion: "the communist leaderships found it difficult to adapt to the new situation of having a leader in Moscow who was more progressive than they were." ⚠️ For forty years the satellites' problem had been a Moscow more orthodox than they wished; now it was a Moscow more reformist than they could survive. ⚠️ That inversion is one of the most quotable analytical points in the topic.

### 6. Malta, and the winding up of the rivalry at the top

⚠️ While the bloc dissolved beneath them, the two leaderships were settling the rivalry above. ⚠️ President Bush and Gorbachev met at Malta on 2-3 December 1989, weeks after the breach of the Berlin Wall, in the meeting conventionally treated as the point at which both sides publicly accepted that the confrontation was over; no treaty was signed there.

✅ The formal termination came the following year, and the record dates it: "In November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were 'no longer adversaries', and that none of their weapons would ever be used except in self-defence. The Cold War was over."

✅ The account's allocation of credit is balanced and worth reproducing: "Gorbachev must take much of the credit for bringing it to an end. His determination to work for disarmament broke the stalemate and impressed Reagan, who also deserves much credit for responding so positively to Gorbachev's initiatives."

⚠️ That sentence is the single best short answer to "Western pressure or Soviet choice?" - it is neither alone, and the account distributes the credit explicitly rather than leaving it to inference. Lesson 10 takes the instruments of November 1990 in detail.

### Comparative note: two kinds of imperial withdrawal

⚠️ Compare this withdrawal with the decolonisation sequences owned elsewhere in the syllabus, and one difference stands out. ⚠️ In most colonial withdrawals the metropole conceded under pressure from organised nationalist movements, and the concession was negotiated case by case over years. ⚠️ Here the metropole withdrew its guarantee **first**, across a whole region, and the organised movements then acted on the space that appeared. ⚠️ Poland is the partial exception that proves the pattern: Solidarity's strikes of August 1988 preceded the clearest demonstrations of non-intervention, which is exactly why the Polish case is taught first in Lesson 7.

⚠️ The comparison has a limit that must be stated. The eastern European states were formally sovereign allies, not colonies; the instrument of control was a treaty alliance, a party network and the memory of previous interventions, not an imperial administration. ⚠️ Calling the bloc an empire is a useful shorthand only if the difference is conceded in the same sentence.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ World history's clause on the redrawing of boundaries and on international relations transformed; the account's own end-of-chapter question asks directly why the Cold War came to an end and how this affected international relations.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Was the Cold War ended more by Soviet weakness, Western pressure or political choice?" is the standard evaluative stem, and this lesson supplies the third term without denying the first two.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Presenting the 1987 treaty as major disarmament. ✅ At most 4 per cent of stocks; its significance is that weapons were destroyed for the first time, and that Soviet missiles left East Germany and Czechoslovakia.
2. ⚠️ Treating non-intervention as a passive absence. ⚠️ It was a decision, with a stated arithmetic reason, and it has a standard designation.
3. ⚠️ Writing that Gorbachev "let eastern Europe go" as a single act. ✅ He also actively promoted reformist replacements in order to save communism there.
4. ⚠️ Asserting his involvement in the removal of the East German, Romanian and Bulgarian leaders as fact. ✅ The account says "probably involved"; preserve the hedge.
5. ⚠️ Dating the end of the Cold War to Malta. ⚠️ Malta was the public acknowledgement in December 1989; the formal instrument is the November 1990 treaty.
6. ⚠️ Giving all the credit to one side. ✅ The account credits Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response together.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ New thinking affected foreign affairs first: detente, China, Afghan withdrawal and "ultimately the ending of the Cold War in late 1990".
2. ✅ 1987 intermediate-range treaty: 436 American and 1,575 Soviet warheads scrapped over three years; strict verification; includes all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia; at most 4 per cent of stocks; first time any weapons had been destroyed.
3. ✅ Afghan settlement signed at **Geneva on 14 April 1988**, conditional on the ending of American military aid to the resistance; withdrawal begun **15 May 1988** and completed 15 February 1989; the account's 1 May 1988 is the agreed timetable. ✅ Reagan in Moscow in June 1988.
4. ✅ Chinese grievances of 1984: Afghanistan, Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea, the build-up on the Mongolian and Manchurian frontiers. ✅ Trade agreements July 1985; formal reconciliation May 1989 in Beijing; Vietnamese withdrawal from Kampuchea in 1989.
5. ✅ The decisive omission: critics grew bolder "as they realized that Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them"; no satellite government except Romania used sufficient force.
6. ✅ The arithmetic reason: simultaneous risings would have needed a huge commitment; after Afghanistan there was no appetite for a greater involvement.
7. ✅ The verdict on agency: "a triumph of 'people power'" by demonstrators too numerous to shoot.
8. ⚠️ The renunciation's standard designation is the "Sinatra Doctrine", used by the Soviet spokesman Gennadi Gerasimov in October 1989.
9. ✅ Moscow actively engineered leadership change in Czechoslovakia and was "probably involved" in East Germany, Romania and Bulgaria, hoping progressive leaders would save communism there.
10. ✅ The inversion: satellite leaderships now faced a Moscow more progressive than themselves.
11. ⚠️ Malta, 2-3 December 1989: Bush and Gorbachev publicly accept that the confrontation is over; no treaty signed there.
12. ✅ November 1990: NATO and Warsaw Pact states sign a treaty agreeing they are "no longer adversaries"; the account credits Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's positive response together.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the two halves of the explanation of 1989 that the record insists on, show why each is insufficient alone, and name the evidence for each.

**Model answer:** The first half is permission: Moscow's decision not to use force. The evidence is that critics became more daring as they realised Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them, that no communist government except Romania was prepared to use sufficient force once no help could be expected from Moscow, and that the reason was partly arithmetical - simultaneous risings across the region would have required a huge commitment of tanks and troops, which a leadership that had only just extracted itself from Afghanistan did not want. The second half is agency: organised society acting on the space that appeared. The evidence is that the process began in Poland in August 1988 when the Solidarity trade union organised huge anti-government strikes that eventually forced the government to allow free elections, and the account's own verdict that the outcome was a triumph of people power by demonstrators too numerous to shoot. Each is insufficient alone because permission without agency produces a reform programme rather than a revolution - nobody was obliged to use the space - while agency without permission produces 1953, 1956 and 1968, when comparable movements met force. The explanation is the conjunction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the non-intervention decision as a moral gesture and nothing else. The account gives a hard reason alongside it: holding down the whole of eastern Europe simultaneously would have required a commitment beyond what was contemplated after Afghanistan. An answer that presents the omission as purely principled loses the structural half of the point, and an answer that presents it as purely calculating loses the fact that it was still a choice a different leadership could have made differently - as Romania's initial use of the army and China's in June 1989 both show.

---

## Lesson 6 - Why the reforms failed at home: four recorded mechanisms

Progress: 6/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the opposition from radicals and conservatives and the Yeltsin-Ligachev split; the packed Supreme Soviet of May 1989 and the Moscow protests; the Law on State Enterprises and the wage-output mechanism; inflation, deficit and shortage; the Siberian miners' strike of July 1989 and its 42 demands; the poverty calculation of early 1990; and the Gorbachev-Yeltsin rivalry over union, party and market

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's numbered analysis of what went wrong with Gorbachev's policies, covering opposition from radicals and conservatives, the failure of the economic reforms including the Law on State Enterprises and the miners' strike, nationalist pressures, and the Gorbachev-Yeltsin rivalry set out point by point
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a partial reform, and the loop it created

```text
  THE LAW ON STATE ENTERPRISES - HOW A HALF-REFORM DESTROYS ITSELF

   Intention: link wages to output, so that effort is rewarded
        |
        v
   But output was measured BY ITS VALUE IN ROUBLES
        |
        v
   So factories had an incentive NOT to raise overall output,
   but to shift production towards MORE EXPENSIVE GOODS and
   to REDUCE OUTPUT OF CHEAPER GOODS
        |
        +---------------------------+
        |                           |
        v                           v
   HIGHER WAGES                CHEAP BASIC GOODS DISAPPEAR
        |                      soap, washing powder, razor
        v                      blades, cups and saucers,
   Government prints           TV sets, food
   more money to pay them           |
        |                           v
        v                      QUEUES GET LONGER
   INFLATION SOARS;
   BUDGET DEFICIT SOARS
        |                           |
        +------------+--------------+
                     v
        A POPULATION WHOSE EXPECTATIONS HAD BEEN RAISED
        BY PROMISES NOW FACES WORSE SHORTAGES THAN BEFORE
                     |
                     v
        JULY 1989: SIBERIAN MINERS CANNOT WASH AFTER A SHIFT
        "What kind of a regime is it, if we can't even get
         washed?"  -> sit-in -> strike -> half a million
         miners -> 42 demands -> the first major strike
         since 1917
```

⚠️ Everything in the diagram is a mechanism with a recorded outcome. Learn the loop rather than the list; the loop is what an examiner rewards.

### 1. The learner's problem: why would reform make things worse?

⚠️ The intuitive model is that reform is a movement from a bad system towards a better one, so partial reform should produce partial improvement. ⚠️ The Soviet case is the textbook refutation.

⚠️ A command system and a market system are each internally consistent in a specific way. The command system suppresses prices as signals and substitutes plan targets; the market system uses prices as signals and dispenses with targets. ⚠️ A halfway arrangement can have **neither** coordinating device working: the plan no longer commands, and prices are not yet allowed to allocate. ⚠️ The result is not a half-improvement but a distinct third condition, often worse than either.

⚠️ This is the single most transferable analytical point in the whole topic, and it applies equally to Russia's shock therapy in Lesson 12 and to the eastern European transitions in Lesson 8.

### 2. Mechanism one: the elite splits in two directions at once

✅ "As the reforms got under way, Gorbachev ran into problems. Some party members, such as Boris Yeltsin, were more radical than Gorbachev, and felt that the reforms were not drastic enough. They wanted a change to a western-style market economy as quickly as possible, though they knew this would cause great short-term hardship for the Russian people. On the other hand, the conservatives, like Yegor Ligachev, felt that the changes were too drastic and that the Party was in danger of losing control."

✅ "This caused a dangerous split in the Party and made it difficult for Gorbachev to satisfy either group. Although he had some sympathy with Yeltsin's views, he could not afford to side with Yeltsin against Ligachev, because Ligachev controlled the party apparatus."

⚠️ The last clause is the whole political problem in one line. ⚠️ A reforming leader whose programme must be executed by an apparatus controlled by its opponents is dependent on the people he is reforming. ⚠️ He can move only as fast as they permit, which is slower than his supporters demand, which costs him his supporters.

✅ The mechanism then produced a visible crisis: "The conservatives were in a large majority, and when the Congress of People's Deputies elected the new Supreme Soviet (May 1989), it was packed with conservatives; Yeltsin and many other radicals were not elected. This led to massive protest demonstrations in Moscow, where Yeltsin was a popular figure, since he had cleaned up the corrupt Moscow Communist Party organization."

⚠️ And then the account's own closing of the loop with Lesson 4: "Demonstrations would not have been allowed before Gorbachev's time, but glasnost - encouraging people to voice their criticisms - was now in full flow, and was beginning to turn against the Communist Party."

### 3. Mechanism two: the economic reforms did not deliver, and then made things worse

✅ The record: "The rate of economic growth in 1988 and 1989 stayed exactly the same as it had been in previous years. In 1990 national income actually fell, and continued to fall - by about 15 per cent - in 1991. Some economists think that the USSR was going through an economic crisis as serious as the one in the USA in the early 1930s."

✅ And the specific cause: "A major cause of the crisis was the disastrous results of the Law on State Enterprises. The problem was that wages were now dependent on output, but since output was measured by its value in roubles, factories were tempted not to increase overall output, but to concentrate on more expensive goods and reduce output of cheaper goods. This led to higher wages, forcing the government to print more money to pay them with. Inflation soared, and so did the government's budget deficit. Basic goods such as soap, washing-powder, razor-blades, cups and saucers, TV sets and food were in very short supply, and the queues in the towns got longer."

⚠️ Three things make this the best single example in the topic.

- ⚠️ It shows an incentive reform failing because the **measure** was wrong, not because the idea was wrong. Linking pay to output is unobjectionable; measuring output in roubles at administered prices converts it into an instruction to make expensive things.
- ⚠️ It shows the fiscal transmission explicitly: higher wages, money printed to pay them, inflation and deficit. That is a causal chain a candidate can reproduce in three clauses and earn marks for.
- ⚠️ It shows exactly **who** was hurt: the people who buy soap and razor blades. ⚠️ Reform that worsens the supply of the cheapest goods is politically the most dangerous possible reform, because its victims are the majority.

✅ The account's judgement on the political effect is blunt: "Disillusion with Gorbachev and his reforms rapidly set in, and, having had their expectations raised by his promises, people became outraged at the shortages."

### 4. Mechanism three: the miners of July 1989

✅ The trigger is recorded exactly: "In July 1989 some coal miners in Siberia found there was no soap to wash themselves with at the end of their shift. 'What kind of a regime is it', they asked, 'if we can't even get washed?'"

✅ The escalation: "After staging a sit-in, they decided to go on strike; they were quickly joined by other miners in Siberia, in Kazakhstan and in the Donbass (Ukraine), the biggest coalmining area in the USSR, until half a million miners were on strike. It was the first major strike since 1917."

✅ The organisation: "The miners were well disciplined and organized, holding mass meetings outside party headquarters in the main towns. They put forward detailed demands, 42 in all. These included better living and working conditions, better supplies of food, a share in the profits and more local control over the mines."

✅ The escalation of content, which is the analytically decisive part: "Later, influenced by what was happening in Poland (where a non-communist president had just been elected), they called for independent trade unions like Poland's Solidarity, and in some areas they demanded an end to the privileged position of the Communist Party."

✅ The outcome: "The government soon gave way and granted many of the demands, promising a complete reorganization of the industry and full local control. By the end of July the strike was over, but the general economic situation did not improve."

⚠️ Four points to take.

1. ⚠️ **The symbolic force of the trigger.** A workers' state in which workers cannot wash after a shift has failed on the one claim it could not afford to fail on. The miners' own question is better than any commentary and should be quoted.
2. ⚠️ **The constituency.** ✅ The first major strike since 1917 was by the industrial working class in whose name the state existed. ⚠️ This is why "the workers turned against the workers' state" is not rhetoric here but a description.
3. ⚠️ **The demand escalation.** ✅ Demands began materially - soap, food, conditions - and became **political** under Polish influence: independent unions, and in places an end to the Party's privileged position. ⚠️ That is the permission-and-agency mechanism of Lesson 5 operating inside the USSR itself, transmitted from the satellites.
4. ⚠️ **The concession that solved nothing.** ✅ The government granted much and the situation did not improve, which demonstrated that the problem was not the government's willingness but its capacity.

✅ The account adds the measured consequence: "Early in 1990 it was calculated that about a quarter of the population was living below the poverty line; worst affected were those with large families, the unemployed and pensioners." ⚠️ Treat this as a recorded calculation, and attribute it as one; do not convert it into a different statistic.

✅ And the political verdict: "Gorbachev was fast losing control of the reform movement which he had started, and the success of the miners was bound to encourage the radicals to press for even more far-reaching changes."

### 5. Mechanism four: the rivalry that gave the opposition a state

⚠️ Nationalist pressure, the third recorded mechanism, was taught in Lesson 3. The fourth is the rivalry, and it is the one that converts discontent into an alternative government.

| Question | ✅ Yeltsin's position | ✅ Gorbachev's position | ⚠️ What the disagreement really was |
|---|---|---|---|
| The union | Voluntary: "each republic should be independent but also have joint responsibilities to the Soviet Union as well"; any republic wanting to opt out, as Lithuania did, "should be allowed to do so" | "A purely voluntary union would lead to disintegration" | ⚠️ Whether the union's authority came from the republics or preceded them - a constitutional question disguised as a practical one |
| The Party | "Completely disillusioned with the Communist Party and the way the traditionalists had treated him"; the Party "no longer deserved its privileged position in the state" | "Still hoping against hope that the Party could be transformed into a humane and democratic organization" | ⚠️ Whether the instrument of reform could be the object of reform |
| The economy | "A rapid changeover to a market economy, though he knew that this would be painful for the Russian people" | "Much more cautious, realizing that Yeltsin's plans would cause massive unemployment and even higher prices"; aware of his own unpopularity and that "if things got even worse, he might well be overthrown" | ⚠️ Whether to take the pain at once with a mandate, or gradually without one |

⚠️ What makes this more than a personal quarrel is the institution behind it. ✅ Yeltsin had been excluded from the Supreme Soviet by conservatives; he returned by being elected president of the parliament of the Russian republic in May 1990. ⚠️ From that moment the opposition to the union leadership was not a faction but a **government**, with a territory, a budget, an apparatus and its own electoral mandate. ⚠️ Lesson 11 shows what that made possible in August 1991.

### Criticism and reply: was Gorbachev simply too slow?

⚠️ **The objection**, which the account itself preserves as an end-of-chapter proposition: "It was Gorbachev's reluctance to commit himself to sufficiently radical changes that led to the break-up of the Soviet Union."

⚠️ **The evidence for it.** The half-reform loop above is the strongest possible support: a fully executed price and ownership reform would at least have had one working coordinating mechanism. Yeltsin's radicals argued exactly this at the time.

⚠️ **The reply.** Two facts cut against it. ✅ First, Gorbachev's caution had a stated basis: Yeltsin's plans "would cause massive unemployment and even higher prices", and he was already unpopular enough to fear being overthrown - which is a political constraint, not timidity. ✅ Second, the counterfactual was actually run: Lesson 12 shows what happened when the radical programme was executed from January 1992, and the record is prices thirty times higher within a year, over a million jobs lost, thousands homeless in tents, and most of former state industry in the hands of a small group of financiers by the end of 1995. ⚠️ Speed did not rescue the economy; it changed who suffered and when.

⚠️ **The residual.** The honest verdict is that both criticisms are partly right because they concern different subsystems. ⚠️ Politically he moved faster than his apparatus could absorb; economically he moved slower than his half-reform could survive. ⚠️ Saying that in one sentence - that the components of the system could not be reformed at compatible speeds - is worth more than choosing a side.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Political philosophies and their effect on society: the Law on State Enterprises is the clearest available demonstration that institutional design, not ideology alone, determines what a system delivers.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Any stem containing "why did Gorbachev's reforms fail", or the account's own proposition about insufficiently radical change, routes here.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the economy collapsed because of communism as such. ⚠️ The specific collapse of 1990-91 was a **reform-period** collapse, and the named mechanism is a mis-specified incentive law.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the miners' strike. ⚠️ It is the single most vivid legitimacy datum in the topic and it was the first major strike since 1917.
3. ⚠️ Treating the Gorbachev-Yeltsin split as personal rivalry. ⚠️ It was a three-part programmatic disagreement, and after May 1990 it was institutional.
4. ⚠️ Giving your own poverty or inflation figures. ✅ Use the recorded calculation of early 1990 - about a quarter of the population below the poverty line - and attribute it.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the transmission from Poland. ✅ The miners' political demands followed the election of a non-communist Polish president.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Radicals like Yeltsin wanted a western-style market economy quickly; conservatives like Ligachev thought the changes too drastic; Ligachev controlled the party apparatus, so Gorbachev could not side with Yeltsin.
2. ✅ May 1989: the new Supreme Soviet was packed with conservatives and Yeltsin was excluded, producing massive protest demonstrations in Moscow.
3. ✅ Growth unchanged 1988-89; national income fell in 1990 and by about 15 per cent in 1991; an attributed comparison with the American crisis of the early 1930s.
4. ✅ The Law on State Enterprises: wages tied to output, output measured in roubles, so factories shifted to expensive goods and cut cheap ones; wages rose, money was printed, inflation and the deficit soared, and basic goods vanished.
5. ✅ The shortage list to quote: soap, washing powder, razor blades, cups and saucers, TV sets and food.
6. ✅ July 1989: Siberian miners without soap; sit-in; strike; joined in Siberia, Kazakhstan and the Donbass until half a million were out; the first major strike since 1917; 42 demands.
7. ✅ Demands escalated from material to political under Polish influence: independent unions like Solidarity, and in places an end to the Party's privileged position.
8. ✅ The government granted much of it; the economic situation did not improve; early in 1990 about a quarter of the population was calculated to be below the poverty line, worst affected being large families, the unemployed and pensioners.
9. ✅ Gorbachev-Yeltsin: voluntary union against feared disintegration; the Party's privilege against the Party's transformation; rapid marketisation against caution about unemployment and prices.
10. ✅ May 1990: Yeltsin elected president of the parliament of the Russian republic, converting opposition into a government.
11. ⚠️ The transferable principle: a system stuck between plan and market may have neither coordinating mechanism working, and partial reform can therefore be worse than either pure arrangement.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the Law on State Enterprises, explain in a single causal chain how a reform intended to raise productivity ended by emptying the shops, and state why this particular failure was politically more dangerous than slow growth would have been.

**Model answer:** Wages were made dependent on output; output was measured by its value in roubles; so enterprises had an incentive not to produce more in total but to shift towards more expensive goods and to cut production of cheaper ones; this raised the wage bill; the government printed money to pay it; inflation and the budget deficit soared; and the goods that disappeared were precisely the cheap basics - soap, washing powder, razor blades, cups and saucers, television sets and food - so the queues lengthened. It was politically more dangerous than slow growth because slow growth disappoints expectations while this reform inflicted a visible, daily, universal deterioration on exactly the constituency in whose name the state existed. The proof is the miners' strike of July 1989, which began when Siberian miners found no soap at the end of a shift and asked what kind of regime it was if they could not even get washed; half a million miners joined, it was the first major strike since 1917, and the 42 demands moved from food and conditions to independent trade unions and, in places, an end to the Communist Party's privileged position.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating this as proof that the reform was too timid and that faster marketisation would have worked. The counterfactual was later run in full: shock therapy from January 1992 produced prices on average thirty times higher within the year, over a million people laid off, thousands living in tents, and most of former state industry in the hands of a small group of financiers by the end of 1995. Speed changed the distribution and timing of the pain; it did not by itself supply a working economy, and an answer that assumes otherwise is arguing against evidence held in this very session.

---

## Lesson 7 - 1989 in Eastern Europe I: Poland and Hungary, or how a regime negotiates its own exit

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Jaruzelski's martial law of 1981 and its lifting in July 1983; the subsidy cuts and protest strikes of 1988; the February 1989 talks and the April 1989 constitutional agreement with its five terms; the June 1989 election results; Mazowiecki as the first non-communist prime minister in the bloc; Walesa's election in December 1990; and Hungary's reform-communist route from Kadar's 1985 admission to the March 1990 election

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's treatment of why and how communism collapsed in eastern Europe, specifically its sections on Poland leading the way and on the peaceful revolution spreading to Hungary, together with its earlier statement that the whole process began in Poland in August 1988
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the Polish sequence, with the decision points marked

```text
  1981  Jaruzelski becomes leader
          |
          | Solidarity demands a referendum to show its support
          v
        DECISION POINT 1: USE FORCE
        Martial law declared - the army takes over control;
        Solidarity banned; thousands of activists arrested.
        "The army obeyed his orders because everybody was
         still afraid of Russian military intervention."
          |
  Jul 1983  Martial law lifted; Solidarity members gradually released
          |
          | "But the underlying problem was still there: all
          | attempts to improve the economy failed."
          v
  1988  Jaruzelski cuts government subsidies to economize
          -> food prices rise -> PROTEST STRIKES
          |
          v
        DECISION POINT 2: USE FORCE AGAIN?
        "This time Jaruzelski decided not to risk using force;
         he knew that there would be no backing from Moscow,
         and realized that he needed opposition support to
         deal with the economic crisis."
          |
  Feb 1989  TALKS open: communist government + Solidarity +
          |  other opposition groups (the Roman Catholic Church
          |  "had been loud in its criticisms")
          v
  Apr 1989  CONSTITUTIONAL AGREEMENT - five terms
          |   1. Solidarity may become a political party
          |   2. Two houses: a lower house and a senate
          |   3. Lower house: 65% of seats reserved for communists
          |   4. Senate: freely elected, no guaranteed seats
          |   5. The two houses voting together elect a president,
          |      who then chooses a prime minister
          v
  Jun 1989  ELECTIONS
          |   Senate: Solidarity wins 92 of 100
          |   Lower house: Solidarity wins 160 of the 161 seats
          |               it was allowed to contest
          v
  Aug 1989  THE COMPROMISE
          |   Jaruzelski narrowly elected president - thanks to
          |   the guaranteed communist seats
          |   He chooses Tadeusz Mazowiecki, a Solidarity
          |   supporter, as prime minister: "the first
          |   non-communist leader in the eastern bloc"
          |   Mazowiecki forms a mixed government
          v
  Dec 1990  Guaranteed communist seats removed; Lech Walesa
            elected president. "The peaceful revolution in
            Poland was complete."
```

### 1. The learner's problem: why start with Poland at all?

⚠️ Because the record does. ✅ "The process began in Poland in August 1988 when the 'Solidarity' trade union organized huge anti-government strikes. These eventually forced the government to allow free elections, in which the communists were heavily defeated (June 1989). Revolutionary protests rapidly spread to all the other Russian satellite states."

⚠️ And because Poland is the case in which the two halves of the explanation are separately visible. ⚠️ The same leader faced the same kind of challenge twice, in 1981 and in 1988, and answered it differently - and the account states the reason for the change in one clause: "he knew that there would be no backing from Moscow". ⚠️ Very few historical cases supply their own controlled comparison so cleanly.

⚠️ There is a second reason, and it matters for the structure of the whole session. Poland's strikes of August 1988 **precede** the most visible demonstrations of Soviet non-intervention elsewhere. ⚠️ That ordering protects the answer from the lazy formulation that Gorbachev simply released eastern Europe and the region then collapsed. ⚠️ Polish society moved first; Moscow's restraint then determined what that movement could achieve.

### 2. 1981 against 1988: the same man, the same problem, two answers

| | ✅ 1981 | ✅ 1988 |
|---|---|---|
| Trigger | Solidarity demands a referendum to demonstrate the strength of its support | Subsidy cuts raise food prices; protest strikes break out |
| Response | Martial law: the army takes over control; Solidarity banned; thousands of activists arrested | No force; talks opened in February 1989 with Solidarity and other opposition groups |
| Why it worked or did not | "The army obeyed his orders because everybody was still afraid of Russian military intervention" | "He knew that there would be no backing from Moscow, and realized that he needed opposition support to deal with the economic crisis" |
| Result | "By July 1983 the government was in firm control" - but "all attempts to improve the economy failed" | April 1989 constitutional agreement; June 1989 elections; August 1989 the first non-communist prime minister in the bloc |

⚠️ The comparison establishes three things at once, and an answer that draws all three is doing serious analytical work.

- ⚠️ **Coercion was available and had worked before.** The 1989 outcome was therefore not the automatic result of popular pressure; the same pressure had been suppressed seven years earlier.
- ⚠️ **What changed was the external guarantee, and the army knew it.** ✅ The account is explicit that obedience in 1981 rested on fear of Russian intervention, and that in 1988 the absence of backing was Jaruzelski's stated reason for not repeating it.
- ⚠️ **The economic failure supplied the second motive.** ✅ He "realized that he needed opposition support to deal with the economic crisis". ⚠️ A regime that cannot fix the economy alone must share responsibility for the pain, and sharing responsibility requires sharing power.

### 3. The April 1989 agreement: a designed transition, and why its design failed

⚠️ Read the five terms again as a piece of political engineering. ⚠️ The communists reserved 65 per cent of the lower house, conceded a freely elected senate, and arranged that the two houses voting together would elect the president. ⚠️ The intended arithmetic is obvious: guaranteed seats in the larger chamber would secure the presidency, the senate would absorb opposition energy, and Solidarity would be co-opted into shared responsibility for austerity without acquiring control.

⚠️ The design worked exactly once and then broke. ✅ The election produced 92 of 100 senate seats and 160 of the 161 contestable lower-house seats for Solidarity. ✅ The guaranteed seats still delivered the presidency: "Jaruzelski was narrowly elected president, thanks to all the guaranteed communist seats in the lower house". ✅ But he then "chose a Solidarity supporter, Tadeusz Mazowiecki, as prime minister - the first non-communist leader in the eastern bloc", and Mazowiecki "chose a mixed government of communists and Solidarity supporters".

⚠️ Why did the design break? ⚠️ Because a reserved majority is not the same as a political majority. ⚠️ Winning 92 of 100 freely contested senate seats and 160 of 161 contestable lower-house seats is a public measurement of support, and once that measurement exists, governing against it becomes impossible even when it remains formally legal. ⚠️ The guaranteed seats delivered an office; they could not deliver authority.

✅ The account draws the conclusion itself: "The new constitution proved to be only transitional. After the collapse of communism in the other east European states, further changes in Poland removed the guaranteed communist seats, and in the elections of December 1990, Lech Walesa, the Solidarity leader, was elected president. The peaceful revolution in Poland was complete."

### 4. Hungary: the same outcome by a different route

⚠️ Where Poland's transition was forced from outside the Party by an organised social movement, Hungary's was conceded from inside it by reform communists.

✅ The admission: "In Hungary even Kadar himself admitted in 1985 that living standards had fallen over the previous five years, and he blamed poor management, poor organization and outdated machinery and equipment in the state sector of industry. He announced new measures of decentralization - company councils and elected works managers."

✅ The internal struggle: "By 1987 there was conflict in the Communist Party between those who wanted more reform and those who wanted a return to strict central control. This reached a climax in May 1988 when, amid dramatic scenes at the party conference, Kadar and eight of his supporters were voted off the Politburo, leaving the progressives in control."

✅ The insufficiency: "But as in the USSR, progress was not drastic enough for many people. Two large opposition parties became increasingly active. These were the liberal Alliance of Free Democrats, and the Democratic Forum, which stood for the interests of farmers and peasants."

✅ The decision and the outcome: "The Hungarian communist leadership, following the example of the Poles, decided to go peacefully. Free elections were held in March 1990, and in spite of a change of name to the Hungarian Socialist Party, the communists suffered a crushing defeat. The election was won by the Democratic Forum, whose leader, Jozsef Antall, became prime minister."

⚠️ Four observations that convert this from narrative into analysis.

1. ⚠️ **The demonstration effect is stated in the source.** ✅ "Following the example of the Poles." ⚠️ Sequence is causal here, not merely chronological, and an answer that lists the countries in any order loses that.
2. ⚠️ **Reform from inside met the same wall as reform from inside the USSR.** ✅ "As in the USSR, progress was not drastic enough for many people." ⚠️ The same Lesson 4 formula - permission outrunning delivery - operates in Hungary too.
3. ⚠️ **Renaming did not work.** ✅ The communists changed their name to the Hungarian Socialist Party and were still crushed. ⚠️ Compare Bulgaria in Lesson 8, where a comparable renaming succeeded, and the comparison becomes a question worth asking.
4. ⚠️ **Hungary's decisive external act belongs to Lesson 8.** ✅ The opening of the Hungarian frontier with Austria is what allowed thousands of East Germans to escape westward, and it is therefore taught where its consequence falls.

### 5. What made negotiated exit possible here and not elsewhere

⚠️ Two preconditions are visible in both cases, and their absence elsewhere explains the divergences in Lesson 8.

```text
  PRECONDITION 1: AN ORGANISED COUNTERPART
  Poland  -> Solidarity: a banned but surviving trade union with
             national reach, plus a Church "loud in its criticisms"
  Hungary -> two large opposition parties: the Alliance of Free
             Democrats and the Democratic Forum
  => Somebody exists who can negotiate, sign, and deliver
     compliance afterwards.

  PRECONDITION 2: A DIVIDED OR REFORM-MINDED RULING PARTY
  Poland  -> a leadership that had already tried force once and
             knew its cost
  Hungary -> progressives who removed Kadar and eight supporters
             from the Politburo in May 1988
  => Somebody exists on the other side of the table who prefers
     a negotiated exit to a fight.

  WHERE EITHER IS MISSING, THE ROUTE CHANGES:
  no counterpart + no division  -> Romania (violence)
  no counterpart + division      -> Bulgaria (managed continuity)
```

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Political philosophies and their effect on society, and the redrawal of political boundaries: this is the mechanism by which a one-party system exits without a war.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Analyse the causes of the collapse of communism in the USSR and eastern Europe" requires at least one case treated in this depth; Poland is the best choice because it contains its own control.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Beginning the eastern European story in 1989. ✅ It began in August 1988 in Poland, and Solidarity's earlier suppression in 1981 is part of the evidence.
2. ⚠️ Writing that Solidarity "overthrew" the government. ✅ It negotiated a constitutional agreement in April 1989, won an election in June, and its supporter became prime minister in August under a communist president.
3. ⚠️ Forgetting that Jaruzelski remained president after the June 1989 election. ⚠️ The transitional compromise is the interesting part, not an embarrassment to be tidied away.
4. ⚠️ Treating Poland and Hungary as one case. ⚠️ One was forced from outside the Party, the other conceded from inside it, and the two preconditions above explain the difference.
5. ⚠️ Adding Solidarity's membership, leadership history or round-table detail beyond what is recorded here. ⚠️ Keep to the facts the record actually supplies.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The whole eastern European process "began in Poland in August 1988" with Solidarity's huge anti-government strikes.
2. ✅ 1981: Jaruzelski declared martial law, banned Solidarity and arrested thousands; the army obeyed "because everybody was still afraid of Russian military intervention"; martial law lifted by July 1983.
3. ✅ 1988: subsidy cuts raised food prices and produced protest strikes; Jaruzelski "decided not to risk using force" because there would be "no backing from Moscow" and he needed opposition support on the economy.
4. ✅ February 1989: talks with Solidarity and other opposition groups, the Roman Catholic Church having been "loud in its criticisms".
5. ✅ April 1989 agreement: Solidarity legalised as a party; two houses; 65 per cent of lower-house seats reserved for communists; a freely elected senate; the two houses jointly electing a president who chooses a prime minister.
6. ✅ June 1989: Solidarity won 92 of 100 senate seats and 160 of the 161 contestable lower-house seats.
7. ✅ August 1989: Jaruzelski narrowly elected president on the guaranteed seats; Mazowiecki became the first non-communist leader in the eastern bloc and formed a mixed government.
8. ✅ December 1990: guaranteed seats removed; Walesa elected president; "the peaceful revolution in Poland was complete".
9. ✅ Hungary: Kadar admitted falling living standards in 1985 and blamed management, organisation and outdated equipment; May 1988 he and eight supporters were voted off the Politburo; March 1990 free elections; the renamed Hungarian Socialist Party was crushed; the Democratic Forum won and Jozsef Antall became prime minister.
10. ✅ The Hungarian leadership acted "following the example of the Poles" - the demonstration effect is in the source.
11. ⚠️ Two preconditions for negotiated exit: an organised counterpart who can deliver compliance, and a divided or reform-minded ruling party that prefers negotiation to a fight.

### Concept check

**Question:** The same Polish leader met comparable challenges in 1981 and 1988 and answered them differently. Use the contrast to show why "popular pressure" is an incomplete explanation of 1989.

**Model answer:** In 1981 Solidarity demanded a referendum to demonstrate its support, and Jaruzelski declared martial law, banned the union and arrested thousands of activists; the account explains why it worked, saying the army obeyed his orders because everybody was still afraid of Russian military intervention, and by July 1983 the government was in firm control. In 1988 subsidy cuts raised food prices and produced protest strikes, and this time he decided not to risk using force, for two stated reasons: he knew there would be no backing from Moscow, and he realised he needed opposition support to deal with the economic crisis. Popular pressure is therefore incomplete as an explanation because the pressure existed in both years and produced opposite results; what changed was the credibility of the external guarantee on which the coercive option rested, together with an economic failure that made shared responsibility attractive. The strongest form of the argument is that pressure supplies the demand and the withdrawal of the guarantee supplies the opportunity, and neither alone produces the outcome.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the April 1989 agreement as a communist surrender. It was designed to preserve control: 65 per cent of the lower house was reserved, and those reserved seats did in fact deliver the presidency to Jaruzelski in August 1989. What defeated the design was not its arithmetic but the public measurement of support that the free part of the election produced - 92 of 100 senate seats and 160 of 161 contestable lower-house seats - after which governing against that measurement was politically impossible even where it remained formally legal.
