## Lesson 8 - 1989 in Eastern Europe II: four more routes, and why the outcomes differed

Progress: 8/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: East Germany's escape route, New Forum, the October demonstrations, Honecker's removal and Krenz's concessions; Czechoslovakia's Velvet Revolution from 17 November to 29 December 1989; Romania's violent overthrow from Timisoara to 25 December 1989 and the National Salvation Front; Bulgaria's managed continuity under a renamed party; Albania's delayed collapse; and the shared post-communist economic problem

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's country-by-country treatment of the collapse in eastern Europe, covering Germany reunited, Czechoslovakia, and the rest of eastern Europe under Romania, Bulgaria, Albania and Yugoslavia, together with its section on eastern Europe after communism
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one wave, five routes, and the two variables that sort them

```text
                    ORGANISED OPPOSITION COUNTERPART?
                         YES                    NO
                 +---------------------+---------------------+
                 |                     |                     |
     D  YES      | POLAND (L7)         | BULGARIA            |
     I           | negotiated exit,    | Politburo votes     |
     V           | April 1989 pact     | Zhivkov out,        |
     I           | HUNGARY (L7)        | 10 Nov 1989;        |
     D           | reform communists   | renamed Bulgarian   |
     E           | concede, Mar 1990   | Socialist Party     |
     D           | CZECHOSLOVAKIA      | WINS free elections,|
                 | Charter 77 + Havel; | Jun 1990            |
     R           | Velvet Revolution   | = MANAGED CONTINUITY|
     U           | in days             |                     |
     L           +---------------------+---------------------+
     I  NO       | EAST GERMANY        | ROMANIA             |
     N           | New Forum + the     | Ceausescu; the      |
     G           | Protestant Church,  | Securitate; "no     |
                 | but the decisive    | equivalent of the   |
     P           | split is INSIDE the | Polish Solidarity   |
     A           | elite: Honecker     | and Czech Charter   |
     R           | wants to fire, his  | 77"                 |
     T           | colleagues refuse   | = VIOLENCE, then    |
     Y           | = ELITE COLLAPSE    |   former communists |
                 |   + EXIT PRESSURE   |   in the new body   |
                 +---------------------+---------------------+

   ALBANIA sits outside the grid: communist since 1945 by its own
   resistance movement, not Soviet imposition; poorest and most
   backward in Europe; collapse delayed to 1991-92.
```

⚠️ The grid is an analytical device, not a source claim. ⚠️ Its value is that it converts five narratives into two variables, and a 20-mark answer that deploys two variables beats one that recites five stories.

### 1. East Germany: exit, demonstration, and a split at the top

⚠️ East Germany had a problem no other satellite had: an adjacent, wealthier state that recognised its citizens as its own. ⚠️ That made **exit** a political weapon, and the record shows it being used.

✅ The sequence begins with a deal: "Gorbachev, desperate to get financial help for the USSR from West Germany, paid a visit to Chancellor Kohl in Bonn, and promised to help bring an end to the divided Europe, in return for German economic aid. In effect he was secretly promising freedom for East Germany (June 1989)."

✅ Then the exit: "During August and September 1989, thousands of East Germans began to escape to the west via Poland, Czechoslovakia and Hungary, when Hungary opened its frontier with Austria."

✅ Then organised opposition: "The Protestant Church in East Germany became the focus of an opposition party called New Forum, which campaigned to bring an end to the repressive and atheistic communist regime. In October 1989 there was a wave of demonstrations all over East Germany demanding freedom and an end to communism."

✅ Then the decision that mattered: "Honecker wanted to order the army to open fire on the demonstrators, but other leading communists were not prepared to cause widespread bloodshed. They dropped Honecker, and his successor Egon Krenz made concessions. The Berlin Wall was breached (9 November 1989) and free elections were promised."

⚠️ Three analytical points.

- ⚠️ **Hungary's frontier decision was an act of foreign policy with a domestic target.** Opening the Austrian border did not change Hungary's own transition; it destroyed East Germany's ability to contain its population. ⚠️ This is the clearest single instance in the topic of one country's domestic liberalisation becoming another country's regime crisis.
- ⚠️ **Exit and voice reinforced each other.** Those who left reduced the regime's labour force and its legitimacy; those who stayed demonstrated. ⚠️ A regime facing both simultaneously cannot answer either without worsening the other, since tightening the borders inflames the demonstrations and tolerating departure advertises the failure.
- ⚠️ **The fatal division was inside the leadership, not between leadership and society.** ✅ Honecker wanted to shoot; his colleagues would not. ⚠️ The comparison with Romania below shows what happens when that division does not occur in time.

### 2. Czechoslovakia: why the Velvet Revolution took days

✅ The background: "Czechoslovakia had one of the most successful economies of eastern Europe. She traded extensively with the west and her industry and commerce remained buoyant throughout the 1970s. But during the early 1980s the economy ran into trouble, mainly because there had been very little attempt to modernize industry. Husak, who had been in power since 1968, resigned (1987), but his successor, Milos Jakes, did not have a reputation as a reformer."

✅ The sequence: "Then things changed suddenly in a matter of days, in what became known as the Velvet Revolution. On 17 November 1989 there was a huge demonstration in Prague, at which many people were injured by police brutality. Charter 77, now led by the famous playwright Vaclav Havel, organized further opposition, and after Alexander Dubcek had spoken at a public rally for the first time since 1968, a national strike was declared. This was enough to topple the communist regime: Jakes resigned and Havel was elected president (29 December 1989)."

⚠️ Speed requires explanation, and three things supply it.

1. ⚠️ **A pre-existing opposition with moral authority.** Charter 77 had existed for over a decade; it did not have to be built in November.
2. ⚠️ **A usable memory.** ✅ Dubcek's reappearance "for the first time since 1968" linked the new movement to the earlier suppressed reform, which both legitimised it and named the grievance.
3. ⚠️ **A general strike as the instrument.** ⚠️ A national strike converts street demonstrations into an economy-wide refusal, which no government can wait out.

⚠️ The comparison with East Germany is instructive: Prague had a counterpart ready to take power, so the transfer could be immediate; East Berlin had a movement but its crisis ran through an elite split and then, almost at once, into the German question of Lesson 9.

### 3. Romania: the exception that anchors every generalisation

✅ The regime: "In Romania the communist regime of Nicolae Ceausescu (leader since 1965) was one of the most brutal and repressive anywhere in the world. His secret police, the Securitate, were responsible for many deaths."

✅ The sequence: "When the revolution came, it was short and bloody: it began in Timisoara, a town in western Romania, with a demonstration in support of a popular priest who was being harassed by the Securitate. This was brutally put down and many people were killed (17 December 1989). This caused outrage throughout the country, and when, four days later, Ceausescu and his wife appeared on the balcony of Communist Party headquarters in Bucharest to address a massed rally, they were greeted with boos and shouts of 'murderers of Timisoara'. Television coverage was abruptly halted and Ceausescu abandoned his speech."

✅ The turn: "It seemed as though the entire population of Bucharest now streamed out onto the streets. At first the army fired on the crowds and many were killed and wounded. The following day the crowds came out again; but by now the army was refusing to continue the killing, and the Ceausescus had lost control. They were arrested, tried by a military tribunal and shot (25 December 1989)."

⚠️ **Attribution discipline applies here without exception.** ✅ The account says "many people were killed" at Timisoara and "many were killed and wounded" in Bucharest. ❌ Do not substitute any numerical figure, from any source, for those words. ⚠️ Figures for the Romanian December were disputed at the time and remain contested; the session states what its source states.

✅ The aftermath, which is the analytically important half: "The hated Ceausescu had gone, but many elements of communism remained in Romania. The country had never had democratic government, and opposition had been so ruthlessly crushed that there was no equivalent of the Polish Solidarity and Czech Charter 77. When a committee calling itself the National Salvation Front (NSF) was formed, it was full of former communists, though admittedly they were communists who wanted reform. Ion Iliescu, who had been a member of Ceausescu's government until 1984, was chosen as president. He won the presidential election of May 1990, and the NSF won the elections for a new parliament."

⚠️ The lesson generalises. ⚠️ Repression that is thorough enough to prevent any organised opposition from surviving does not protect the regime; it merely guarantees that when the regime falls there is nobody to hand power to except people from inside the regime. ⚠️ Violence at the moment of collapse and continuity of personnel afterwards are therefore two faces of the same fact, not a contradiction.

### 4. Bulgaria and Albania: managed continuity and delayed collapse

✅ Bulgaria: "the communist leader Todor Zhivkov had been in power since 1954. He had stubbornly refused all reforms, even when pressurized by Gorbachev. The progressive communists decided to get rid of him. The Politburo voted to remove him (December 1989) and in June 1990 free elections were held. The communists, now calling themselves the Bulgarian Socialist Party, won a comfortable victory over the main opposition party, the Union of Democratic Forces, probably because their propaganda machine told people that the introduction of capitalism would bring economic disaster."

⚠️ **One date has to be corrected before this case can be used.** ⚠️ The account places the Politburo vote in December 1989. ✅ Zhivkov was in fact removed on **10 November 1989**, the day after the Berlin Wall was breached. ⚠️ The correction changes the sequence rather than the argument, and the sequence is the interesting part: Bulgaria's managed exit begins **inside** the November wave rather than a month after it, so five different national outcomes were produced within weeks of one another from the same regional permission.

⚠️ Note the contrast with Hungary, where the same manoeuvre - remove the old leader, rename the party, hold free elections - produced a crushing defeat. ⚠️ Two differences are visible in the record: Hungary had two large, established opposition parties, and Bulgaria's ruling party retained a propaganda machine whose argument about the costs of capitalism was, on the evidence of the next few years, not obviously wrong. ⚠️ The account attaches "probably" to that explanation and so should any answer.

✅ Albania: "communist since 1945 when the communist resistance movement seized power and set up a republic; so, as with Yugoslavia, the Russians were not responsible for the introduction of communism." ✅ Under Enver Hoxha, "a great admirer of Stalin", and then Ramiz Alia, Albania "was still the poorest and most backward country in Europe". ✅ "During the winter of 1991 many young Albanians tried to escape from their poverty by crossing the Adriatic Sea to Italy, but most of them were sent back. By this time student demonstrations were breaking out, and statues of Hoxha and Lenin were overturned. Eventually the communist leadership bowed to the inevitable and allowed free elections. In 1992 the first non-communist president, Sali Berisha, was elected."

⚠️ Albania and Yugoslavia share a structural feature that is easy to miss and worth marks: ✅ in both, communism was installed by an indigenous resistance movement rather than by the Soviet army. ⚠️ Regimes that did not depend on Soviet installation were correspondingly less affected by the withdrawal of the Soviet guarantee - which is one reason both collapsed later and differently, and in Yugoslavia's case catastrophically.

### 5. What all of them faced the next morning

✅ "The states of eastern Europe faced broadly similar problems: how to change from a planned or 'command' economy to a free economy where 'market forces' ruled. Heavy industry, which in theory should have been privatized, was mostly old-fashioned and uncompetitive; it had now lost its guaranteed markets within the communist bloc, and so nobody wanted to buy shares in it. Although shops were better stocked than before, prices of consumer goods soared and very few people could afford to buy them. The standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism, and very little help was forthcoming from the west. Many people had expected a miraculous improvement, and, not making allowances for the seriousness of the problems, they soon grew disillusioned with their new governments."

✅ Four national outcomes are recorded: East Germans "were the most fortunate, having the wealth of the former West Germany to help them", though with resentment in both directions over transfers, taxes, interest rates and jobs; ✅ in Poland the first four years were hard, with "clear signs of recovery" by 1994, yet in December 1995 Walesa "was defeated by a former Communist Party member, Aleksander Kwasniewski"; ✅ in Czechoslovakia, Slovakia demanded independence, "for a time civil war seemed a strong possibility", and "a peaceful settlement was worked out and the country split into two - the Czech Republic and Slovakia (1992)"; ✅ "predictably, the slowest economic progress was made in Romania, Bulgaria and Albania, where the first half of the 1990s was beset by falling output and inflation."

⚠️ Two conclusions worth carrying to Lesson 20. ⚠️ First, the phrase "the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism" is the single most useful corrective to a triumphalist narrative, and it comes from the account itself. ⚠️ Second, the Czechoslovak separation of 1992 is the decisive counter-example to any claim that post-communist multinational states had to break up violently: the same kind of question that produced war in Yugoslavia produced an agreed separation here.

### Criticism and reply: is the two-variable grid too neat?

⚠️ **The objection.** Sorting five countries by "organised counterpart" and "divided ruling party" imposes order on a chaotic period, and any such grid will have exceptions. Albania does not fit it; East Germany's route was dominated by a factor - an adjacent co-national state - that the grid does not contain.

⚠️ **The reply.** The grid is offered as an **answer-writing device**, not as a causal law, and both objections are stated in the lesson itself rather than hidden: Albania is placed outside the grid, and East Germany's exit pressure is named as an additional variable. ⚠️ Its value is that it generates comparisons a narrative cannot: it makes Bulgaria and Hungary a genuine puzzle rather than two entries in a list, and it explains why Romanian violence and Romanian continuity of personnel are the same fact.

⚠️ **The residual.** A grid cannot explain timing. ⚠️ Why the wave ran from August 1988 to December 1989 rather than over five years is a question the grid does not answer, and the answer lies in the demonstration effect - each success shortening the next regime's deliberation - which is a sequential mechanism rather than a structural one.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The redrawal of national boundaries, and political philosophies and their effect on society: five contemporaneous transitions from the same starting system.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Why did communism collapse in eastern Europe?" is the standard stem; the differentiating material is the comparison, not the narrative.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that 1989 was a uniformly peaceful revolution. ✅ Romania's was "short and bloody" and the army initially fired on the crowds.
2. ⚠️ Giving casualty numbers for Timisoara or Bucharest. ✅ The account says "many people were killed" and "many were killed and wounded"; use those words.
3. ⚠️ Assuming the communists always lost the first free election. ✅ In Bulgaria the renamed Bulgarian Socialist Party won in June 1990.
4. ⚠️ Treating Albania and Yugoslavia as Soviet impositions. ✅ In both, communism was installed by an indigenous resistance movement in 1945.
5. ⚠️ Claiming living standards rose immediately after 1989. ✅ The record says the standard of living was even lower than in the final years of communism.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting Czechoslovakia's peaceful separation of 1992. ⚠️ It is the strongest available counter-example to Yugoslav inevitability.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ June 1989: Gorbachev visited Kohl in Bonn seeking West German financial help and promised to help end the division of Europe - "in effect he was secretly promising freedom for East Germany".
2. ✅ August-September 1989: thousands of East Germans escaped west via Poland, Czechoslovakia and Hungary after Hungary opened its frontier with Austria.
3. ✅ New Forum grew around the Protestant Church; October 1989 saw demonstrations across East Germany.
4. ✅ Honecker wanted the army to fire; colleagues refused; he was dropped; Krenz made concessions; the Berlin Wall was breached on 9 November 1989 and free elections promised.
5. ✅ Czechoslovakia: Husak resigned in 1987, Jakes was no reformer; 17 November 1989 Prague demonstration and police brutality; Charter 77 under Havel; Dubcek spoke publicly for the first time since 1968; a national strike; Jakes resigned; Havel elected president 29 December 1989.
6. ✅ Romania: Ceausescu leader since 1965; the Securitate "responsible for many deaths"; Timisoara demonstration for a harassed priest put down on 17 December 1989 with many killed; the Bucharest rally four days later collapsed into "murderers of Timisoara"; the army first fired then refused; the Ceausescus were arrested, tried by a military tribunal and shot on 25 December 1989.
7. ✅ Romania had "no equivalent of the Polish Solidarity and Czech Charter 77"; the National Salvation Front was full of former communists who wanted reform; Ion Iliescu, in Ceausescu's government until 1984, won the presidency in May 1990.
8. ✅ Bulgaria: Zhivkov in power since 1954, refused all reform even under Gorbachev's pressure; the Politburo voted him out on **10 November 1989**, the day after the Berlin Wall was breached (the account's wording gives December 1989); the renamed Bulgarian Socialist Party won free elections in June 1990 over the Union of Democratic Forces.
9. ✅ Albania: communist since 1945 by its own resistance movement; Hoxha, an admirer of Stalin, then Alia; poorest and most backward in Europe; escapes across the Adriatic in the winter of 1991; Sali Berisha elected first non-communist president in 1992.
10. ✅ After communism: uncompetitive heavy industry with no guaranteed bloc markets; better-stocked shops at unaffordable prices; a standard of living lower than in the final years of communism; little western help; rapid disillusion.
11. ✅ East Germany subsidised by former West Germany with resentment both ways; Poland recovering by 1994 but electing a former communist in December 1995; Czechoslovakia separating peacefully in 1992; Romania, Bulgaria and Albania slowest.
12. ⚠️ Two sorting variables: an organised opposition counterpart, and a divided or reform-minded ruling party. Their combinations explain negotiated exit, elite collapse, managed continuity and violence.

### Concept check

**Question:** Romania was both the most violently repressive regime and the one where former communists returned to office most quickly. Show why these two facts are connected rather than contradictory.

**Model answer:** They are connected through the effect of thorough repression on the opposition. The account records that Romania had never had democratic government and that opposition had been so ruthlessly crushed that there was no equivalent of the Polish Solidarity or the Czech Charter 77. That has two consequences. First, because no organised counterpart existed, there was nobody with whom the regime could have negotiated an exit even if it had wished to, so the only available route was confrontation: the Timisoara demonstration of 17 December 1989 was brutally put down with many killed, the army initially fired on the Bucharest crowds, and the Ceausescus were arrested, tried by a military tribunal and shot on 25 December 1989. Second, because no organised counterpart existed, there was nobody to whom power could be transferred afterwards, so the National Salvation Front that formed was full of former communists - reform-minded ones, but insiders - and Ion Iliescu, a member of Ceausescu's government until 1984, won the presidency in May 1990. Violence at the moment of collapse and continuity of personnel afterwards are therefore two effects of a single cause.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that 1989 shows repression always fails. The comparison in this session shows something narrower and more useful: repression succeeded for decades where the external guarantee held, as Polish martial law in 1981 demonstrates, and it failed in 1989 when that guarantee had gone. What thorough repression does reliably determine is not whether a regime survives but what exists to replace it, and Romania is the demonstration.

## Lesson 9 - German reunification: a revolution completed by diplomacy

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the breach of the Wall settled nothing legally; the March 1990 East German election and the pressure for immediate union; Soviet and American agreement and the withdrawal timetable to 1994; French and British reluctance; the Two Plus Four settlement of 12 September 1990 and its entry into force; formal reunification at midnight on 3 October 1990; the December 1990 all-German election; and the problems reunification created for its neighbours

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on Germany reunited within the collapse of communism in eastern Europe, together with its later list of the problems the reunification created, including Polish suspicion about the Oder-Neisse territories and the refugee inflow of 1992
CA search: "National Museum of American Diplomacy Berlin Wall Signature Segment opening date"
CA found: United States Department of State, National Museum of American Diplomacy, official museum page - the museum states that it will open to the public in October 2026, and that it received the "Signature Segment" of the Berlin Wall in October 2015
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four stages, four different kinds of act

```text
  STAGE 1  POPULAR           9 November 1989
           ACT               The Berlin Wall is breached.
                             WHAT IT CHANGED: enforced separation.
                             WHAT IT DID NOT CHANGE: borders,
                             sovereignty, alliance membership,
                             the legal rights of the four powers.

  STAGE 2  ELECTORAL         March 1990
           MANDATE           West German parties move into the East;
                             Kohl stages an election tour; the East
                             German CDU wins "an overwhelming
                             victory"; de Maiziere becomes prime
                             minister hoping for GRADUAL union -
                             but "nearly everybody in East Germany
                             seemed to want immediate union".

  STAGE 3  GREAT-POWER       1990
           CONSENT           "The USSR and the USA agreed that
                             reunification could take place;
                             Gorbachev promised that all Russian
                             troops would be withdrawn from East
                             Germany by 1994. France and Britain,
                             who were less happy about German
                             reunification, felt bound to go along
                             with the flow."
                             Signed as the Two Plus Four settlement
                             in Moscow, 12 September 1990.

  STAGE 4  LEGAL ACT         3 October 1990
                             "Germany was formally reunited at
                             midnight on 3 October 1990."
                             December 1990: all-German elections;
                             CDU/CSU with FDP win a comfortable
                             majority over the SPD; the renamed
                             communists (PDS) win 17 of 662 seats;
                             Kohl becomes the first Chancellor of
                             all Germany since the Second World War.
```

⚠️ Read the four stage labels downward - popular, electoral, great-power, legal - and you have the argument that answers every question on this subtopic: a revolution that opened the question, and a diplomacy that closed it.

### 1. The learner's problem: what was left to settle after 9 November 1989?

⚠️ A great deal, and naming it precisely is where the marks are.

⚠️ Germany in 1989 was not simply divided; it was **legally encumbered**. The four wartime allies retained rights and responsibilities with respect to Germany as a whole and to Berlin, arising from the wartime and postwar settlements. ⚠️ Those rights did not lapse because a crowd crossed a checkpoint. ⚠️ Three questions therefore remained open even after the Wall was breached:

1. ⚠️ **Borders** - in particular the eastern frontier on the Oder and Neisse, about which, as the account records, "the Poles were very suspicious of a united and powerful Germany, fearing that it might try to take back the former German territory east of the rivers Oder and Neisse, given to Poland after the Second World War."
2. ⚠️ **Sovereignty** - whether a united Germany would possess full authority over its own external affairs, or continue to be encumbered by four-power rights.
3. ⚠️ **Alliance status and forces** - which alliance a united Germany would belong to, and what would happen to the Soviet troops stationed on East German soil.

⚠️ None of those can be settled by a demonstration. ⚠️ Each requires the consent of states, expressed in an instrument. ⚠️ That is why this topic's formula is: the street opened the question and the treaty closed it.

### 2. The electoral stage, and why it accelerated everything

✅ "When the great powers began to drop hints that they would not stand in the way of a reunited Germany, the West German political parties moved into the East. Chancellor Kohl staged an election tour, and the East German version of his party (CDU) won an overwhelming victory (March 1990). The East German CDU leader, Lothar de Maiziere, became prime minister. He was hoping for gradual moves towards reunification, but again the pressure of 'people power' carried all before it. Nearly everybody in East Germany seemed to want immediate union."

⚠️ Two mechanisms are worth naming.

- ⚠️ **The election converted a demand into a mandate.** Before March 1990, "the East Germans want union" was an assertion about crowds. After it, it was an electoral result, and negotiators on every side had to treat it as a fact about the population rather than about the street.
- ⚠️ **Popular pressure overrode the preferred pace of its own elected government.** ✅ De Maiziere wanted gradual moves and did not get them. ⚠️ This is a useful corrective to the idea that reunification was managed from above: the chosen leadership of the East was pushed by its own electorate.

### 3. The great-power stage: consent, reluctance, and the withdrawal timetable

✅ "The USSR and the USA agreed that reunification could take place; Gorbachev promised that all Russian troops would be withdrawn from East Germany by 1994. France and Britain, who were less happy about German reunification, felt bound to go along with the flow."

⚠️ Three things to extract.

1. ⚠️ **Soviet consent was the indispensable item**, and it was not free. ✅ Lesson 8 records the June 1989 Bonn visit in which Gorbachev sought West German financial help and "in effect he was secretly promising freedom for East Germany". ⚠️ The economics of the Soviet crisis and the politics of German unity are therefore linked, and saying so is a strong analytical move.
2. ⚠️ **French and British reluctance is recorded and should not be airbrushed.** ⚠️ A larger and richer Germany at the centre of Europe was the problem that European integration had been designed to manage since 1951, and Lesson 18 shows how integration was used to answer it again: ✅ the French, Dutch and Belgian governments supported the Maastricht Treaty strongly "because they thought it was the best way to make sure that the power of the reunified Germany was contained and controlled within the Community."
3. ⚠️ **A dated withdrawal timetable converts consent into verification.** ⚠️ "All Russian troops withdrawn by 1994" is the kind of commitment whose performance can be checked, which is what distinguishes a settlement from a declaration.

### 4. The instrument and the legal act

✅ The settlement was signed on **12 September 1990** by the two German states and the four former occupying powers; ⚠️ its formal title is the Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany, it was signed at Moscow, and it entered into force on 15 March 1991. ⚠️ It is universally referred to by the shorthand **Two Plus Four**, from its signatories: the two Germanies and the four powers.

✅ Legal reunification followed: "Germany was formally reunited at midnight on 3 October 1990."

✅ And the political consolidation: "In elections for the whole of Germany (December 1990) the conservative CDU/CSU alliance, together with their liberal FDP supporters, won a comfortable majority over the socialist SDP. The communists (renamed the Party of Democratic Socialism - PDS) won only 17 of the 662 seats in the Bundestag (lower house of parliament). Helmut Kohl became the first Chancellor of all Germany since the Second World War."

⚠️ Note the chronological trap the dates set. ⚠️ The treaty was signed **before** reunification took legal effect, and entered into force **after** it. ⚠️ A question that asks which came first - the treaty or reunification - is testing exactly this, and the correct answer distinguishes signature, legal unification and entry into force as three separate moments.

### 5. What reunification then created

⚠️ The account is unusually careful to record that the settlement produced problems rather than closing the file, and this is the part most answers omit.

| ✅ Problem recorded | ⚠️ Why it belongs in an answer |
|---|---|
| ✅ Polish suspicion of a united and powerful Germany over the Oder-Neisse territories | ⚠️ Shows that boundary settlement is a live issue rather than a formality, which is precisely the syllabus's "redrawal of national boundaries" clause |
| ✅ Germany "found itself providing refuge for people fleeing from disturbances in other states of Europe; by October 1992, at least 16 000 refugees a month were entering Germany" | ⚠️ The end of the Cold War generated population movement, and the Yugoslav wars of Lesson 16 are the principal source |
| ✅ "Violent protests from right-wing neo-Nazi groups who believed that Germany had problems enough of its own - especially the need to modernize the industry and amenities of the former East Germany - without admitting foreigners" | ⚠️ Demonstrates that liberal outcomes were contested from the far right inside the West as well, which is a direct corrective to convergence narratives |
| ✅ East Germans "were the most fortunate, having the wealth of the former West Germany to help them", yet West Germans resented the transfers, higher taxes and higher interest rates while easterners resented westerners taking the best jobs | ⚠️ Even the best-resourced transition in the region produced mutual resentment; this is the strongest single case against assuming that resources alone solve transition |

📰 **Current linkage, used once in this session and limited here.** ⚠️ The Berlin Wall's place in public memory remains institutionally curated rather than settled. ✅ The United States Department of State's National Museum of American Diplomacy states on its official page that it received the "Signature Segment" of the Berlin Wall in October 2015 and that the museum "will open to the public in October 2026" to showcase its permanent inaugural exhibitions. ⚠️ That is evidence about a museum's stated plans and holdings on the date consulted, and nothing more: ❌ it is not evidence for any causal claim about 1989-90, and ❌ no other current item is used anywhere in this session.

### Comparative note: 1871 and 1990 are not the same word

⚠️ This trap is worth flagging explicitly, and it is worth a sentence in any answer that risks it.

| | ✅ Nineteenth-century unification | ✅ Reunification, 1990 |
|---|---|---|
| Process | States assembled under Prussian leadership through war and diplomacy | Two existing German states merged after a popular movement and a four-power settlement |
| Instrument | Treaties following military victories | The Two Plus Four settlement of 12 September 1990 and a legal act on 3 October 1990 |
| External role | Achieved partly against the wishes of neighbouring powers | Required the explicit consent of the four former occupying powers, two of them reluctant |
| Analytical point | ⚠️ Unification created a new great power and altered the European balance | ⚠️ Reunification restored full sovereignty to an existing state within an existing alliance and integration framework, and the framework was then deepened partly in order to contain it |

⚠️ The two cases share only a name. ⚠️ Writing "Germany was unified in 1990 as it had been in 1871" is a category error that a well-prepared examiner will notice immediately.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The redrawal of national boundaries after 1945 and after 1991: German reunification is one of the three principal cases, with Soviet and Yugoslav succession.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Explain how German reunification combined popular revolution and great-power diplomacy" is the standard stem, and the four-stage visual is the answer's skeleton.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the Berlin Wall's opening reunified Germany. ✅ Three further stages followed: an election in March 1990, a settlement on 12 September 1990 and a legal act on 3 October 1990.
2. ⚠️ Confusing nineteenth-century unification with 1990 reunification. ⚠️ Different processes, instruments, external roles and consequences.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the Soviet consent and the withdrawal timetable to 1994. ⚠️ Consent was the indispensable and non-free item.
4. ⚠️ Presenting reunification as smooth. ✅ Polish suspicion over the Oder-Neisse line, at least 16,000 refugees a month entering Germany by October 1992, neo-Nazi violence and mutual east-west resentment are all in the record.
5. ⚠️ Confusing the three dates of the settlement. ⚠️ Signature 12 September 1990; legal reunification 3 October 1990; entry into force 15 March 1991.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 9 November 1989: the Berlin Wall was breached; free elections were promised. It ended enforced separation and settled nothing legal.
2. ⚠️ Three open questions after the breach: borders, sovereignty, alliance status and foreign forces.
3. ✅ March 1990: West German parties moved into the East; Kohl toured; the East German CDU won overwhelmingly; de Maiziere became prime minister wanting gradual union; the population wanted immediate union.
4. ✅ The USSR and the USA agreed to reunification; Gorbachev promised all Russian troops out of East Germany by 1994; France and Britain were less happy and went along with the flow.
5. ⚠️ The Two Plus Four settlement - formally the Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany - was signed at Moscow on 12 September 1990 by the two German states and the four former occupying powers, and entered into force on 15 March 1991.
6. ✅ "Germany was formally reunited at midnight on 3 October 1990."
7. ✅ December 1990: the CDU/CSU with the FDP won a comfortable majority over the SPD; the renamed communists (PDS) took 17 of 662 Bundestag seats; Kohl became the first Chancellor of all Germany since the Second World War.
8. ✅ Consequences: Polish suspicion over the Oder-Neisse territories; at least 16,000 refugees a month entering Germany by October 1992; neo-Nazi violence; and resentment in both directions over transfers, taxes, interest rates and jobs.
9. ⚠️ Maastricht was supported by France, the Netherlands and Belgium partly as the best way to contain the reunified Germany's power inside the Community.
10. ⚠️ 1871 and 1990 share a word and nothing else: different processes, instruments, external roles and consequences.
11. 📰 One dated current item is used in this session, in this lesson, and is limited to what the museum's own page states.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that "Germany was reunified when the Berlin Wall fell". List what still had to be settled after 9 November 1989, and identify the instrument and the act that settled it.

**Model answer:** Three things remained unsettled. First, borders - in particular the eastern frontier on the Oder and Neisse, about which the account records that the Poles were very suspicious of a united and powerful Germany and feared it might try to take back the former German territory given to Poland after the Second World War. Second, sovereignty, because the four wartime allies retained rights with respect to Germany as a whole that no demonstration could extinguish. Third, alliance status and the presence of foreign forces, which is why Gorbachev's promise that all Russian troops would be withdrawn from East Germany by 1994 mattered. These were settled by the Two Plus Four treaty, formally the Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany, signed at Moscow on 12 September 1990 by the two German states and the four former occupying powers and in force from 15 March 1991, and then by the legal act of reunification at midnight on 3 October 1990. Between the breach and the settlement stood a fourth stage that is often omitted: the March 1990 East German election, which converted a popular demand into an electoral mandate that even the East's own new prime minister could not slow down.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the diplomacy as a formality that merely registered what the street had already decided. The reluctance of two of the four powers is recorded, Soviet consent was obtained in a context in which Gorbachev was seeking West German financial help, and France, the Netherlands and Belgium then supported Maastricht partly in order to contain the reunified Germany's power inside the Community. Popular pressure made the question unavoidable; it did not determine the answer, and the answer had a price.

---

## Lesson 10 - The institutional termination, 1990-91: how a rivalry is formally ended

Progress: 10/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the November 1990 declaration that NATO and Warsaw Pact states were no longer adversaries and the instruments that carried it; the Charter of Paris; the dissolution of Comecon and of the Warsaw Treaty Organisation in 1991; the four new problems the account says surfaced immediately; and why formal termination is a distinct analytical category from collapse

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on how international relations were affected by the collapse of communism, which records the November 1990 treaty, the statement that the Cold War was over, the allocation of credit between Gorbachev and Reagan, and the six new problems that followed
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the dismantling of a bloc system, instrument by instrument

```text
   1987  INF Treaty            436 US + 1,575 Soviet warheads
         ------------------->  to be destroyed; strict verification;
                               ALL Russian missiles in East Germany
                               and Czechoslovakia removed
                                        |
   Dec   Malta meeting                   |  Bush and Gorbachev,
   1989  ------------------->            |  2-3 December 1989:
                                         |  public acceptance that
                                         |  the confrontation is over
                                         v
   Nov   THE TERMINATION       "In November 1990 the countries of
   1990  ------------------->  NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a
                               treaty agreeing that they were 'no
                               longer adversaries', and that none of
                               their weapons would ever be used
                               except in self-defence.
                               The Cold War was over."
                               Carried by the Paris instruments of
                               19-21 November 1990: a conventional
                               forces treaty, a joint declaration of
                               twenty-two states, and a charter for
                               a new Europe
                                        |
   Jun   Comecon dissolved              |  28 June 1991: the economic
   1991  ------------------->           |  architecture of the bloc ends
                                        v
   Jul   Warsaw Pact dissolved   1 July 1991: the military
   1991  ------------------->    architecture of the bloc ends
                                        |
   Dec   THE STATE ENDS                 v  Republican decisions; the
   1991                                    USSR dissolves; Gorbachev
                                           resigns on 25 December

   NOTE THE ORDER: the RIVALRY is terminated by agreement BEFORE
   the bloc's institutions are dissolved, and both BEFORE the
   Soviet state ends. Three separate terminations, thirteen months.
```

### 1. The learner's problem: rivalries do not simply stop

⚠️ Students routinely write that the Cold War "ended" in 1989 or 1991 without asking what ending consists of. ⚠️ A rivalry between alliance systems is a structure of expectations: each side plans, arms, deploys and allies on the assumption that the other is a potential enemy. ⚠️ Ending it therefore requires that the expectation be publicly and mutually withdrawn, and that the machinery built on the expectation be dismantled.

⚠️ That is why this short lesson exists. ⚠️ It teaches four kinds of act - a declaration of non-hostility, a verifiable reduction of forces, a normative charter, and the dissolution of the alliance institutions - and shows that all four were performed within about twenty months.

### 2. The November 1990 termination and the instruments that carried it

✅ The account's sentence is the anchor: "In November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were 'no longer adversaries', and that none of their weapons would ever be used except in self-defence. The Cold War was over."

⚠️ The account does not name the instruments, and the standard names are worth having because questions use them.

| ⚠️ Instrument | ⚠️ Date | ⚠️ What it did |
|---|---|---|
| Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe | 19 November 1990 | Set equal ceilings on major categories of conventional equipment between the Atlantic and the Urals for the two groups of states, attacking the capacity for surprise attack rather than merely the numbers |
| Joint Declaration of Twenty-Two States | 19 November 1990 | The signatories of that treaty declared that they were "no longer adversaries" and would not use their weapons except in self-defence - the words the account quotes |
| Charter of Paris for a New Europe | 21 November 1990 | Adopted by the participating states of the European security conference; declared that the era of confrontation and division of Europe had ended and committed the signatories to democracy, human rights and cooperative security |

⚠️ The three together are the reason November 1990 is the defensible date for the end of the rivalry. ⚠️ A declaration alone is rhetoric; a force treaty alone is arms control; a charter alone is aspiration. ⚠️ The combination is a public, verifiable, normatively framed withdrawal of the expectation of hostility - which is what ending a rivalry actually means.

✅ Note also which alliance the account says signed: "the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact". ⚠️ The Warsaw Pact still existed in November 1990 and still had members whose communist governments had already fallen. ⚠️ An alliance signing away its own reason to exist is a strong image and a defensible sentence.

### 3. Dissolving the architecture

⚠️ The bloc had two institutional pillars, and both were wound up in the summer of 1991 - after the rivalry was declared over and before the Soviet state ended.

- ⚠️ The **Council for Mutual Economic Assistance**, the bloc's economic organisation, was dissolved on 28 June 1991. ✅ Its significance is visible in the account's description of the aftermath: eastern European heavy industry "had now lost its guaranteed markets within the communist bloc, and so nobody wanted to buy shares in it". ⚠️ Dissolving the economic bloc removed the demand side of the region's industry at the same moment as its supply side was being privatised.
- ⚠️ The **Warsaw Treaty Organisation** was dissolved on 1 July 1991. ⚠️ Its dissolution left NATO as the only surviving alliance system in Europe, which is the structural fact behind the enlargement question of Lesson 19.

⚠️ For the boundary: ✅ the origins and operation of both institutions are studied as part of the Cold War itself; this session uses only their termination as part of the end phase.

### 4. Why "the Cold War was over" is not the same as "the problems were over"

⚠️ The account is emphatic on this point, and it is the transition to the second half of the session: "Many people in the west thought that with the collapse of communism in eastern Europe, the world's problems would miraculously disappear. But nothing could have been further from the truth and a range of new problems surfaced."

✅ Six are recorded, and four belong to this topic.

1. ✅ **New conflicts caused by nationalism.** "During the Cold War, the USSR and the USA ... kept tight control, by force if necessary, over areas where their vital interests might be affected. Now, a conflict which did not directly affect the interests of East or West would probably be left to find its own solution, bloody or otherwise." ✅ The instances named are Czechoslovakia's peaceful separation, war between Azerbaijan and Armenia, fighting in Georgia, and "most tragic of all", Yugoslavia. ⚠️ The mechanism is important: bipolar discipline had suppressed some conflicts; its removal did not create them but removed the suppression, and also removed the incentive for outside powers to care.
2. ✅ **Supervision of nuclear, biological and chemical weapons**, "now that the Russians and the USA were less willing to act as 'policemen'". ⚠️ Note the account's careful formulation - "countries with what the powers considered to be unstable or irresponsible governments" - which attributes the judgement rather than endorsing it, and should be reproduced in that form.
3. ✅ **Economic problems** in all former communist states, needing "a carefully planned and generous programme of financial help from the West", failing which "nationalism and economic unrest could cause a right-wing backlash, especially in Russia itself, which could be just as threatening as communism was once thought to be". ⚠️ This is a recorded contemporary anxiety, not a retrospective claim, and it is one of the account's sharpest sentences.
4. ✅ **Relations between the western allies.** "They had been held together by the need to stand firm against communism, but now differences emerged over trade and the extent to which the USA and Japan were prepared to help solve the problems of eastern Europe. For instance, during the war in Bosnia, relations between the USA and the states of western Europe became strained when the USA refused to provide troops for the UN peacekeeping forces, leaving the burden to other member states."

✅ And the sentence that opens the second half of this session: "The overriding fact now was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower; it remained to be seen how Washington would choose to play its new role on the world stage."

⚠️ The two German items in the account's list of six were taken in Lesson 9, where their consequences fall.

### Doubt and resolution: "if the rivalry ended in November 1990, why do we say the Cold War ended in 1991?"

⚠️ Both dates are defensible, and the difference is a difference of definition rather than of fact.

⚠️ If the Cold War is defined as **an adversarial relationship between two alliance systems**, it ended when the parties said so and dismantled the machinery: November 1990, completed by the dissolutions of June and July 1991.

⚠️ If it is defined as **a bipolar structure of world politics**, it ended when one of the two poles ceased to exist: December 1991.

⚠️ If it is defined as **an ideological contest between systems**, it ended unevenly, since communist party-states continued to exist elsewhere, which is precisely why the China comparison cannot be avoided.

⚠️ The examiner-proof formulation is to state the definition you are using in the first sentence and then be consistent. ⚠️ Candidates lose marks not for choosing a date but for using two definitions in one answer without noticing.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ International institutions, their structure and mandate, in the Paper II sense; ✅ and world history's transformation of international relations in the Paper I sense.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ The account's own end-of-chapter question is exactly this: "Explain why the Cold War came to an end, and show how this affected international relations." The second clause is this lesson.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the end of the Cold War as a single dated event without stating a definition. ⚠️ Three defensible dates exist for three different definitions.
2. ⚠️ Writing that the Warsaw Pact ended in 1989. ⚠️ It signed the November 1990 declaration and was dissolved on 1 July 1991; Comecon was dissolved on 28 June 1991.
3. ⚠️ Claiming that the end of bipolarity created the new conflicts. ✅ The account's mechanism is the removal of suppression and of outside interest, not creation.
4. ⚠️ Endorsing the "unstable or irresponsible governments" judgement in your own voice. ✅ The account attributes it to what "the powers considered"; keep the attribution.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting that western allied unity was itself a casualty. ✅ Trade differences and the American refusal to provide troops for the Bosnian peacekeeping force are recorded.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ November 1990: NATO and Warsaw Pact countries signed a treaty agreeing that they were "no longer adversaries" and that none of their weapons would be used except in self-defence; "The Cold War was over."
2. ⚠️ The instruments: the Conventional Armed Forces in Europe treaty and the Joint Declaration of Twenty-Two States on 19 November 1990, and the Charter of Paris for a New Europe on 21 November 1990.
3. ⚠️ Comecon dissolved 28 June 1991; the Warsaw Treaty Organisation dissolved 1 July 1991 - both after the rivalry was declared over and before the Soviet state ended.
4. ✅ Credit is distributed: Gorbachev's determination to work for disarmament broke the stalemate and impressed Reagan, "who also deserves much credit for responding so positively".
5. ✅ The expectation that problems would disappear was wrong; new ones surfaced immediately.
6. ✅ Nationalism-driven conflicts: Czechoslovakia peacefully, Azerbaijan-Armenia by war, Georgia, and "most tragic of all" Yugoslavia, which broke into five states - Serbia with Montenegro, Bosnia-Herzegovina, Croatia, Slovenia and Macedonia.
7. ✅ Weapons supervision became a concern as the superpowers became less willing to act as policemen; the judgement about which governments were unstable is attributed to the powers.
8. ✅ Former communist states needed planned and generous western help, failing which nationalism and economic unrest might produce a right-wing backlash "especially in Russia itself".
9. ✅ Western allied unity weakened: trade disputes, and strain when the USA refused troops for the Bosnian peacekeeping force.
10. ✅ "The overriding fact now was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower."
11. ⚠️ Three definitions, three defensible dates: alliance rivalry ends November 1990; bipolar structure ends December 1991; ideological contest ends unevenly and not everywhere.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is November 1990 a defensible date for the end of the Cold War even though the Soviet Union still existed, and what four kinds of act had to be performed for the termination to be real?

**Model answer:** It is defensible because the Cold War, understood as an adversarial relationship between two alliance systems, is a structure of mutual expectation, and in November 1990 that expectation was publicly and mutually withdrawn by the parties themselves: the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were no longer adversaries and that none of their weapons would be used except in self-defence. Four kinds of act were required and all were performed within about twenty months. First, a verifiable reduction of forces - the conventional armed forces treaty of 19 November 1990 set equal ceilings between the Atlantic and the Urals, following the 1987 treaty that had already destroyed warheads under strict verification. Second, a declaration of non-hostility - the joint declaration of twenty-two states on the same day. Third, a normative framework - the Charter of Paris for a New Europe on 21 November 1990, declaring the era of confrontation and division ended. Fourth, dissolution of the machinery built on the expectation - Comecon on 28 June 1991 and the Warsaw Treaty Organisation on 1 July 1991. The survival of the Soviet state for a further thirteen months is irrelevant on this definition, because a state can exist without being an adversary.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the rivalry was terminated the problems it had suppressed were also solved. The account is explicit that the opposite happened: conflicts previously held down by superpower discipline were now likely to be left to find their own solution, bloody or otherwise, and it names Azerbaijan and Armenia, Georgia and above all Yugoslavia. The end of a rivalry removes a suppressant, not a cause.

---

## Lesson 11 - Soviet dissolution, 1990-91: why a state ended and not merely a government

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: republican sovereignty claims and the Baltic declarations; Yeltsin's Russian presidency and dual power; the failed coup of 19-21 August 1991 and its effect on party and union authority; the republican decisions of December 1991 and the Commonwealth of Independent States; Gorbachev's resignation on 25 December 1991; and why coercive restoration destroyed the authority it was meant to save

                              |

        A self-declared State Committee on the State of
        Emergency detains Gorbachev and claims power
                              |
