```text
        INTENDED RESULT                 ACTUAL RESULT
        Union and party                 - The coup fails in three days
        authority restored              - Resistance is led from
                                          Authority B, not Authority A
                                        - The Party is discredited as
                                          the instrument of the attempt
                                        - Republican exits accelerate
                              |
                              v
        DECEMBER 1991: republican leaders agree the union has
        ceased to exist and create a Commonwealth of Independent
        States (8 December); eight more republics join at
        Alma-Ata (21 December); Gorbachev resigns 25 December;
        the union is formally wound up the following day
```

### 1. The learner's problem: why is this not just "the USSR broke up"?

⚠️ Because the phrase hides the mechanism, and the mechanism is the answer.

⚠️ A state can end in several ways: conquest, partition by outside powers, or dissolution by its own constituent units. ⚠️ This was the third, and the third requires three conditions that this session has already established separately.

```text
  CONDITION 1  Units that can act as states
               -> 15 republics with territories, parliaments and
                  administrations (Lesson 3)
  CONDITION 2  A centre that has lost the capacity or the will to
               prevent exit
               -> economic failure, legitimacy loss and the
                  non-coercion choice (Lessons 2, 5, 6)
  CONDITION 3  A rival authority that the units can rally to
               -> Yeltsin's Russian presidency from May 1990
                  (Lessons 3 and 6)
  PLUS A TRIGGER that destroys the centre's remaining authority
               -> the failed coup of August 1991
```

⚠️ Write those four and the question is answered. ⚠️ Write "nationalism plus economic failure" and it is not.

### 2. Dual power, and why it was structurally unstable

⚠️ Two authorities in one capital, each with a claim the other cannot dismiss, is an arrangement that resolves itself. ⚠️ Gorbachev's authority ran through the Party and the union institutions; ✅ Yeltsin's ran through his election as president of the parliament of the Russian republic in May 1990, after the conservatives had excluded him from the new Supreme Soviet.

⚠️ Three features made the Russian republic a uniquely dangerous rival platform.

1. ⚠️ **Size.** The Russian republic was by far the largest of the fifteen, so a union without it was not a union.
2. ⚠️ **Location.** ✅ Its parliament sat in Moscow, "also the meeting place for the federal Supreme Soviet and Congress of People's Deputies". ⚠️ The rival authority was not in a distant capital; it was down the road.
3. ⚠️ **Programme.** ✅ Yeltsin held that the union should be voluntary and that any republic wanting to opt out "should be allowed to do so", that the Party "no longer deserved its privileged position in the state", and that the economy needed "a rapid changeover to a market economy". ⚠️ Every one of those positions gave other republics something to align with.

⚠️ The result is the classic situation in which the formal centre retains the offices and the rival retains the initiative.

### 3. The coup of August 1991 and the logic of failed restoration

⚠️ The outcome is best stated in these terms: the failed hard-line coup of August 1991 destroyed much of the remaining legitimacy of the party and accelerated republican exits; ✅ "coercive restoration destroyed the authority it sought to save."

⚠️ The standard facts, held as part of the declared connecting set: the attempt ran from 19 to 21 August 1991; the participants organised themselves as a self-declared State Committee on the State of Emergency; Gorbachev was detained away from Moscow; and resistance was organised from the Russian republic's leadership.

⚠️ The analytical structure is what matters, and it generalises far beyond this case.

| ⚠️ What a coup must do | ⚠️ What this one did instead |
|---|---|
| Demonstrate that the old centre can still compel obedience | Demonstrated that it could not, within three days and in public |
| Keep the reforming leader as a figure to be either used or replaced | Removed him from the scene at the exact moment when only he could have defended the union's institutions |
| Leave the opposition without a stage | Gave the opposition the most favourable stage imaginable - defence of legality against an unconstitutional seizure |
| Preserve the credibility of the institution acting | Identified the Party and the union's security organs with an illegal attempt, discrediting both |

⚠️ The general principle to carry into any answer on authoritarian breakdown: **an attempted restoration that fails is worse for the old order than no attempt at all**, because it converts a contested question - can the centre still compel? - into a demonstrated answer, in public, and it transfers the vocabulary of legality to the other side.

⚠️ There is a second consequence specific to a federation. ⚠️ Republican leaderships that had been weighing the risk of exit had just watched the centre attempt force and fail. ⚠️ The perceived cost of exit collapsed, and the perceived cost of remaining - subordination to a centre capable of such attempts - rose. ⚠️ Both movements pointed the same way.

### 4. December 1991: the republican decisions

⚠️ The dissolution was accomplished by agreements among republican leaderships rather than by any union institution, and that is its constitutional significance.

⚠️ The standard sequence, held as part of the declared connecting set: on 8 December 1991 the leaders of the Russian, Ukrainian and Belarusian republics signed an agreement in the Belovezh forest stating that the union had ceased to exist and establishing a Commonwealth of Independent States; on 21 December 1991 eight further republics joined by protocol at Alma-Ata; ✅ Gorbachev resigned on 25 December 1991; and the union was formally wound up by its own legislature the following day.

⚠️ Three observations.

1. ⚠️ **The act was horizontal, not vertical.** No superior authority dissolved the union; its constituent republics agreed that it had ceased to exist. ⚠️ That is why the correct verb is "dissolved" rather than "was abolished".
2. ⚠️ **The Baltic states are the exception that clarifies the rule.** ✅ Lithuania had declared independence in March 1990 and Latvia and Estonia had followed more gradually; their claim rested on the illegitimacy of the 1940 incorporation, and they did not join the new commonwealth. ⚠️ Their route was restoration of a prior statehood, not secession from a valid union - a distinction that is itself examinable.
3. ⚠️ **Fifteen states, not one renamed state.** ✅ The account's own summary is that "the USSR itself had split up into separate republics and Gorbachev had resigned. Communist rule in Russia was over after 74 years." ⚠️ Russia was the largest successor, not the continuation of the whole.

### 5. What ended on 25 December 1991, and what did not

⚠️ Four things are commonly merged in candidates' answers, and separating them is quick marks.

```text
  ENDED IN DECEMBER 1991
  - the union state
  - the union presidency (Gorbachev's resignation, 25 December)
  - communist party rule in Russia, after 74 years

  DID NOT END IN DECEMBER 1991
  - the economic crisis: it worsened, and Lesson 12 records
    what happened from January 1992
  - the territorial questions: Nagorno-Karabakh, Georgia and
    others were already violent and remained so
  - nuclear weapons and their custody: the account records
    western anxiety about this precisely because the weapons
    outlived the state that built them
  - communist party-states elsewhere in the world
```

⚠️ The last two are the strongest single correctives to a triumphalist reading, and both are recorded in the account rather than inferred.

### Criticism and reply: could the union have been saved?

⚠️ **The objection.** The account's own end-of-chapter questions ask both whether different policies could have saved the USSR "in the same way that communism survived in China", and whether it was "Gorbachev's reluctance to commit himself to sufficiently radical changes" that led to the break-up. ⚠️ Both presuppose that survival was available.

⚠️ **The case for availability.** ⚠️ A voluntary union on Yeltsin's terms was on the table before August 1991; several republics had interests in a common market and a shared defence; and the coup, which destroyed the remaining possibility, was not inevitable. ⚠️ The Chinese case shows that a party-state facing comparable pressures survived by taking a different decision about force in June 1989.

⚠️ **The case against.** ⚠️ Three things had already happened by mid-1991 that no renegotiation could reverse: the Baltic republics' claim rested on the invalidity of their incorporation and could not be satisfied inside any union; the ideological binding had lost credibility, so the union had no universal loyalty to invoke; and the centre's economic performance had become negative, so it had nothing to offer in exchange for subordination.

⚠️ **The resolution to write.** ⚠️ A smaller, looser union of willing republics was probably available until August 1991; the union as constituted was not. ⚠️ The coup did not create the outcome but removed the last version of it that had a plausible chance. ⚠️ That is a contingent, evidence-bounded answer, and it is exactly the register the examiner is testing for.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The redrawal of national boundaries: fifteen successor states is the largest single boundary change of the period.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Why did the Soviet Union disintegrate?" and the account's own propositions about Gorbachev's policies and about the China comparison all route here.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying the USSR "became Russia". ✅ Fifteen union republics became independent states; Russia was one of them.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the August coup. ⚠️ Without it the chronology has no trigger and the answer has no mechanism.
3. ⚠️ Writing that the coup caused the dissolution. ⚠️ It accelerated it and destroyed the remaining alternative; the conditions were already in place.
4. ⚠️ Treating the Baltic exits as ordinary secessions. ⚠️ Their claim was restoration of statehood, resting on the illegitimacy of the 1940 incorporation.
5. ⚠️ Dating the end of communist rule in Russia to 1989. ✅ It was over "after 74 years", in December 1991.
6. ⚠️ Assuming that the state's end resolved its problems. ⚠️ The economic crisis, the territorial conflicts and the custody of nuclear weapons all outlived it.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Four requirements of dissolution: units that can act as states; a centre unable or unwilling to prevent exit; a rival authority to rally to; and a trigger.
2. ✅ Yeltsin became president of the parliament of the Russian republic in May 1990 after conservatives excluded him from the Supreme Soviet, creating dual power in one city.
3. ✅ Yeltsin's three positions: a voluntary union with a right to opt out; an end to the Party's privileged position; rapid marketisation despite the pain.
4. ✅ Gorbachev's three: a voluntary union would disintegrate; the Party could still be transformed; rapid change would cause massive unemployment and higher prices.
5. ✅ Lithuania declared independence in March 1990; Latvia and Estonia followed more gradually; Moscow refused recognition.
6. ⚠️ The coup ran 19-21 August 1991, was mounted by a self-declared State Committee on the State of Emergency, detained Gorbachev, and was resisted from the Russian republic's leadership.
7. ✅ It failed, destroyed much of the remaining legitimacy of the party and accelerated republican exits: coercive restoration destroyed the authority it sought to save.
8. ⚠️ 8 December 1991: Russian, Ukrainian and Belarusian leaders agreed the union had ceased to exist and founded a Commonwealth of Independent States; 21 December 1991: eight further republics joined at Alma-Ata.
9. ✅ Gorbachev resigned on 25 December 1991; the union was formally wound up the following day; communist rule in Russia ended after 74 years.
10. ⚠️ The Baltic claim was restoration of statehood, not secession, and the Baltic states did not join the commonwealth.
11. ⚠️ What did not end: the economic crisis, the territorial conflicts, the custody problem of nuclear weapons, and communist party-states elsewhere.
12. ⚠️ The transferable principle: a failed restoration is worse for an old order than no attempt, because it converts a contested question into a demonstrated answer and hands the vocabulary of legality to the opposition.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the failure of the August 1991 coup accelerated the dissolution it was intended to prevent, and state the general principle it illustrates.

**Model answer:** A coup has to demonstrate that the old centre can still compel obedience. This one demonstrated the opposite within three days and in public. It removed Gorbachev from the scene at the only moment when the union's own institutions might have been defended by their head; it handed the opposition the most favourable possible ground, since resistance could be framed as the defence of legality against an unconstitutional seizure; and it identified the Party and the union's security organs with that seizure, so that the instrument through which any restoration would have had to work was discredited by the attempt. For the republics the effect was double: they had just watched the centre try force and fail, so the perceived cost of leaving fell, while the prospect of subordination to a centre capable of such attempts raised the perceived cost of staying. Both movements pointed the same way, and within four months republican leaders had agreed that the union had ceased to exist. The general principle is that a failed restoration is worse for an old order than no attempt at all, because it converts a contested question about the centre's capacity into a publicly demonstrated answer.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the coup caused the dissolution. The conditions were already assembled - fifteen republics with state institutions, a centre that had lost both performance legitimacy and the will to coerce, and a rival authority with an electoral mandate in the largest republic since May 1990. What the coup destroyed was the last version of the union that still had a plausible chance: a smaller, voluntary association of willing republics. Saying that is both more accurate and more valuable than calling the coup a cause.

---

## Lesson 12 - The aftermath in Russia: what the collapse of communism did not automatically produce

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: shock therapy from January 1992 and its recorded results; the voucher privatisation and the emergence of a small group of financier owners by 1995; the constitutional conflict of 1992-93 and the April 1993 referendum; the Duma elections of December 1995 and the presidential contest of 1996; the Putin period's economic recovery and its political costs; and the recorded judgement that collapse did not create democracy or a fair market

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the aftermath of communism under Yeltsin, Putin and Medvedev, including the shock therapy programme from January 1992, the voucher privatisation, the opposition in the Supreme Soviet, the 1995 and 1996 elections and the later economic record; several pages of this section are blank in the copy held for this session and the gap is recorded in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what shock therapy promised and what the record states

```text
   PROMISED (the adviser's assurance)          RECORDED OUTCOME
   ================================            ================

   Price liberalization, then                  Jan 1992: price controls
   privatization of almost the                 removed from about 90 per
   entire economy, within ONE YEAR             cent of goods; industrial
                                               subsidies ended
   "difficult for about six months"      -->   Prices rose steeply and KEPT
                                               rising after six months
   then "things would stabilize and      -->   By the end of the year prices
   people's lives would gradually              were ON AVERAGE 30 TIMES
   improve"                                    HIGHER than at the beginning

   Shops would fill                      -->   "there were plenty of goods in
                                               the shops but most people
                                               could not afford to buy them"

   Wages would follow                    -->   "wages did not keep pace with
                                               prices"; as sales fell,
                                               factory workers were laid off
                                               and OVER A MILLION people lost
                                               their jobs; thousands were
                                               homeless and forced to live in
                                               tents outside the towns; many
                                               relied on food parcels from
                                               abroad

   JOINT OWNERSHIP BY ALL THE PEOPLE     -->   Vouchers worth 10,000 roubles
   Every citizen given vouchers worth          were "the equivalent of about
   10,000 roubles; workers to buy              £35 - a minute amount at a time
   shares in their enterprise                  of rapid inflation"; most
                                               workers could not afford
                                               shares; managers accumulated
                                               vouchers and took over their
                                               plants; by the end of 1995
                                               most former state industry had
                                               passed to "a relatively small
                                               group of financiers, who became
                                               known as the 'oligarchs'"
                                               whose profits came "from
                                               government subsidies, which were
                                               reintroduced, rather than from
                                               the market", and which were
                                               moved into Swiss bank accounts
                                               and foreign investments.
                                               "Total investment in Russia fell
                                               by two-thirds."
```

⚠️ Every item in the right-hand column is a recorded statement, and the column as a whole is the single most useful body of evidence in the topic against the proposition that the collapse of communism was self-executing.

### 1. The learner's problem: why teach the aftermath at all?

⚠️ Because the examiner's question is almost never "what happened in 1991?" It is "what did 1991 mean?" - and the meaning depends on what followed.

⚠️ The point is worth stating directly: the post-1991 Russian record matters precisely because it shows that **the collapse of communism did not automatically create a healthy democracy or a fair market economy**. ⚠️ That sentence, supported by the evidence below, is the strongest single move available against a convergence narrative, and it is available from inside the same source that records the collapse.

### 2. Shock therapy: the mechanism, not just the misery

⚠️ The detail in the visual is worth converting into a mechanism, because listing hardship is description while explaining it is analysis.

- ⚠️ **Removing price controls in a shortage economy raises prices before it raises supply.** Supply responds only if producers can expand, and Soviet producers were uncompetitive, indebted and losing their bloc markets simultaneously - ✅ eastern Europe's industry "had now lost its guaranteed markets within the communist bloc", and Russian industry faced the same loss.
- ⚠️ **Ending subsidies while demand collapses destroys employment quickly.** ✅ "As sales fell, factory workers were laid off, and over a million people lost their jobs."
- ⚠️ **Voucher privatisation distributes claims, not control.** ⚠️ A claim is only worth what its holder can afford to keep. ✅ At 10,000 roubles - "about £35" at a time of rapid inflation - vouchers could be, and were, bought up; ✅ "managers were able to buy up and accumulate enough vouchers to take over the ownership of their plant."
- ⚠️ **Ownership without competition reproduces rent-seeking.** ✅ The new owners "made enormous profits, but from government subsidies, which were reintroduced, rather than from the market", and transferred them abroad rather than reinvesting. ✅ "Total investment in Russia fell by two-thirds."

⚠️ The conclusion to write is precise and defensible: ⚠️ marketisation transferred ownership faster than it created markets, and ownership without competition or investment is not capitalism in the sense the reform claimed to be building.

⚠️ The account records contemporary judgements, and each must be attributed rather than adopted. ✅ One report at the end of 1993, after two years of shock therapy, said: "Our country has been thrown back two centuries to the 'savage era' of capitalism." ✅ Daniel Beer is quoted: "the Yeltsin government presided over an economic collapse so vast and devastating that for most Russians the term became synonymous with chaos and the plunder of state property (that is, society's) by a small clique of robber barons." ✅ A report prepared for Yeltsin early in 1994 "estimated that criminal mafias had gained control of between 70 and 80 per cent of all business and banking". ✅ Alexander Chubarov described the policies as "deformed capitalism". ⚠️ All four are attributed estimates and judgements; none may be restated as an unowned fact.

### 3. Politics without institutions

✅ "The leading politicians lacked experience of democracy as well as of how to organize a market economy. At first there were no properly organized political parties on the western model, and the constitution, a leftover from the Soviet era, was unclear about the division of powers between president and parliament."

✅ "However, in November 1992 the Communist Party was legalized again, and other groups began to form, although Yeltsin himself did not have a supporting party. A majority in parliament strongly opposed Yeltsin's policies and tried to get rid of him, but in a referendum in April 1993, 53 per cent of voters" supported his position.

⚠️ Two structural points, both transferable.

1. ⚠️ **A constitution that does not allocate powers clearly converts every policy dispute into a constitutional crisis.** ⚠️ When president and parliament each believe they hold the decisive authority, there is no procedure for losing, and the dispute escalates to the level of the institution itself.
2. ⚠️ **A president without a party has no legislative instrument.** ✅ Yeltsin "did not have a supporting party", which is why he governed by referendum, decree and confrontation rather than by majority.

✅ The electoral record is equally instructive. ✅ In the Duma elections of December 1995, "Yeltsin and his supporters won only 65 seats out of the 450, whereas the Communist Party, led by Gennady Zyuganov, took 157 seats; together with their allies, they could muster 186 seats, by far the largest grouping." ✅ The account's own comment: "There was obviously much residual support and nostalgia for the old days of the USSR and strong government."

✅ In the 1996 presidential election, some advisers wanted Yeltsin to cancel the vote "and resort to force if necessary"; ✅ "to his credit he allowed it to go ahead"; ✅ over twenty candidates registered, including Zyuganov and Gorbachev; ✅ in the first round Yeltsin took 35 per cent to Zyuganov's 32, and **Gorbachev received barely 1 per cent**; ✅ in the second round Yeltsin won with 54 per cent.

⚠️ Gorbachev's 1 per cent is one of the most quotable facts in the topic. ⚠️ The man credited abroad with ending the Cold War was, five years later, almost unelectable at home. ⚠️ It is the sharpest available demonstration that international reputation and domestic legitimacy are different currencies, and that the population experienced the period primarily as loss.

✅ The account's explanation of the 1996 result is a model of qualified causation: "The reason for Yeltsin's victory was not so much that people liked him, but that they liked the alternative even less. If the communists had put forward genuine social democrat policies, Zyuganov might well have won. But Zyuganov was not a social democrat; he made no secret of his admiration for Stalin, and this was a fatal mistake."

### 4. Recovery, and its price

✅ The later record is mixed and must be written as mixed. ✅ Oil prices rose from around £28 a barrel in 2000 to £40 and then over £60 by the end of 2006; ✅ Russia became the largest producer of gas in the world and the second largest exporter of oil after Saudi Arabia; ✅ the economy "had grown steadily by over 6 per cent a year"; ✅ software exports were worth $1.5 billion in 2006; ✅ and in 2005 National Priority Projects were announced for health, education, housing and agriculture, including wage increases for health workers and teachers.

✅ Against that: ✅ much of the revenue went into a large reserve fund rather than investment, meaning "less government investment and stagnation in the economic reform programme"; ✅ after the 2008-9 world financial crisis cut off cheap credit, a reserve fund of $90 billion helped Russia cope but the priority projects suffered; ✅ the higher-education budget had been cut to 12 per cent of its 1989 level after the 1998 crisis and had largely not been restored by 2008; ✅ the health service remained free but "the care is far inferior to that provided under the communists", with payment required for the best and quickest treatment; ✅ pensioners were probably worst affected, with prices rocketing and pensions not increasing.

✅ And the political cost is recorded with named victims and attributed allegations: ✅ the journalist Anna Politkovskaya, a long-time critic of the Chechen war and author of a 2004 book claiming that Russia still had elements of a police state, "was shot dead in the lift of her apartment block" in October 2006; ✅ in November 2006 Alexander Litvinenko was killed in London by polonium 210, a Russian security agent was charged, extradition was refused, "the UK government did not directly blame the Russian government" though "there was a clear inference", and in 2007 a book by Alex Goldfarb with Litvinenko's widow argued that Putin himself must have ordered the murder. ⚠️ Every element of that paragraph is an attributed claim and must be written as one.

✅ The account also records a structural explanation of quiescence, attributed to Perry Anderson: the liberal intelligentsia "is compromised by its own part in bringing to being what it now so dislikes. By clinging to Yeltsin long after the illegality and corruption of his rule were plain, in the name of defence against a toothless Communism, it destroyed its credibility in the eyes of the population, only to find that Yeltsin had landed it with Putin."

⚠️ The compressed judgement to memorise is that the later period restored state authority more effectively than liberal credibility. ✅ And the account records that many ordinary Russians "look back on post-Stalinist Soviet times with nostalgia, in spite of its drawbacks".

### Criticism and reply: is this just pessimism about the post-communist transition?

⚠️ **The objection.** Emphasising the costs risks implying that the old system was preferable, which the evidence of Lessons 2 and 6 - shortages, queues, a quarter of the population below the poverty line by early 1990 - flatly contradicts.

⚠️ **The reply.** The argument here is not comparative but **conditional**. It does not say that communism was better; it says that removing communism did not by itself install anything. ⚠️ The two claims are compatible and both are evidenced in this session. ⚠️ The eastern European material makes the same point without the Russian extremes: ✅ shops were better stocked "but prices of consumer goods soared and very few people could afford to buy them", and "the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism".

⚠️ **The counter-evidence that must be conceded.** ✅ Not every transition produced the Russian outcome. ✅ Poland showed "clear signs of recovery" by 1994; ✅ East Germany had the resources of the former West Germany; ✅ Czechoslovakia separated peacefully in 1992; ✅ and by 2004 ten former communist or southern European states had entered the European Union. ⚠️ Divergence, not uniform failure, is the accurate finding - and divergence is also what refutes teleology in Lesson 21.

⚠️ **The residual.** Why did outcomes diverge so sharply from a shared starting point? ⚠️ The evidence in this session points to three variables - the presence or absence of an organised political counterpart, the availability of external resources and an external anchor, and the sequencing and design of the economic programme - but the session cannot rank them, and it says so rather than pretending otherwise.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Political philosophies and their effect on society: the transition is the test case for what replaces a planned economy and a one-party state.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ The account's own end-of-chapter questions include "Explain why the collapse of the USSR was followed by serious economic and political problems" and the proposition that Putin's Russia "may well have been a police state, but at least he rescued the country from the chaos of the Yeltsin years".

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that Russia became a democracy and a market economy in 1991. ⚠️ The recorded judgement is that collapse created neither automatically.
2. ⚠️ Adopting the attributed judgements as your own. ✅ The savage-era quotation, Daniel Beer's assessment, the 70-80 per cent mafia estimate and Chubarov's "deformed capitalism" are all attributed and must stay attributed.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the 1996 election detail. ⚠️ Gorbachev's barely 1 per cent is the single sharpest datum on the gap between foreign reputation and domestic legitimacy.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the later recovery as vindication or as fraud. ✅ Both sides are recorded: over 6 per cent annual growth, oil and gas rank, software exports and priority projects on one side; reserve-fund stagnation, unrestored education and health spending, pensioner hardship and named killings on the other.
5. ⚠️ Generalising the Russian outcome to the whole region. ✅ Poland, East Germany, Czechoslovakia and the 2004 accession states diverged sharply.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Shock therapy began January 1992: price controls removed from about 90 per cent of goods; industrial subsidies ended.
2. ✅ By the end of 1992 prices were on average thirty times higher; goods were in the shops but unaffordable; wages lagged; over a million lost their jobs; thousands lived in tents; many relied on food parcels from abroad.
3. ✅ Vouchers worth 10,000 roubles were about £35; workers could not afford shares; managers accumulated vouchers; by the end of 1995 most former state industry was held by a small group of financiers known as the oligarchs.
4. ✅ Their profits came from reintroduced government subsidies rather than the market, and were moved abroad; total investment in Russia fell by two-thirds.
5. ✅ Attributed judgements: the "savage era" report of 1993; Daniel Beer on plunder by robber barons; a 1994 report estimating criminal mafia control of 70-80 per cent of business and banking; Chubarov's "deformed capitalism".
6. ✅ The Soviet-era constitution was unclear on the division of powers between president and parliament; the Communist Party was relegalised in November 1992; Yeltsin had no supporting party; a referendum in April 1993 gave him 53 per cent.
7. ✅ December 1995 Duma elections: Yeltsin's supporters 65 of 450; the Communist Party 157, with allies 186, the largest grouping - evidence of "residual support and nostalgia".
8. ✅ 1996 presidential election: advisers urged cancellation and force; Yeltsin allowed it; first round Yeltsin 35 per cent, Zyuganov 32, Gorbachev barely 1; second round Yeltsin 54 per cent; the account attributes the result to dislike of the alternative and to Zyuganov's open admiration for Stalin.
9. ✅ Later record: oil from about £28 a barrel in 2000 to over £60 by end-2006; largest gas producer and second largest oil exporter; growth over 6 per cent a year; software exports $1.5 billion in 2006; National Priority Projects announced 2005.
10. ✅ Against it: a large reserve fund at the cost of investment; the 2008-9 crisis absorbed by a $90 billion fund; higher-education budget cut to 12 per cent of the 1989 level and not restored by 2008; inferior free health care; pensioners worst affected; nostalgia for post-Stalinist Soviet times.
11. ✅ Named attributed cases: Politkovskaya shot in October 2006; Litvinenko killed in London in November 2006 by polonium 210, with extradition refused and a clear inference but no direct governmental attribution by the UK.
12. ⚠️ The master sentence: the collapse of communism did not automatically create a healthy democracy or a fair market economy - and in the later period state authority was restored more effectively than liberal credibility.

### Concept check

**Question:** Voucher privatisation was designed to transfer state industry to joint ownership by all the people. Explain the mechanism by which it produced concentrated ownership instead, and state what this shows about marketisation as a process.

**Model answer:** Every citizen received vouchers to the value of 10,000 roubles as their share, and workers were to be able to buy shares in their enterprise. Two things defeated the design. First, the vouchers were worth very little in real terms - the equivalent of about £35 at a time of rapid inflation - so they were easy for others to purchase and hard for holders to value. Second, most workers could not afford to buy shares at all, because wages were not keeping pace with prices in a year in which prices rose on average thirty times. The result was that managers were able to buy up and accumulate enough vouchers to take over the ownership of their plants, and by the end of 1995 most former state industry had passed to a relatively small group of financiers. What this shows about marketisation is that transferring ownership is much faster than creating a market: the new owners made enormous profits from government subsidies that were reintroduced rather than from competition, transferred them into Swiss bank accounts and foreign investments rather than reinvesting, and total investment in Russia fell by two-thirds. Ownership without competition or investment reproduces rent-seeking under a new name.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from this that the Soviet system was preferable. The argument is conditional, not comparative: removing a command system does not by itself install a functioning market or a functioning democracy, and the evidence of shortages, queues and a quarter of the population below the poverty line by early 1990 stands unchanged. The accurate finding is divergence - Poland showing clear signs of recovery by 1994, East Germany subsidised by the former West, Czechoslovakia separating peacefully in 1992 - and divergence is exactly what refutes any claim that the period had a single predetermined destination.

## Lesson 13 - Explaining 1989-91: six causal families, four readings, one contingency test

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the six causal families and what each explains and fails to explain; the four historiographical readings and their characteristic errors; the counterfactual test using China and Romania; the distinction between vulnerability, timing and character; and the balanced verdict the evidence supports

### 1. The learner's problem: the temptation to pick a winner

⚠️ Examiners on this topic reward a ranked, evidenced combination and punish both a single-cause answer and a list. ⚠️ The list looks safe and earns little, because listing six causes without saying what each does is the analytical equivalent of shrugging.

⚠️ The method that works is to separate three different questions that the word "why" conflates.

```text
  VULNERABILITY   Why was this system capable of collapsing at all?
                  -> causes 1, 2, 5 (structure)

  TIMING          Why 1989-91 and not 1979 or 2001?
                  -> causes 3, 4, 6 (reform, mobilisation, diplomacy)

  CHARACTER       Why largely without mass violence, except in
                  Romania and later Yugoslavia?
                  -> the non-coercion decision, the scale
                     arithmetic, and the elite splits
```

⚠️ An answer organised on those three questions cannot produce a list, because each question forces a selection and a justification.

### 2. The six families, with their strongest evidence

| ⚠️ Family | ✅ Strongest evidence in this session | ⚠️ The qualification that must accompany it |
|---|---|---|
| Economic stagnation and technological weakness | ✅ Gorbachev's own "absurd situation"; the diagnosis of over-centralisation and consumer shortage; Glenny's judgement on the bloc's social record; growth unchanged in 1988-89 and national income falling 15 per cent in 1991 | ⚠️ Armed states survive stagnation through coercion; this explains urgency, not outcome |
| Defence and imperial burdens | ✅ Brezhnev's simultaneous disarmament rhetoric and force expansion; the unresolved strategic-defence programme; over 100,000 troops in Afghanistan; a drain on resources and a blow to prestige | ⚠️ Burden does not explain why the withdrawal was peaceful or why it happened when it did |
| Gorbachev's agency | ✅ The 1985 accession, the 1988 diagnosis, the glasnost measures, the 1987 treaty, the Afghan withdrawal, the engineered replacement of hardline satellite leaders, and above all the non-intervention choice | ⚠️ He intended to save the system; the outcome was not his project, and he acted inside constraints he inherited |
| Society from below | ✅ Solidarity's August 1988 strikes; the Polish Church "loud in its criticisms"; the Protestant Church and New Forum; Charter 77 and the national strike; half a million Soviet miners and 42 demands; "a triumph of 'people power'" | ⚠️ Organisation and sequence differed greatly by country, as Romania's absence of any equivalent movement shows |
| Nationality mobilisation | ✅ Fifteen republics with parliaments; Nagorno-Karabakh in February 1988; Lithuania in March 1990; Yeltsin's Russian presidency; the December 1991 republican decisions | ⚠️ Nationalism strengthened because central authority and ideology were already weakening; it is partly an effect |
| Western pressure and diplomacy | ✅ The 1987 treaty; Reagan in Moscow in June 1988; Malta in December 1989; the November 1990 declaration; the account's explicit distribution of credit to Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response | ⚠️ A triumphalist "the West defeated the USSR" thesis ignores Soviet and eastern European agency, which the same account records in detail |

### 3. The four readings, and the characteristic error of each

⚠️ Historiography on this topic is not decoration; it is a way of showing an examiner that you know your own answer is a choice among alternatives.

| ⚠️ Reading | ⚠️ What it stresses | ⚠️ What it explains well | ⚠️ Its characteristic error |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Western-triumphalist** | Arms pressure and American resolve | An external constraint that genuinely narrowed Soviet options | Understates Soviet reform choices and eastern European agency; turns a dismantling into a victory |
| **Structuralist** | Stagnation, technology, nationality contradictions | Why the system was vulnerable | Cannot explain why collapse came in 1989-91 rather than earlier; risks implying inevitability |
| **Agency-centred** | Gorbachev, Yeltsin, Reagan | Why particular decisions produced particular turning points | Can exaggerate individual freedom from institutions and inherited crises |
| **Social-historical** | Workers, churches, civic networks, demonstrators | Why openings became regime change | Must still distinguish Poland, Hungary, East Germany, Czechoslovakia and Romania rather than writing "the people" |
| **Postcolonial** | The claim that one Western model became history's endpoint | Why convergence was a claim rather than a law; hierarchy, sovereignty and unequal globalisation | Is a critique of interpretation rather than a causal account of 1989-91, and should be used where it belongs, in Lessons 20 and 21 |

⚠️ The practical use in an answer is a single sentence: name the reading you are rejecting, state what it gets right, then state what it cannot carry. ⚠️ That is worth more than a paragraph of narrative.

### 4. The contingency test, run twice

⚠️ The cleanest way to defeat determinism is to point at a case with the same inputs and a different output. ⚠️ This session holds two.

**Test one: China, 1989.** ✅ Comparable structural pressures existed. ✅ In June 1989 the Chinese government "used troops to disperse a student demonstration in Tiananmen Square", the account records that at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed, and the action brought worldwide condemnation. ✅ The communist system survived; ✅ the account's own end-of-chapter question asks whether the USSR could have survived "in the same way that communism survived in China". ⚠️ Conclusion: the pressures did not determine the outcome. What differed was a decision about force.

**Test two: Romania, December 1989.** ✅ Within the same regional wave, one regime did use force: the Timisoara demonstration was brutally put down with many killed, and the army initially fired on the Bucharest crowds. ⚠️ It still fell - but only after ✅ "the army was refusing to continue the killing". ⚠️ Conclusion: force was available in eastern Europe too, and what defeated it was not its absence but its limits - the army's refusal to continue, and, as the account says of the region, the impossibility of shooting "a large proportion of the population in the big cities".

⚠️ Put the two tests together and the defensible position emerges. ⚠️ Coercion could still work where a regime's own security forces would sustain it and the demonstrations could be contained. ⚠️ It failed in eastern Europe because Moscow would not supply it, because local elites divided rather than authorise it, and because the numbers involved would have required killing on a scale the forces themselves refused. ⚠️ That is a contingent explanation resting on three separately evidenced conditions - which is exactly what a 20-mark answer should contain.

### 5. The verdict, in the form the evidence supports

> ⚠️ **Balanced verdict:** structural weakness made reform urgent; reform altered incentives and released opposition; organised societies and republics supplied alternatives; and diplomacy together with the refusal to use mass force made reversal increasingly difficult.

⚠️ Four qualified theses follow from it, and each can open an answer.

- ⚠️ **On collapse:** "Soviet communism was not defeated; it was dismantled from above by leaders who tried to reform it and then declined to shoot in order to save it."
- ⚠️ **On agency:** "Moscow's refusal to coerce and Poland's capacity to mobilise are the two halves of one explanation; naming only the first makes the collapse a Kremlin decision, and naming only the second makes it inevitable."
- ⚠️ **On nationalism:** "National mobilisation was the form the crisis took rather than the cause it had, because federal institutions supplied ready-made vehicles for a loyalty that the failing ideology could no longer outrank."
- ⚠️ **On non-teleology:** "The collapse of Soviet communism was not proof that every state would converge on one political-economic model; outcomes depended on institutions, social coalitions, state capacity and external constraints."

### Criticism and reply: does a multi-causal answer say anything at all?

⚠️ **The objection.** An explanation that names six causes and ranks none is a refusal to answer. Examiners reward decisiveness.

⚠️ **The reply.** The answer here is decisive; it is decisive about **what each cause does** rather than about which one is biggest. ⚠️ It says that structure explains vulnerability and not timing; that agency explains timing and not vulnerability; that mobilisation explains the conversion of reform into regime change; and that non-coercion explains the character. ⚠️ Those are four ranked assignments, not a shrug. ⚠️ And the assignment is testable: each claim can be refuted by producing a case where the named cause does the other job, which is precisely why the China and Romania tests are included.

⚠️ **The residual.** One thing the evidence held here genuinely cannot settle: the relative weight of Western pressure. ⚠️ The account credits Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response together, and gives no basis for weighting them. ⚠️ Honest practice is to say so and to add that the triumphalist thesis is rejected not because Western pressure was negligible but because it cannot account for the Soviet and eastern European decisions that the same source records in detail.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This is the analytical centre of the whole topic for Paper I, and it supplies the framework for any evaluative directive.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "The Cold War ended through interaction between structural decay and political choice. Discuss." ⚠️ "Was the Cold War ended more by Soviet weakness, Western pressure or political choice?" ⚠️ "Analyse the causes of the collapse of communism in the USSR and eastern Europe."

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing "communism failed". ⚠️ What collapsed was a regime type in a particular set of states; the doctrinal question is a separate one, and the collapse of a regime is not a refutation of a doctrine.
2. ⚠️ Adopting the triumphalist thesis. ⚠️ It cannot carry the Soviet and eastern European decisions that the evidence records.
3. ⚠️ Adopting pure structuralism. ⚠️ It cannot explain 1989-91 rather than 1979, and it implies an inevitability the China case refutes.
4. ⚠️ Listing causes without assignments. ⚠️ Say what each explains and where it stops.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the contingency test. ⚠️ China and Romania are the two cases that make the answer non-deterministic, and they are in the same source base.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Three separate questions: vulnerability, timing, character. Assign causes to questions rather than ranking them in the abstract.
2. ✅ Family one, economic stagnation: explains urgency and loss of performance legitimacy; does not explain why coercion was not used.
3. ✅ Family two, defence and imperial burdens: explains resource scarcity and reluctance to take on more; does not explain timing or peacefulness.
4. ✅ Family three, Gorbachev's agency: explains why reform began in 1985 in this form and why the satellites lost their guarantee; he intended to save the system.
5. ✅ Family four, society from below: explains why openings became regime change; organisation and sequence differed by country.
6. ✅ Family five, nationality mobilisation: explains why a multinational state dissolved rather than changing government; partly an effect of weakening authority.
7. ✅ Family six, Western pressure and diplomacy: explains changed Soviet calculations; cannot carry a triumphalist thesis.
8. ⚠️ Four readings and their errors: triumphalist understates Soviet and eastern European agency; structuralist cannot date the collapse; agency-centred overstates individual freedom; social-historical must differentiate the countries; postcolonial belongs to the interpretation of the order, not to causation.
9. ✅ Contingency test one: China in June 1989 - comparable pressures, troops used, at least a thousand students killed and many later executed, worldwide condemnation, and the system survived.
10. ✅ Contingency test two: Romania - force was used, many were killed, and the regime still fell once the army refused to continue the killing.
11. ⚠️ The balanced verdict: structural weakness made reform urgent; reform released opposition; societies and republics supplied alternatives; diplomacy and non-coercion made reversal increasingly difficult.
12. ⚠️ Four qualified theses: dismantled not defeated; permission plus agency; nationalism as form rather than cause; and explicit non-teleology.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the China and Romania cases together defeat the claim that the Soviet bloc's structural problems made its collapse inevitable, and state the contingent explanation they support instead.

**Model answer:** China had comparable structural problems and met a mass demonstration in June 1989 by using troops to disperse it in Tiananmen Square, with at least a thousand students killed and many later executed and worldwide condemnation following; its communist system survived. That case shows the pressures do not entail the outcome, because the same kind of pressure met a different decision and produced a different result - which is why the account's own end-of-chapter question asks whether the USSR could have survived in the same way that communism survived in China. Romania shows the converse: within the eastern European wave, one regime did use force - the Timisoara demonstration of 17 December 1989 was brutally put down with many killed, and the army initially fired on the Bucharest crowds - and it still fell, but only once the army refused to continue the killing. Together they support a contingent explanation with three conditions: coercion remained effective where a regime's own security forces would sustain it and the crowds could be contained; it failed across eastern Europe because Moscow would not supply it, because local elites divided rather than authorise it, and because, as the account puts it, troops would have had to shoot a large proportion of the population in the big cities to keep control.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the multi-causal verdict as a refusal to take a position. The position taken is precise: structure explains vulnerability but not timing, agency explains timing but not vulnerability, social mobilisation explains why reform became regime change, and the non-coercion decision explains the largely non-violent character. Each of those four assignments can be tested and refuted, which is what distinguishes an argument from a list.
