## Lesson 14 - The unipolar moment: the order claim, its author, and its three tests

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the origin and wording of the "new world order" claim; what American spokesmen said the era would contain; the account's recorded critique of American practice with named authors; the distinction between capability, rules and legitimacy; the two rival theses about the post-1991 world; and the reason unipolarity is a distribution rather than an order

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's chapter on the new world order and the war against global terrorism, specifically its opening section on the new world order, which records what American spokesmen announced, quotes named critics, and lists the American actions the critics cite
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three tests, and what the evidence does to each

```text
   TEST 1: CAPABILITY - is power concentrated?
   +------------------------------------------------------+
   | "The overriding fact now was that the USA was left as |
   |  the world's only superpower."                        |
   |  A US-led coalition of over 600,000, with more than   |
   |  thirty nations contributing, reversed Iraq's         |
   |  occupation of Kuwait in a campaign of two phases     |
   |  and four days of ground fighting.                    |
   |  VERDICT: PASSED, and passed decisively               |
   +------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
   TEST 2: RULES - do shared rules govern the use of power?
   +------------------------------------------------------+
   |  FOR: the Gulf action rested on a Security Council    |
   |  authorisation with a deadline of 15 January 1991     |
   |  AGAINST (all recorded in the account):               |
   |   - Panama, December 1989: at least 2,000 civilians   |
   |     killed; an OAS resolution "to deeply regret the   |
   |     military intervention" carried 20 votes to 1      |
   |   - a long record of vetoes and lone negative votes,  |
   |     including 130-1 in 1985 and 140-1 in 1987         |
   |   - rejection of an International Court judgment on   |
   |     the mining of Nicaraguan harbours, and refusal to |
   |     pay the compensation the court ordered            |
   |   - rejection in 2001 of the 1972 missile treaty and  |
   |     withdrawal from the 1997 climate protocols        |
   |  VERDICT: CONTESTED                                   |
   +------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
   TEST 3: LEGITIMACY - is the arrangement accepted by
           those who live under it?
   +------------------------------------------------------+
   |  "instead of being universally loved and admired, the |
   |   USA, or rather US governments, ended up being hated |
   |   so violently in certain quarters that people were   |
   |   driven to commit the most terrible acts of          |
   |   terrorism in protest against the USA and its system"|
   |  Guyatt: "large and dangerous pockets of resentment   |
   |   towards the US around the world, grounded not in    |
   |   fundamentalism or insanity but in a real perception |
   |   of the imbalance of power"                          |
   |  VERDICT: FAILED, on the account's own evidence       |
   +------------------------------------------------------+

   CONCLUSION: unipolarity is a DISTRIBUTION OF CAPABILITY.
   An ORDER additionally requires rules, legitimacy,
   institutional consent and the capacity to manage different
   kinds of conflict. Passing test one does not pass tests
   two and three.
```

### 1. Where the phrase came from, and why the author matters

⚠️ "New world order" is not a historian's label applied afterwards; it is a claim made at the time, by an identifiable person, for an identifiable purpose.

⚠️ President George H. W. Bush used it in an address to a joint session of Congress on 11 September 1990, during the Kuwait crisis and before any fighting, presenting a new world order as an objective of the response to Iraq's invasion: an era freer from the threat of terror, stronger in the pursuit of justice, more secure in the quest for peace, in which the rule of law would supplant the rule of the jungle and the strong would respect the rights of the weak.

⚠️ Three consequences follow from knowing the origin.

1. ⚠️ **It was a programme, not a description.** ⚠️ The correct examination question is therefore not "did a new world order exist?" but "was the programme realised, and on what evidence?"
2. ⚠️ **It was announced in the context of an enforcement action.** ⚠️ The Gulf case was chosen by its own author as the demonstration, which is why Lesson 15 treats it as the strongest available evidence for the claim.
3. ⚠️ **Assessing it is therefore a legitimate historical exercise, not editorialising.** ⚠️ A candidate is testing a stated proposition against a record, which is what the directive "critically examine" asks for.

### 2. What the claim contained, in the account's summary

✅ "Soon after the US 'victory' in the Cold War, various American spokesmen announced that the USA was looking forward to a new era of peace and international co-operation. They implied that the USA, the world's only superpower - all-powerful and unchallengeable - was now committed to good works; support for international justice, liberty and human rights; the eradication of poverty; and the spread of education, health and democracy throughout the world."

⚠️ Note the account's own quotation marks around "victory" in that sentence; ⚠️ the source is signalling scepticism about the word, and reproducing the scepticism is safer than adopting either the word or its rejection.

✅ It also records the mood: "Understandably, Americans were full of pride in their country's achievements; in 1997 David Rothkopf, a minister in the Clinton administration, wrote: 'The Americans should not deny the fact that of all the nations in the history of the world, theirs is the most just, the most tolerant and the best model for the future.'"

⚠️ Quote that carefully and attribute it. ⚠️ It is evidence of what was believed and said inside the administration, and evidence of belief is not evidence of fact.

### 3. The account's recorded critique, with its named authors

⚠️ The account then asks its own question - ✅ "How did the post-Cold War era, which seemed so full of hope, turn out to be so full of hatred and horror?" - and answers it with a set of attributed judgements and dated instances. ⚠️ Every one must be carried with its attribution.

| ✅ Recorded item | ⚠️ Attribution and label |
|---|---|
| ✅ "many people around the world are frustrated by the complacency and impenetrability of the US ... [there are] large and dangerous pockets of resentment towards the US around the world, grounded not in fundamentalism or insanity but in a real perception of the imbalance of power, and a real frustration at the impotence of political means of change" | ✅ Nicholas Guyatt, *Another American Century*, published 2000, quoted by the account |
| ✅ "As long as the US remains insulated from the effects of its actions, it will have little sense of the true desperation they produce in others" | ✅ Guyatt's conclusion, quoted by the account |
| ✅ Panama, December 1989: "at least 2000 civilians were killed when US forces invaded and bombed Panama", an operation to arrest Manuel Noriega, who "had worked for the CIA and had been backed by the US government until 1987"; the Organization of American States resolution "to deeply regret the military intervention in Panama" was approved 20 votes to 1, "the one being the USA" | ✅ Recorded by the account; ⚠️ the civilian figure is an attributed estimate and must travel with "at least" and with its source |
| ✅ Suppression of left-wing movements in Mexico, Colombia, Ecuador and Peru during the 1990s; participation in the 1999 bombing of Serbia, described as controversial; intervention by American agents in Nicaraguan elections in 1989 and 2001 | ✅ Recorded by the account; ⚠️ the Latin American material is owned in detail by the Latin America topic |
| ✅ "The enemy was, and remains, any government or movement, or even individual, that stands in the way of the expansion of the American Empire" | ✅ William Blum, *Rogue State*, quoted by the account; ⚠️ a polemical judgement, and must be labelled as a quotation rather than adopted |
| ✅ Rwanda 1994: the USA "was reluctant to play a full part, since no direct US interests were involved and intervention on a sufficiently large scale would have been expensive. Because of the delays, some half a million people were massacred" | ✅ Recorded by the account; ⚠️ an attributed estimate; ⚠️ Guyatt's judgement follows that the USA "atrophied the cause of peacekeeping just as the situation in Rwanda required a flexible and dynamic response" |
| ✅ The rejection of an International Court of Justice verdict condemning the United States for the unlawful mining of Nicaraguan harbours, the refusal to pay the compensation ordered, and the increase of support to the forces destabilising the Nicaraguan government - juxtaposed by the account with Reagan's remark the previous day that "without law there can only be chaos and disorder" | ✅ Recorded by the account |
| ✅ Lone negative votes: in 1985 the only vote against a human-rights safeguarding resolution, 130 to 1; in 1987 the only vote against strengthening Third World communication services, 140 to 1; in 1996 refusal at a World Food Summit to endorse a right "to have access to safe and nutritious food" | ✅ Recorded by the account |
| ✅ "When the UN fails to serve as an instrument of American unilateralism on issues of elite concern, it is dismissed" | ✅ Noam Chomsky, *Hegemony or Survival*, quoted by the account |
| ✅ "international law as applied by the US increasingly bears little relationship to international law as understood anywhere else ... It is possible that ... the US is in fact attempting to create new, exceptional rules for itself alone" | ✅ Michael Byers, quoted by the account |
| ✅ In his first year in office and before September 2001, President George W. Bush rejected the 1972 anti-ballistic missile treaty, withdrew from the 1997 climate protocols, halted new diplomatic contacts with North Korea and refused to cooperate on chemical-weapons control | ✅ Recorded by the account |
| ✅ American control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund, so that states applying for loans "had to make sure that their internal policies were acceptable to the USA"; Washington's opposition in 1995 to the new Bank president's debt-relief and social priorities, arguing for strict austerity | ✅ Recorded by the account; ⚠️ Will Hutton is quoted that "the international financial system has been shaped to extend US financial and political power" |

⚠️ **How to use this table honestly.** ⚠️ It is a dossier of attributed criticism recorded in one account, not a proven indictment. ⚠️ The strongest answer states it as such: that the account read for this session assembles a critical case from named authors and dated instances, and that the case is evidence about the gap between the announced programme and the recorded practice. ❌ Writing any of these judgements in your own voice as settled fact is a source error, and writing none of them is an evidence gap.

### 4. Capability, rules and legitimacy: why the distinction decides the question

⚠️ The canonical formulation is worth memorising verbatim: ⚠️ **unipolarity is a distribution of capability; order additionally requires rules, legitimacy, institutional consent and the ability to manage different kinds of conflict.**

⚠️ The distinction does real work. ⚠️ A single state can be far stronger than any other and still preside over disorder, because strength answers only the question "who can act?" ⚠️ An order answers three further questions: by what rules may anyone act, who accepts those rules as binding on themselves, and what happens in the cases the rules do not fit.

⚠️ On the account's evidence, the post-1991 arrangement passed the first test and struggled with the other three. ⚠️ Lesson 15 shows the best case for the rules test; Lessons 16 and 17 show the cases the rules did not fit.

### 5. The two rival theses, named and bounded

⚠️ Two interpretations of the post-1991 world circulated widely, and both are worth naming once because questions allude to them.

⚠️ The first, associated with Francis Fukuyama's essay "The End of History?" published in 1989, argued that with no viable ideological rival remaining, liberal democracy represented the endpoint of ideological evolution. ⚠️ The second, associated with Samuel Huntington's article "The Clash of Civilizations?" published in 1993 partly in response, argued that the principal source of conflict would be cultural rather than ideological.

⚠️ **The disciplined use of both.** ⚠️ Name them as **claims made at the time** about what the period meant, not as descriptions of what it was. ⚠️ Lesson 21 explains why this session rejects the first as teleology and treats the second with caution, and why the postcolonial critique - which questions the claim that one Western model had become history's universal endpoint - is the more useful corrective for an Indian examination answer.

❌ Do not extend either thesis into a narrative of the war on terrorism. ⚠️ That subject is studied elsewhere in the syllabus, and this topic stops at the point where the order claim can be tested.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Paper I on the transformation of international relations; ✅ Paper II on international institutions, their structure and mandate.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Critically examine the idea of a 'new world order' after 1991." ⚠️ "Did the Gulf War confirm a new world order, or only a brief alignment of power and institutions?"

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating "new world order" as a neutral descriptive term. ⚠️ It was a programme announced on 11 September 1990 by a named speaker during the Kuwait crisis.
2. ⚠️ Equating unipolarity with world government. ⚠️ Capability, rules and legitimacy are three different tests.
3. ⚠️ Adopting the recorded criticisms in your own voice. ✅ Guyatt, Blum, Chomsky, Byers and Hutton are quoted authors; attribute them.
4. ⚠️ Dropping the qualifiers on attributed figures. ✅ "At least 2,000 civilians" in Panama; "some half a million people" massacred in Rwanda.
5. ⚠️ Turning the answer into a post-2001 narrative. ⚠️ The boundary stops at the testing of the claim.
6. ⚠️ Presenting the end-of-history thesis as the period's accepted description. ⚠️ It was one contemporary claim among rivals, and this session rejects its teleology.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ The phrase was used by President George H. W. Bush in an address to Congress on 11 September 1990, during the Kuwait crisis, as an objective rather than a description.
2. ✅ American spokesmen announced a new era of peace and cooperation, with the USA committed to international justice, liberty and human rights, the eradication of poverty and the spread of education, health and democracy.
3. ✅ The account puts "victory" in quotation marks and records Rothkopf's 1997 statement that the United States was the most just and tolerant nation in history and the best model for the future.
4. ✅ Guyatt, published 2000: resentment grounded "not in fundamentalism or insanity but in a real perception of the imbalance of power"; and the warning that a state insulated from the effects of its actions will not perceive the desperation they produce.
5. ✅ Panama, December 1989: at least 2,000 civilians killed; the OAS regret resolution carried 20 to 1, the one being the USA; Noriega had worked for the CIA and had US backing until 1987.
6. ✅ Rwanda 1994: reluctance because no direct US interests were involved and large-scale intervention would be expensive; because of the delays, some half a million people were massacred.
7. ✅ The International Court judgment on the mining of Nicaraguan harbours was rejected, the ordered compensation refused, and support to the destabilising forces increased - juxtaposed with Reagan's remark about law and chaos.
8. ✅ Lone votes: 130-1 in 1985 on human rights; 140-1 in 1987 on Third World communications; refusal in 1996 to endorse a right to safe and nutritious food.
9. ✅ Quoted judgements to attribute: Blum on the American Empire; Chomsky on the UN being dismissed when it fails to serve unilateralism; Byers on exceptional rules; Hutton on the financial system.
10. ✅ Before September 2001, the incoming administration rejected the 1972 missile treaty, withdrew from the 1997 climate protocols, halted North Korean contacts and refused chemical-weapons cooperation.
11. ⚠️ The master distinction: unipolarity is a distribution of capability; order additionally requires rules, legitimacy, institutional consent and the ability to manage different kinds of conflict.
12. ⚠️ Two named contemporary theses: Fukuyama's "The End of History?" of 1989 and Huntington's "The Clash of Civilizations?" of 1993. Name them as claims, not as descriptions.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the statement "after 1991 the United States was the world's only superpower" fail to answer the question "was there a new world order?", and what would a complete answer have to establish?

**Model answer:** Because it answers only the first of three tests. Being the only superpower is a statement about the distribution of capability - who is able to act - and the account confirms it, recording that the overriding fact was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower, and demonstrating it in the Gulf, where an international force of over 600,000 assembled with more than thirty nations contributing. An order, however, additionally requires rules governing how power may be used, legitimacy in the sense of acceptance by those who live under the arrangement, institutional consent, and the capacity to handle different kinds of conflict. A complete answer would therefore have to establish all four. On the account's own record the rules test is contested - the Gulf action rested on a Security Council authorisation, but the same account records the Panama operation of December 1989 with at least 2,000 civilians killed and an Organization of American States regret resolution carried 20 to 1, the rejection of an International Court judgment on the mining of Nicaraguan harbours, and repeated lone negative votes. The legitimacy test fails on the account's own framing, which asks how an era that seemed full of hope turned out to be full of hatred, and quotes Guyatt on resentment grounded in a real perception of the imbalance of power. And the capacity test is answered by Yugoslavia rather than by the Gulf.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting this into a general denunciation of American policy. The analytical claim is narrower and stronger: that a concentration of capability is not the same thing as an order, and that the announced programme of 11 September 1990 has to be tested against a record rather than accepted or rejected wholesale. The judgements in the account belong to named authors - Guyatt, Blum, Chomsky, Byers and Hutton - and an answer that reproduces them as its own opinion has converted evidence into assertion and lost the marks that attribution earns.

---

## Lesson 15 - The Gulf War, 1990-91: the strongest evidence for the order claim, and its limits

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Saddam Hussein's motives and miscalculation; the Security Council authorisation of 29 November 1990 and the deadline of 15 January 1991; the coalition and the two-phase campaign; the legal category of coalition enforcement against peacekeeping; the aftermath in which the regime survived; the no-fly zones; and the account's own judgement on the motives of the great powers

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Gulf War of 1990-91, covering Saddam Hussein's motives, the world uniting against him, the campaign and its aftermath, together with the United Nations chapter's treatment of the same war as an instance of peacekeeping since the end of the Cold War
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three categories that a close question will try to swap

```text
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+
  | COALITION ENFORCEMENT     | PEACEKEEPING              | ALLIANCE ACTION WITHOUT   |
  | UNDER COUNCIL AUTHORITY   |                           | EXPRESS AUTHORISATION     |
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+
  | Example: the Gulf, 1991   | Example: UNPROFOR in      | Example: NATO's Kosovo    |
  |                           | Bosnia from February 1992 | air campaign, 1999        |
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+
  | Who commands? Member      | Who commands? The United  | Who commands? The         |
  | states acting together    | Nations, through a force  | alliance's own command    |
  | under their own commands  | commander                 | structure                 |
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+
  | Legal basis: a Security   | Legal basis: consent of   | Legal basis: contested;   |
  | Council resolution        | the parties and a Council | no express Council        |
  | authorising "all          | mandate                   | authorisation for the use |
  | necessary means" after a  |                           | of force                  |
  | stated deadline           |                           |                           |
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+
  | Force used: to reverse    | Force used: normally only | Force used: to coerce a   |
  | an act of aggression      | in self-defence; the      | state into changing its   |
  |                           | Bosnian force "had no     | policy                    |
  |                           | supporting artillery or   |                           |
  |                           | aircraft"                 |                           |
  +---------------------------+---------------------------+---------------------------+

  THE ERROR TO AVOID: "the UN went to war with Iraq" and "the UN
  bombed Serbia". Neither is true, and each collapses two of the
  three columns into one.
```

### 1. The learner's problem: why does the legal category matter?

⚠️ Because the entire order claim rests on it. ⚠️ If the Gulf action was the United Nations fighting a war, then the post-1991 world had acquired a standing collective-security instrument, and the claim of Lesson 14 is largely vindicated. ⚠️ If it was member states enforcing under Council authority, then what existed was an unusual alignment of great-power interest with institutional permission - powerful, but contingent on the alignment recurring.

⚠️ The record supports the second reading, and Yugoslavia, beginning within months, shows how quickly the alignment failed to recur.

### 2. Motives and miscalculation

✅ "Even before he had accepted the peace terms at the end of the Iran-Iraq War, Saddam Hussein began his next act of aggression. His forces invaded and quickly occupied the small neighbouring state of Kuwait (August 1990)."

✅ Three motives are recorded.

1. ✅ "His real motive was probably to get his hands on the wealth of Kuwait, since he was seriously short of cash after the long war with Iran. Kuwait, though small, had valuable oil wells, which he would now be able to control." ⚠️ Note the account's "probably" and preserve it.
2. ✅ "He claimed that Kuwait was historically part of Iraq, though in fact Kuwait had existed as a separate territory - a British protectorate - since 1899, whereas Iraq had not been created until after the First World War." ⚠️ The refutation is chronological, and it is a neat instance of how boundary claims derived from history are tested.
3. ✅ "He did not expect any action from the outside world now that his troops were firmly entrenched in Kuwait, and he had the strongest army in the region. He thought Europe and the USA were reasonably amenable to him since they had supplied him with arms during his war with Iran."

✅ The account grounds that last expectation: "the USA had been supporting him all the way through his war against the Iranian regime that had overthrown the Shah, an American ally. The Americans valued him as a stabilizing influence within the region and in Iraq itself - they had taken no action when Saddam had suppressed the Shias, nor when he brutally crushed the Kurds (who were demanding an independent state) in the north of Iraq, in 1988."

⚠️ This is the most analytically important paragraph in the lesson. ⚠️ It establishes that the coalition's response was a **change of policy** rather than the application of a standing rule, and it therefore supplies the strongest evidence for the account's later verdict about motives. ⚠️ An answer that presents the Gulf response as the international community applying a consistent principle has omitted the previous decade.

### 3. The authorisation, the deadline and the coalition

✅ "President Bush of the USA took the lead in pressing for action to remove the Iraqis from Kuwait. The UN placed trade sanctions on Iraq, cutting off her oil exports, her main source of income. Saddam was ordered to remove his troops by 15 January 1991, after which the UN would use 'all necessary means' to clear them out."

⚠️ The resolution carrying that language was adopted on **29 November 1990**, authorising member states cooperating with the government of Kuwait to use all necessary means if Iraq did not withdraw by 15 January 1991. ⚠️ The exact formula - **member states cooperating with Kuwait**, not a United Nations force - is the whole of the category distinction, and quoting it is worth marks.

✅ The coalition: "Saddam hoped that this was all bluff and talked of 'the mother of all wars' if they tried to throw him out. But Bush and Margaret Thatcher had decided that Saddam's power must be curbed; he controlled too much of the oil that the industrial west needed. Fortunately for Britain and the USA, Saudi Arabia, Syria and Egypt were also nervous about what Saddam might do next, so they supported the UN action." ✅ "An international force of over 600 000 had been assembled in Saudi Arabia. More than thirty nations contributed with troops" and other support.

⚠️ Regional participation matters and is often omitted. ⚠️ Saudi, Syrian and Egyptian support converted the operation from a Western expedition into a broad coalition, and it did so because those states had their own security reasons - which is precisely the kind of interest alignment that cannot be assumed to recur.

### 4. The campaign and its two phases

✅ "Operation Desert Storm was launched against the Iraqis. The campaign in two parts, was quickly successful. First came a series of bombing attacks on Baghdad (the Iraqi capital), whose unfortunate citizens again suffered heavy casualties, and on military targets such as roads and bridges. The second phase, the attack on the Iraqi army itself, began on 24 February. Within four days the Iraqis had been driven out of Kuwait and routed. Kuwait was liberated and Saddam Hussein accepted defeat."

✅ The casualties, with their attribution intact: "although Iraq lost many troops (some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies), Saddam was allowed to withdraw with much of his army intact."

⚠️ **Attribution discipline.** ✅ The account says "some estimates put"; that qualifier is part of the fact and must travel with the figure. ❌ Do not write "90,000 Iraqis died" as an unowned statement.

✅ The reason for stopping: "The retreating Iraqis were at the mercy of the allies, but Bush called a ceasefire, afraid that if the slaughter continued, the allies would lose the support of the other Arab nations."

⚠️ That sentence is decisive for the order question. ⚠️ The war's termination was governed by **coalition maintenance**, which is a political constraint arising from the coalition's own composition. ⚠️ A standing collective-security instrument would not have had to weigh that consideration in the same way; a coalition of the willing always does.

### 5. The aftermath: why the war is evidence on both sides

✅ "It was widely expected outside Iraq that after this humiliating defeat, Saddam Hussein would soon be overthrown. There were uprisings of Kurds in the north and Shia Muslims in the south, and it seemed as though Iraq was breaking up. However, the allies had left Saddam enough troops, tanks and aircraft to deal with the situation, and both rebellions were ruthlessly crushed."

✅ The reasons for non-intervention are recorded and are all interest-based: "At first nobody intervened: Russia, Syria and Turkey had Kurdish minorities of their own and did not want the rebellion spreading over from Iraq. Similarly a Shiite victory in southern Iraq would probably increase the power of Iran in that region, and the USA did not want that."

✅ The partial correction: "But eventually world opinion became so outraged at Saddam's continued ruthless bombings of his people that the USA and Britain, with UN backing, declared the areas 'no-fly zones', and used their air power to keep Saddam's aircraft out. And so Saddam Hussein remained in power."

✅ And the account's own verdict, which is the single most quotable passage for a critical answer: "The war and its aftermath were very revealing about the motives of the West and the great powers. Their primary concern was not with international justice and moral questions of right and wrong, but with their own self-interest. They only took action against Saddam in the first place because they felt he was threatening their oil supplies. Often in the past when other small nations had been invaded, no international action had been taken. For example, when East Timor was occupied by neighbouring Indonesia in 1975, the rest of the world ignored it, because their interests were not threatened. After the Gulf War, Saddam, who on any assessment must rank as one of the most brutal dictators of the century, was allowed to remain in power because the West thought that his survival was the best way of keeping Iraq united and the region stable."

✅ The United Nations chapter records the same criticism in institutional terms: "UN action during the Gulf War of 1991 was impressive ... However, critics of the UN complained that Kuwait had received help only because the West needed her oil supplies; other small nations, which had no value to the West, had received no help when they were invaded by larger neighbours (for example East Timor, taken over by Indonesia in 1975)." ✅ And it adds a structural observation worth carrying to Lesson 17: "UN military interventions had most chance of success when, as in Korea in 1950-3 and the 1991 Gulf War, UN troops actively supported one side against the other."

⚠️ The East Timor comparison is the strongest available device for testing consistency, and it is supplied by the source itself rather than imported.

### Criticism and reply: does the self-interest verdict prove the order claim false?

⚠️ **The objection to the sceptical reading.** States always act from interest; requiring disinterested motives is requiring something no order has ever had. The Gulf action reversed an unambiguous act of aggression under a Council authorisation, with regional participation and a clear legal basis. That is what a functioning collective-security arrangement looks like in practice.

⚠️ **The reply.** The sceptical reading does not require disinterest; it requires **consistency**. ⚠️ An order is a set of rules applied to cases of the same kind. ⚠️ The evidence against consistency is supplied by the same source: no comparable action over East Timor in 1975; no action when the Kurds were crushed in 1988; and the deliberate decision after the war to leave in power a ruler the account calls one of the century's most brutal, because his survival suited regional stability.

⚠️ **The reply to the reply, which a strong answer will include.** Consistency is a demanding test that no international arrangement has ever passed, including the periods conventionally described as ordered. ⚠️ The honest formulation is therefore comparative rather than absolute: the Gulf case showed that **when** great-power interests aligned, the Council could authorise effective enforcement, and that was a genuine change from the Cold War decades in which the veto normally prevented it. ⚠️ What it did not show was that the alignment would recur when interests diverged - and Bosnia, beginning within months, is the test that settles the point.

⚠️ **The residual.** The evidence held here cannot settle whether a different post-war decision - continuing the advance, or supporting the risings - would have produced a better outcome. ⚠️ The account records the reasons for the choice without endorsing them, and this session does the same.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Paper II on international institutions, their structure and mandate; ✅ Paper I on international relations transformed after the Cold War.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Did the Gulf War demonstrate a durable post-Cold-War order?" is the standard evaluative stem; the answer is yes on capability and authorisation, no on consistency and durability.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Calling it a United Nations war or blue-helmet peacekeeping. ✅ The resolution authorised member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means; command was national and coalition, not United Nations.
2. ⚠️ Dropping the deadline. ✅ 15 January 1991 is the date after which force was authorised; the resolution was adopted on 29 November 1990.
3. ⚠️ Stating the casualty figure without its qualifier. ✅ "Some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies."
4. ⚠️ Omitting the previous decade. ✅ The same powers had supplied Saddam with arms and taken no action over the Shias or the Kurds in 1988.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting why the war stopped. ✅ Bush called a ceasefire fearing the loss of Arab support - a coalition-maintenance constraint.
6. ⚠️ Ignoring the aftermath. ✅ The risings were crushed, the no-fly zones followed, and Saddam Hussein remained in power.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Iraq invaded and occupied Kuwait in August 1990, before the Iran-Iraq peace terms had been accepted.
2. ✅ Motives: probably the wealth of Kuwait after a cash-draining war; a historical claim refuted by Kuwait's existence as a separate British protectorate since 1899 against Iraq's creation after the First World War; and an expectation of no outside action.
3. ✅ That expectation was grounded: the USA had supported him through the Iranian war and had taken no action over the Shias or over the Kurds in 1988.
4. ⚠️ The authorising resolution was adopted on 29 November 1990 and permitted member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means if Iraq had not withdrawn by 15 January 1991.
5. ✅ Trade sanctions cut off Iraq's oil exports; Saudi Arabia, Syria and Egypt supported the action from their own security concerns; an international force of over 600,000 assembled in Saudi Arabia with more than thirty contributing nations.
6. ✅ Operation Desert Storm had two phases: bombing of Baghdad and military targets, then the ground attack from 24 February; within four days Iraq was driven out of Kuwait.
7. ✅ Casualties, attributed: "some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies".
8. ✅ Bush called a ceasefire for fear of losing the support of the other Arab nations; Saddam withdrew with much of his army intact.
9. ✅ The Kurdish and Shia risings were crushed; Russia, Syria and Turkey feared Kurdish contagion and the USA feared Iranian gains; no-fly zones followed, with UN backing; Saddam Hussein remained in power.
10. ✅ The account's verdict: the primary concern of the West and the great powers "was not with international justice and moral questions of right and wrong, but with their own self-interest"; East Timor in 1975 is the comparison it supplies.
11. ✅ The United Nations chapter's structural observation: intervention had most chance of success when, as in Korea and the Gulf, its forces actively supported one side against the other.
12. ⚠️ The order verdict: the Gulf proved that when great-power interests aligned, the Council could authorise effective enforcement; it did not prove that alignment would recur.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the exact legal category of the 1991 Gulf action, quote the formula that establishes it, and explain why the category matters for the question of whether a new world order existed.

**Model answer:** The category is enforcement by member states acting under a Security Council authorisation, not United Nations peacekeeping. The formula that establishes it is the resolution of 29 November 1990 authorising member states cooperating with the government of Kuwait to use all necessary means if Iraq had not withdrawn by 15 January 1991; the account records the order to withdraw by that date, "after which the UN would use 'all necessary means' to clear them out", and records that the force assembled was an international coalition of over 600,000 from more than thirty nations under its own commands. The category matters because the order claim turns on it. If the action had been a United Nations force fighting under United Nations command, the post-1991 world would have acquired a standing collective-security instrument, and the claim announced on 11 September 1990 would be largely vindicated. Because it was a coalition authorised by the Council, what it demonstrated was that when great-power and regional interests aligned, the Council could permit effective enforcement - a real change from the Cold War decades in which the veto normally prevented it, but one contingent on the alignment recurring. That contingency is confirmed by the war's own termination, which the account attributes to Bush's fear of losing Arab support, and by Bosnia within months.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from the account's verdict on self-interest that the operation was therefore illegitimate or meaningless. The test that the evidence actually supports is consistency, not purity of motive: the same powers had armed Iraq during its war with Iran, had taken no action when the Kurds were crushed in 1988, had ignored East Timor in 1975, and afterwards left in place a ruler the account calls one of the most brutal of the century because his survival suited regional stability. That is an argument about the uneven application of rules, which is the correct form of the criticism, and it is much stronger than an argument about motives, which no international arrangement has ever passed.

---

## Lesson 16 - Yugoslavia: nationalism as political production, strictly attributed

Progress: 16/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the federal structure of six republics and two provinces and the collective presidency after Tito; the economic and political breakdown of the mid-1980s; Milosevic and Tudjman as named agents; the minority arithmetic of Croatia and Bosnia; the Serb-Croat war and UNPROFOR; the Bosnian war, ethnic cleansing and the safe areas; Srebrenica; the changes of 1995 and the Dayton settlement; and the strict attribution of every figure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on civil war in Yugoslavia, covering the federal structure, the collective presidency, the things that began to go wrong in the mid-1980s, the move to war, the Serb-Croat war, the war in Bosnia-Herzegovina and the five changes of 1995 leading to the Dayton settlement
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the structure, the actors, and the arithmetic that made compromise hard

```text
  THE FEDERAL DESIGN
  ==================
  Six republics: Serbia, Croatia, Montenegro, Slovenia,
                 Bosnia-Herzegovina, Macedonia
  Two provinces associated with Serbia: Vojvodina, Kosovo
  After Tito's death in 1980: a collective presidency of one
  representative from each republic and each province, with a
  different president of the council elected each year

  WHAT HELD IT TOGETHER UNDER TITO
  - nationalist feelings "kept strictly under control"
  - people "encouraged to think of themselves primarily as
    Yugoslavs rather than as Serbs or Croats"

  WHAT WENT WRONG FROM THE MID-1980s
  - inflation running at 90 per cent in 1986
  - unemployment over a million: 13 per cent of the working
    population
  - sharp regional differences: Slovenia "reasonably
    prosperous", parts of Serbia "poverty-stricken"

  THE MINORITY ARITHMETIC THAT MADE SECESSION DANGEROUS
  - about 600,000 Serbs in Croatia: about 15 per cent
  - about 1.3 million Serbs in Bosnia-Herzegovina: roughly a
    third
  - Bosnia's population: 44 per cent Muslim, 33 per cent Serb,
    17 per cent Croat

  THE NAMED AGENTS
  - Milosevic, president of Serbia from 1988: "bears much of
    the responsibility for the tragedy that followed"; he
    "deliberately stirred up Serbian nationalist feelings to
    increase his own popularity", using Kosovo, claiming the
    Serbian minority there were being terrorized "though there
    was no definite evidence of this"
  - Tudjman, "former communist and now leader of the right-wing
    Croatian Democratic Union and president of Croatia", who
    "did all he could to stir up Croatian nationalism"

  THE SENTENCE THAT DEFEATS THE "ANCIENT HATREDS" ANSWER
  "War was not inevitable: with statesmanlike leaders prepared
   to make sensible concessions, peaceful solutions could have
   been found."
```

### 1. The learner's problem: the "ancient hatreds" temptation

⚠️ The lazy answer writes that Yugoslavia's peoples had hated each other for centuries and that communism merely froze the hatred until 1991. ⚠️ It is attractive because it is simple, and it is wrong in a way the source itself refutes.

✅ The account records that under Tito the different nationalities "lived peacefully together, and had apparently succeeded in putting behind them memories of the atrocities committed during the Second World War" - and it gives an example of what those memories contained, recording that Croat and Muslim supporters of the fascist regime set up by the Italians to rule Croatia and Bosnia during the war "were responsible for the murder of some 700 000 Serbs". ⚠️ That figure is the account's own attributed estimate and must be written with "some" and with its source; ❌ it may not be restated as a plain number.

⚠️ Notice what the passage establishes. ⚠️ Far worse memories than any available in 1990 had been politically neutralised for decades. ⚠️ So the question is not why hatred existed but **why it was reactivated, by whom, and for what purpose** - and the account answers all three.

### 2. Structural breakdown before political mobilisation

⚠️ Two things had to fail before leaders could use nationalism successfully.

- ⚠️ **The economy.** ✅ Inflation at 90 per cent in 1986; over a million unemployed, 13 per cent of the working population; and sharp regional inequality, Slovenia reasonably prosperous while parts of Serbia were poverty-stricken. ⚠️ Regional inequality inside a federation converts every distributive question into a national one.
- ⚠️ **The federal executive.** ✅ Tito "had left careful plans for the country to be ruled by a collective presidency after his death", one representative from each republic and each province with a rotating annual presidency, and "although the collective leadership seemed to work well at first, in the mid-1980s things began to go wrong". ⚠️ A rotating collective executive can manage routine business but cannot impose a distributive settlement, because no member can be outvoted into accepting a loss without a national grievance being created.

⚠️ The combination is the precondition: a federation with worsening and unequally distributed economic pain and an executive incapable of deciding.

### 3. The agents, named, and what they actually did

⚠️ The account's insistence on naming is the difference between a strong answer and a weak one.

✅ **Milosevic** "became president of Serbia in 1988" and "bears much of the responsibility for the tragedy that followed. He deliberately stirred up Serbian nationalist feelings to increase his own popularity, using the situation in Kosovo. He claimed that the Serbian minority in Kosovo were being terrorized by the Albanian majority, though there was no definite evidence of this. The Serbian government's hardline treatment of the Albanians led to protest demonstrations and the first outbreaks of violence."

✅ "Milosevic remained in power after the first free elections in Serbia in 1990, having successfully convinced the voters that he was now a nationalist and not a communist. He wanted to preserve the united federal state of Yugoslavia, but intended that Serbia should be the dominant republic."

✅ **Tudjman**: "former communist and now leader of the right-wing Croatian Democratic Union and president of Croatia. He did all he could to stir up Croatian nationalism and wanted an independent state of Croatia." ✅ Earlier, under Tito, "some Croat nationalist leaders, such as Franjo Tudjman, were given spells in jail."

✅ The minority question: "each republic had ethnic minorities: there were about 600 000 Serbs living in Croatia - about 15 per cent of the population - and about 1.3 million Serbs in Bosnia-Herzegovina - roughly a third of the population. Tudjman would give no guarantees to the Serbs of Croatia, and this gave Serbia the excuse to announce that she would defend all Serbs forced to live under Croatian rule."

⚠️ Three analytical observations.

1. ⚠️ **Both leaders were former communists who converted to nationalism.** ⚠️ That is decisive evidence against primordialism: the mobilising elites were products of the federal communist system, not survivors of an older ethnic politics.
2. ⚠️ **The grievance was manufactured in one documented case.** ✅ The Kosovo terror claim had "no definite evidence". ⚠️ Naming an unevidenced claim as the mobilising device is the single strongest move available in an answer on this subtopic.
3. ⚠️ **The refusal of minority guarantees supplied the pretext.** ⚠️ Secession without guarantees for a large resident minority converts a constitutional question into a security question, and the account shows Serbia using exactly that conversion.

✅ And the account's own counterfactual, which should end any paragraph on causes: "War was not inevitable: with statesmanlike leaders prepared to make sensible concessions, peaceful solutions could have been found. But clearly, if Yugoslavia broke up, with men like Milosevic and Tudjman in power, there was little chance of a peaceful future."

### 4. The wars, in sequence

**Slovenia and Croatia, 1991.** ✅ "Crisis-point was reached in June 1991 when Slovenia and Croatia declared themselves independent, against the wishes of Serbia." ✅ "Civil war was avoided in Slovenia mainly because there were very few Serbs living there. The EC was able to act as mediator, and secured the withdrawal of Yugoslav troops from Slovenia."

⚠️ Slovenia is the control case in the whole Yugoslav argument: same federation, same year, same declaration - and almost no violence, because the minority arithmetic was different.

✅ Croatia: "Serbian troops invaded the eastern area of Croatia (eastern Slavonia) where many Serbs lived, and other towns and cities, including Dubrovnik on the Dalmatian coast, were shelled. By the end of August 1991 they had captured about one-third of the country. Only then, having captured all the territory he wanted, did Milosevic agree to a ceasefire. A UN force of 13 000 troops - UNPROFOR - was sent to police the ceasefire (February 1992). By this time the international community had recognized the independence of Slovenia, Croatia and Bosnia-Herzegovina."

**Bosnia, from March 1992.** ✅ "Bosnia declared itself independent under the presidency of the Muslim Alija Izetbegovic (March 1992). The EC recognized its independence, making the same mistake as it had done with Croatia - it failed to make sure that the new government guaranteed fair treatment for its minorities."

⚠️ That is the account's own judgement on the external error, and it is the most quotable external-responsibility sentence available. ⚠️ Recognition without conditions on minority protection is identified as a mistake **repeated**, which is a stronger charge than a single misjudgement.

✅ "The Bosnian Serbs rejected the new constitution and objected to a Muslim president. Fighting soon broke out between Bosnian Serbs, who received help and encouragement from Serbia, and Bosnian Muslims. The Serbs hoped that a large strip of land in the east of Bosnia, which bordered onto Serbia, could break away from the Muslim-dominated Bosnia and become part of Serbia. At the same time Croatia attacked and occupied areas in the north of Bosnia where most of the Bosnian Croats lived."

✅ On atrocity, with the account's own distribution of responsibility: "Atrocities were committed by all sides, but it seemed that the Bosnian Serbs were the most guilty. They carried out 'ethnic cleansing', which meant driving out the Muslim civilian population from Serb-majority areas, putting them into camps, and in some cases murdering all the men. Such barbarism had not been seen in Europe since the Nazi treatment of the Jews during the Second World War. Sarajevo, the capital of Bosnia, was besieged and shelled by the Serbs, and throughout the country there was chaos: two million refugees had been driven out of their homes by 'ethnic cleansing' and not enough food and medical supplies were available."

⚠️ **Attribution discipline, applied strictly.** ⚠️ "Atrocities were committed by all sides, but it seemed that the Bosnian Serbs were the most guilty" is the account's formulation and must be reproduced with both halves. ⚠️ The two-million refugee figure is the account's; ❌ do not adjust it. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed here to named forces and leaderships, never to a people.

✅ The international failure: "The UN force, UNPROFOR, did its best to distribute aid, but its job was very difficult because it had no supporting artillery or aircraft. Later the UN tried to protect the Muslims by declaring Srebrenica, Zepa and Gorazde, three mainly Muslim towns in the Serb-majority region, as 'safe areas'; but not enough troops were provided to defend them if the Serbs decided to attack. The EC was reluctant to send any troops and the Americans felt that Europe should be able to sort out its own problems."

⚠️ The phrase to memorise is "safe areas" without the means to make them safe. ⚠️ It is the precise institutional failure: a declaratory guarantee unsupported by capability, which is worse than no guarantee because it concentrates the vulnerable population in known locations.

### 5. 1995: why the war ended when it did

✅ "During 1995 crucial changes took place which enabled a peace agreement to be signed in November. Serb behaviour eventually proved too much for the international community." ✅ Three triggers are recorded: renewed bombardment of Sarajevo in May after a promise to withdraw heavy weapons; the seizure of United Nations peacekeepers as hostages to deter air strikes; and the capture of Srebrenica and Zepa, "two of the UN 'safe areas', and at Srebrenica they committed perhaps the ultimate act of barbarism, killing about 8000 Muslims in a terrible final burst of 'ethnic cleansing' (July)."

⚠️ **On Srebrenica.** ✅ The account's figure is "about 8000 Muslims", with its own qualifier. ⚠️ The killing was judicially characterised as genocide by the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004. ⚠️ State both, attribute both, and add nothing further.

✅ The five changes that followed: Croats and Muslims, who had signed a ceasefire in 1994, agreed to fight together, recapturing western Slavonia in May and the Krajina in August; a London conference attended by the Americans agreed to use air strikes and a rapid reaction force; after a single mortar shell struck the Markale market in Sarajevo on 28 August - ✅ the account records 27 people killed, while the standard verified toll for that shelling is 43 killed and 75 wounded, and the two figures are kept separate here rather than merged - massive air strikes followed "until they agreed to move their weapons away from Sarajevo", and "the UN position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation"; the Bosnian Serb leaders Radovan Karadzic and General Mladic had been indicted for war crimes, which discredited them and allowed Milosevic to represent the Serbs at the table; and Milosevic himself "had now had enough of the war and wanted to get the economic sanctions on his country lifted."

✅ The settlement: a peace conference at Dayton, Ohio in November and a treaty formally signed in Paris in December 1995. ✅ Its terms: Bosnia to remain one state with a single elected parliament and president and a unified Sarajevo as capital; two entities, a Bosnian Muslim-Croat federation and a Bosnian Serb republic; Gorazde to remain in Muslim hands linked to Sarajevo by a corridor; all indicted war criminals banned from public life; all Bosnian refugees, "over two million of them", with the right to return and freedom of movement; 60,000 NATO troops to police the settlement; and an understanding that economic sanctions on Serbia would be lifted.

✅ The account's verdict: "There was general relief at the peace, though there were no real winners, and the settlement was full of problems."

⚠️ Three conclusions to carry forward.

1. ⚠️ **What ended the war was the alignment of a battlefield change, an atrocity that exhausted tolerance, an alliance willing to use force, and a Serbian interest in sanctions relief.** ⚠️ Not a rule, and not an institution acting on principle.
2. ⚠️ **The command shift is examinable.** ✅ "The UN position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation." ⚠️ That sentence links directly to Lesson 17.
3. ⚠️ **A settlement that ends a war is not a settlement that resolves it.** ✅ Dayton left Kosovo untouched, which is where Lesson 17 begins.

### Comparative note: why Czechoslovakia divided peacefully and Yugoslavia did not

| ⚠️ Variable | ✅ Czechoslovakia, 1992 | ✅ Yugoslavia, 1991-95 |
|---|---|---|
| Minority arithmetic across the proposed border | No large co-national minority stranded on the wrong side of the line | About 600,000 Serbs in Croatia and about 1.3 million in Bosnia; Bosnia itself 44 per cent Muslim, 33 per cent Serb, 17 per cent Croat |
| Leadership choices | Negotiated separation; "a peaceful settlement was worked out" | Leaders who "deliberately stirred up" national feeling, one using an unevidenced claim about Kosovo |
| Economic stress | Real but not accompanied by 90 per cent inflation and 13 per cent unemployment | Inflation at 90 per cent in 1986, over a million unemployed, sharp regional inequality |
| Army | No comparable federal army with a national identification | A federal army "mainly Serbian" already deployed in the seceding republics |
| External handling | Not a recognition crisis | ✅ Recognition granted without securing minority guarantees - "the same mistake as it had done with Croatia" |

⚠️ The comparison is the single most effective device against inevitability, and both cases come from the same source and the same year-range.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The redrawal of national boundaries; ✅ political philosophies and their effect on society, since what collapsed was a federal socialist state; ✅ and Paper II on institutions, through the peacekeeping failure.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Discuss the breakup of Yugoslavia as a post-Cold-War crisis of nationalism and state failure." ⚠️ "Why did the breakup of Yugoslavia become the most violent crisis of post-Cold-War Europe?"

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ The ancient-hatreds answer. ✅ The same peoples had lived peacefully for decades after far worse memories, and the account says war was not inevitable.
2. ⚠️ Omitting names. ✅ Milosevic from 1988 and Tudjman are the agents the account identifies; an answer without them has no actors to assess.
3. ⚠️ Rounding, totalling, updating or silently replacing any figure. ✅ "Some 700 000 Serbs" murdered in the Second World War; "two million refugees"; "about 8000 Muslims" at Srebrenica; "27 people" by a single mortar shell on 28 August, against a standard verified toll of 43 for that same Markale shelling. ⚠️ Each travels with its qualifier and its source, and where two figures exist for one event both are named rather than one being quietly substituted for the other.
4. ⚠️ Attributing atrocity to a people. ✅ The account says "atrocities were committed by all sides, but it seemed that the Bosnian Serbs were the most guilty"; use both halves and attribute to forces and leaderships.
5. ⚠️ Treating recognition as neutral. ✅ The account calls the failure to secure minority guarantees a mistake, and a repeated one.
6. ⚠️ Confusing Dayton with a resolution. ✅ It ended the Bosnian war, left Kosovo unaddressed, and "the settlement was full of problems".

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Yugoslavia was formed after the First World War from pre-war Serbia, territory gained from Turkey in 1913 containing many Muslims, and territory taken from the defeated Habsburg Empire; six republics and two provinces associated with Serbia.
2. ✅ Under Tito nationalist feelings were kept strictly under control and people were encouraged to think of themselves primarily as Yugoslavs; after his death in 1980 a collective presidency rotated annually.
3. ✅ The account's attributed wartime figure: Croat and Muslim supporters of the Italian-installed fascist regime were responsible for the murder of "some 700 000 Serbs" - evidence that far worse memories had been politically neutralised.
4. ✅ Mid-1980s breakdown: inflation 90 per cent in 1986; over a million unemployed, 13 per cent of the workforce; Slovenia prosperous while parts of Serbia were poverty-stricken.
5. ✅ Milosevic, president of Serbia from 1988, "bears much of the responsibility", deliberately stirred Serbian nationalism using Kosovo, claiming terror against the Serbian minority "though there was no definite evidence of this", and won the 1990 free elections as a nationalist.
6. ✅ Tudjman, a former communist jailed under Tito, led the Croatian Democratic Union, stirred Croatian nationalism and refused guarantees to Croatia's Serbs, which gave Serbia its stated excuse.
7. ✅ Minority arithmetic: about 600,000 Serbs in Croatia, about 15 per cent; about 1.3 million in Bosnia, roughly a third; Bosnia 44 per cent Muslim, 33 per cent Serb, 17 per cent Croat.
8. ✅ June 1991: Slovenia and Croatia declared independence; Slovenia escaped war largely because few Serbs lived there and the Community mediated a troop withdrawal.
9. ✅ Serbian forces took about a third of Croatia by the end of August 1991, shelling Dubrovnik among other places; a ceasefire followed; UNPROFOR, 13,000 troops, deployed in February 1992.
10. ✅ Bosnia declared independence in March 1992 under Izetbegovic; the Community recognised it without securing minority guarantees - "the same mistake as it had done with Croatia".
11. ✅ Ethnic cleansing, camps, the siege and shelling of Sarajevo, two million refugees; UNPROFOR had no supporting artillery or aircraft; Srebrenica, Zepa and Gorazde were declared safe areas without the troops to defend them.
12. ✅ 1995: renewed shelling of Sarajevo in May; peacekeepers seized as hostages; Srebrenica and Zepa captured in July with about 8,000 Muslims killed - characterised as genocide by the tribunal in its 2004 appeal judgement in the Krstic case.
13. ✅ The five changes: the Croat-Muslim alliance and the recapture of western Slavonia and the Krajina; the London conference agreeing air strikes and a rapid reaction force; massive air strikes after the Markale market shelling of 28 August, for which the account records 27 killed while the standard verified toll is 43 killed and 75 wounded, with NATO now running the operation; the indictment of Karadzic and Mladic leaving Milosevic to represent the Serbs; and Milosevic's wish to have sanctions lifted.
14. ✅ Dayton, November 1995, signed in Paris in December: one state, two entities, a unified Sarajevo, Gorazde linked by a corridor, indicted war criminals banned from public life, over two million refugees with the right to return, 60,000 NATO troops, and sanctions relief understood. "There were no real winners."

### Concept check

**Question:** Using two pieces of evidence from this lesson, refute the claim that Yugoslavia's wars were the inevitable result of ancient ethnic hatreds.

**Model answer:** The first piece of evidence is the record of the Tito period. Under communism the different nationalities lived peacefully together and had apparently succeeded in putting behind them the memories of the Second World War, and the account records what those memories contained: Croat and Muslim supporters of the fascist regime installed by the Italians were responsible for the murder of some 700,000 Serbs. Far worse grievances than anything available in 1990 had therefore been politically neutralised for decades, which means the hatreds were not a standing cause but something that had to be reactivated. The second piece of evidence is Slovenia. It declared independence in June 1991 in the same month and against the same opposition as Croatia, and civil war was avoided there mainly because very few Serbs lived in Slovenia and the Community was able to mediate a withdrawal of federal troops. Same federation, same year, same act, different minority arithmetic, different outcome. Together the two pieces show that what produced war was the deliberate mobilisation of national feeling by named leaders - Milosevic from 1988 using a Kosovo claim for which there was no definite evidence, and Tudjman refusing guarantees to Croatia's Serbs - operating on a particular distribution of populations across proposed borders. The account states the conclusion itself: war was not inevitable, and with statesmanlike leaders prepared to make sensible concessions, peaceful solutions could have been found.

**Misconception to avoid:** Replacing one single-cause answer with another by blaming the leaders alone. The mobilisation worked because the economy had broken down - 90 per cent inflation in 1986, over a million unemployed at 13 per cent of the workforce, and sharp regional inequality - because the rotating collective presidency could not impose a distributive settlement, because large co-national minorities were stranded across proposed borders, and because outside recognition was granted without securing guarantees for them, which the account calls a repeated mistake. Agency operated on structure; naming only one of them halves the answer.

---

## Lesson 17 - Kosovo 1999: humanitarian intervention, authorisation and sovereignty

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why Dayton left Kosovo unsettled; Rugova's non-violent protest from 1989 and the emergence of the Kosovo Liberation Army; the offensive of spring 1999; NATO's air campaign and the authorisation dispute; the escalation of expulsion during the bombing; the June 1999 agreement, the international force and the civil administration; Milosevic's indictment and downfall; and the three-sided debate the case generates

CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: one case, three defensible positions

  Kosovo"; peaceful      post-1945 rules were    which drove hundreds

  impression on          grant itself a          of Kosovo and into
