⚠️ The value of the three-position layout is that an answer can occupy any one of them and still be strong, provided it states the other two and says why it rejects them. ⚠️ An answer that states only one is not answering a "critically examine" directive.

### 1. Why Kosovo was left over

✅ "There was still the problem of Kosovo, where the Albanian majority bitterly resented Milosevic's hardline policies and the loss of much of their local provincial autonomy. Non-violent protests began as early as 1989, led by Ibrahim Rugova. The sensational events in Bosnia diverted attention away from the Kosovo situation, which was largely ignored during the peace negotiations in the USA in 1995."

⚠️ Three points, each worth a clause in an answer.

1. ⚠️ **Kosovo was a province of Serbia, not a republic.** ⚠️ In the federal design taught in Lesson 16, republics had a route to independence and provinces did not, which is why the same grievance produced a different legal question here.
2. ⚠️ **The grievance was constitutional before it was violent.** ✅ The loss of provincial autonomy came first, and protest under Rugova was non-violent from 1989.
3. ⚠️ **Omission from Dayton was a choice with consequences.** ✅ Kosovo "was largely ignored during the peace negotiations in the USA in 1995". ⚠️ A settlement that addresses the war being fought and not the one being prepared is a partial settlement, and this is the clearest case of it in the topic.

### 2. Escalation, and the failure of non-violence

✅ "Since peaceful protest made no impression on Milosevic, more radical Albanian elements came to the forefront with the formation of the Kosovo Liberation Army (KLA). By 1998 the situation had reached the proportions of civil war, as the Serb government security forces tried to suppress the KLA. In the spring of 1999 Serb forces unleashed a full-scale offensive, committing atrocities against the Albanians. These were widely reported abroad and the world's attention at last focused on Kosovo."

⚠️ The sequence is a general mechanism and should be named as one: ⚠️ where a non-violent movement obtains no response over nearly a decade, the political space it occupies is taken by an armed alternative; the state then treats the whole population as a security problem; and the conflict becomes one in which outside actors are eventually drawn in by reporting. ⚠️ Every stage of that chain is in the account's own wording.

⚠️ Note also the causal role of publicity: ✅ the atrocities "were widely reported abroad and the world's attention at last focused on Kosovo". ⚠️ Attention, not principle, is what changed - which is the same finding as in the Gulf and Rwanda cases of Lessons 14 and 15, and it is worth pointing out that the account supplies it three times.

### 3. The intervention and the authorisation problem

✅ "When peace negotiations broke down, the international community decided that something must be done to protect the Albanians of Kosovo. NATO forces carried out controversial bombing attacks against Serbia, hoping to force Milosevic to give way."

⚠️ The standard facts, held as part of the declared connecting set: the air campaign, named Operation Allied Force, ran from 24 March to 10 June 1999, and it was conducted without an express Security Council authorisation for the use of force. ⚠️ That absence is precisely why the account calls the attacks "controversial", and it is the reason this case sits in the third column of Lesson 15's category table.

⚠️ **Why the authorisation was absent.** ⚠️ Two permanent members had stated objections, and a resolution authorising force would not have passed. ⚠️ The alliance therefore acted on its own decision. ⚠️ Whether that is a defensible response to a deadlocked Council or a breach of the Charter is the exact question a "critically examine" directive on this topic is asking.

⚠️ **The strongest argument on each side, stated fairly.**

| ⚠️ For the intervention | ⚠️ Against the intervention |
|---|---|
| A decade of non-violent protest had produced nothing; the Bosnian precedent showed that delay was paid for in civilian lives, and the account records the safe-areas failure in detail | The rule against the use of force without Council authorisation exists precisely to prevent strong states from judging their own cause, and a humanitarian exception available only to those with the capability is not a rule at all |
| The Council was deadlocked for reasons unrelated to the merits; an institution that cannot act does not thereby prohibit action | Deadlock is not a malfunction of the Council but its design: the veto keeps major powers inside the system, and bypassing it weakens the system for every future case |
| The outcome was the return of most Albanian refugees and an internationally supervised administration | ✅ The immediate effect was that the campaign "only made him more determined" and was followed by expulsions on a far larger scale |

### 4. The escalation during the campaign, recorded without softening

✅ "However, this only made him more determined: he ordered a campaign of ethnic cleansing which drove hundreds of thousands of ethnic Albanians out of Kosovo and into the neighbouring states of Albania, Macedonia and Montenegro."

⚠️ **Attribution discipline.** ✅ "Hundreds of thousands" is the account's formulation; ❌ do not substitute a number. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to a named leader's order.

⚠️ This fact must appear in any serious answer, and it cuts in more than one direction. ⚠️ For critics of the intervention it is the central charge: the action intended to protect civilians was followed by their expulsion on a far larger scale. ⚠️ For defenders it is evidence of the intention that the campaign was designed to stop, brought forward rather than caused. ⚠️ The evidence held here does not settle which reading is right, and an honest answer says so while still taking a position.

### 5. Termination, settlement and aftermath

✅ "NATO air strikes continued, and by June 1999, with his country's economy in ruins, Milosevic accepted a peace agreement worked out by Russia and Finland. He was forced to withdraw all Serb troops from Kosovo; many of the Serb civilian population, afraid of Albanian reprisals, went with them. Most of the Albanian refugees were then able to return to Kosovo. A UN and NATO force of over 40 000 arrived to keep the peace, while UNMIK (UN Mission to Kosovo) was to supervise the administration of the country until its own government was capable of taking over."

⚠️ Two details deserve attention. ⚠️ First, the settlement was **mediated by Russia and Finland** - that is, by a permanent member that had opposed the campaign and a neutral European state. ⚠️ An answer that presents the outcome as a simple alliance victory has missed the diplomatic composition of the ending. ⚠️ Second, the Council did supply the framework for the aftermath: its resolution of 10 June 1999 authorised the international civil and security presences, which is why the peace operation was lawful even though the air campaign's basis was contested. ⚠️ That split - contested force, authorised aftermath - is itself one of the most examinable features of the case.

✅ The account also records the unsatisfactory afterlife: "At the end of 2003 there were still 20 000 peacekeeping troops there, and the Kosovars were becoming impatient, complaining of poverty, unemployment, and corruption among the members of UNMIK."

✅ Milosevic's own end is recorded: constitutionally barred from a third term as president of Serbia, he had the federal parliament appoint him president of Yugoslavia in 1997, "though Yugoslavia by then consisted only of Serbia and Montenegro"; ✅ in May 1999 he was indicted by the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia at The Hague for crimes against international law committed by federal troops in Kosovo; ✅ public opinion turned against him during 2000 over economic difficulties, food and fuel shortages and inflation; ✅ he lost the September 2000 presidential election to Vojislav Kostunica, a constitutional court annulled the result, mass demonstrations in Belgrade followed, crowds stormed the federal parliament and took the television stations, and he conceded; ✅ he was arrested in 2001 and handed to the tribunal, conducted his own defence from July 2001, and "no verdict had been reached when he died in March 2006."

✅ And the aftermath in Serbia: an empty treasury, an economy ruined by years of sanctions, rampant inflation and a fuel crisis; a sharp fall in living standards; the anti-Milosevic parties falling out; and in the elections at the end of 2003 "the extreme nationalist Serbian Radicals emerged as the largest single party", their leader then in custody at The Hague awaiting trial. ✅ "The election result was a great disappointment to the USA and the EU, which were both hoping that extreme Serb nationalism had been eradicated." ✅ In July 2008 Radovan Karadzic "was arrested after 13 years in hiding and sent to The Hague to be tried for war crimes."

⚠️ The 2003 Serbian election result is the single best piece of evidence in this session against the assumption that removing a leader removes the politics he mobilised.

### Criticism and reply: does Kosovo show that humanitarian intervention works?

⚠️ **The case that it does.** The expulsions were reversed, most refugees returned, an internationally supervised administration was installed, the responsible leader was indicted and eventually delivered to a tribunal, and the war ended within eleven weeks.

⚠️ **The case that it does not.** ✅ The immediate effect of the bombing was a far larger expulsion. ✅ Four years later there were still 20,000 peacekeepers and the population complained of poverty, unemployment and corruption in the international administration. ✅ Extreme nationalism was not eradicated and won the largest share in Serbia in 2003. ⚠️ And the precedent created - force without Council authorisation, justified by humanitarian necessity - is available to any state strong enough to use it and to characterise its own cause.

⚠️ **The reply that produces a defensible verdict.** ⚠️ Separate three questions that the word "works" merges: did it achieve its stated objective (largely, within months); did it do so at an acceptable immediate cost (contested, given the expulsions during the campaign); and did it establish a rule that will be applied consistently (no, on the evidence of Rwanda in 1994 and East Timor in 1975 recorded elsewhere in this session). ⚠️ A candidate who separates those three and answers each has written a 20-mark answer.

⚠️ **The residual, stated honestly.** ⚠️ This session cannot establish what would have happened in Kosovo without the campaign, and it does not pretend to. ⚠️ The counterfactual is the crux of the whole intervention debate and it is unavailable.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Paper II on international institutions, their structure and mandate; ✅ Paper I on the transformation of international relations and on the redrawal of boundaries.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Any stem on the limits of the post-Cold-War order, on the United Nations after 1991, or on NATO's changing role routes here.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the United Nations bombed Serbia. ✅ NATO carried out the attacks; the Council had not expressly authorised force.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the escalation during the campaign. ✅ Hundreds of thousands were driven out after the bombing began, and any honest answer records it.
3. ⚠️ Substituting a number for "hundreds of thousands". ⚠️ Keep the account's wording.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the ending as a pure alliance victory. ✅ The agreement was worked out by Russia and Finland, and the Council authorised the subsequent presences on 10 June 1999.
5. ⚠️ Treating Milosevic's fall as the end of the politics. ✅ The Serbian Radicals were the largest single party in the elections at the end of 2003.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting that Kosovo was omitted from Dayton. ⚠️ That omission is the beginning of the lesson's causal chain.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Kosovo's Albanian majority resented Milosevic's hardline policies and the loss of much of their local provincial autonomy; non-violent protest under Ibrahim Rugova began as early as 1989.
2. ✅ Kosovo "was largely ignored during the peace negotiations in the USA in 1995" while attention was on Bosnia.
3. ✅ Peaceful protest made no impression, so more radical elements formed the Kosovo Liberation Army; by 1998 the situation had reached the proportions of civil war.
4. ✅ In the spring of 1999 Serb forces unleashed a full-scale offensive committing atrocities against the Albanians, widely reported abroad.
5. ✅ NATO carried out "controversial bombing attacks against Serbia" after negotiations broke down; ⚠️ the campaign, Operation Allied Force, ran from 24 March to 10 June 1999 without an express Security Council authorisation of force.
6. ✅ The immediate effect was that Milosevic "only made him more determined" and ordered ethnic cleansing that drove hundreds of thousands of ethnic Albanians into Albania, Macedonia and Montenegro.
7. ✅ By June 1999, with the economy in ruins, he accepted an agreement worked out by Russia and Finland; Serb troops withdrew and many Serb civilians left with them; most Albanian refugees returned.
8. ✅ A UN and NATO force of over 40,000 deployed and the UN mission supervised the administration; ⚠️ the Council resolution of 10 June 1999 provided the framework for those presences.
9. ✅ At the end of 2003 there were still 20,000 peacekeeping troops and complaints of poverty, unemployment and corruption in the international administration.
10. ✅ Milosevic was indicted by the tribunal in May 1999; lost the September 2000 election to Kostunica; the result was annulled; demonstrations and the storming of the parliament forced his concession; he was arrested in 2001, conducted his own defence from July 2001, and died in March 2006 with no verdict reached.
11. ✅ Serbia after him: an empty treasury, sanctions-ruined economy, inflation and a fuel crisis; the extreme nationalist Serbian Radicals were the largest single party at the end of 2003, disappointing the USA and the EU; Karadzic was arrested in July 2008 after thirteen years in hiding.
12. ⚠️ Three questions to separate when assessing intervention: did it achieve its objective; at what immediate cost; and did it establish a consistently applied rule.

### Concept check

**Question:** Kosovo is usually offered as the defining case of humanitarian intervention. State the strongest argument on each side using evidence from this lesson, and explain why the case cannot settle the general question.

**Model answer:** The strongest argument for is that every alternative had been tried and had failed: Albanian protest had been non-violent under Rugova from 1989 and made no impression on Milosevic, the situation had reached the proportions of civil war by 1998, Serb forces unleashed a full-scale offensive committing atrocities in the spring of 1999, negotiations had broken down, and the Bosnian precedent had already shown what delay produced - safe areas declared at Srebrenica, Zepa and Gorazde without enough troops to defend them. The strongest argument against is twofold: the campaign was conducted without an express Security Council authorisation for the use of force, which is precisely the rule that prevents strong states from judging their own cause, and its immediate effect was the opposite of its purpose, since the account records that it only made Milosevic more determined and that he ordered a campaign of ethnic cleansing which drove hundreds of thousands of ethnic Albanians into Albania, Macedonia and Montenegro. The case cannot settle the general question because the decisive counterfactual - what would have happened in Kosovo without the campaign - is unavailable, and because a rule applied in one case and not in comparable ones is not yet a rule: the same source records reluctance in Rwanda in 1994 because no direct interests were involved, after which some half a million people were massacred, and no action over East Timor in 1975.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the outcome as a simple alliance victory that vindicates the method. The war was ended by an agreement worked out by Russia and Finland, the Council then authorised the civil and security presences on 10 June 1999, four years later 20,000 peacekeepers remained amid complaints of poverty, unemployment and corruption in the international administration, and extreme Serb nationalism was not eradicated - the Serbian Radicals were the largest single party at the end of 2003. Contested force with an authorised aftermath and an unresolved politics is a more accurate description than victory.

---

## Lesson 18 - European integration: peace project into enlargement project

Progress: 18/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the Coal and Steel Community of 1951 and the Treaty of Rome of 1957; the first enlargement of 1973 and the successive accessions; the Lome Convention, direct elections and the exchange-rate mechanism; the 1986 changes and the sovereignty argument; the common agricultural policy and its costs; Maastricht in December 1991 and its terms; the Social Chapter dispute; Amsterdam, Nice and the failed constitution; the eastern enlargement of 1 May 2004; and the crisis after 2008

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's sections on the growth of unity in western Europe, the Community from 1973 to Maastricht, Europe since Maastricht and the European Union in crisis, together with its map of the Community's growth
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one institution, four successive purposes

```text
  PURPOSE 1: SECURITY BY ECONOMIC MEANS            1951-57
  ---------------------------------------------------------
  European Coal and Steel Community set up 1951,
  "the brainchild of Robert Schuman"; pooling the
  industries of war so that renewed Franco-German
  war becomes materially harder
  Treaty of Rome 1957: six founder members - France,
  West Germany, Italy, the Netherlands, Belgium and
  Luxembourg - create the European Economic Community
  1967: the EEC, the ECSC and EURATOM formally merge

  PURPOSE 2: A MARKET BARGAIN, AND WIDENING          1960-86
  ---------------------------------------------------------
  Britain organises a rival European Free Trade
  Association in 1960, "no question of common economic
  policies and no supranational authority", then applies
  1 January 1973: Britain, Ireland and Denmark join -
  "the Six becomes the Nine"; widening begins to compete
  with deepening
  1975 Lome Convention: duty-free entry for goods from
  over 40 African and Caribbean states, mostly former
  colonies, plus promised aid
  1979: first direct elections, 410 members; the
  exchange-rate mechanism links currencies
  1981 Greece; 1986 Portugal and Spain - membership 12,
  population over 320 million
  1986 changes: a completely free internal market by 1992;
  majority voting on many issues; more powers for the
  parliament - and the sovereignty row that followed

  PURPOSE 3: DEEPENING, AND CONTAINING A REUNIFIED   1991-2002
             GERMANY
  ---------------------------------------------------------
  Maastricht, December 1991: more powers for the
  parliament; economic and monetary union leading to a
  common currency; a common foreign and security policy;
  a timetable. Britain objects to federalism, monetary
  union and the Social Chapter, which is removed.
  Ratified by October 1993. France, the Netherlands and
  Belgium supported it strongly "because they thought it
  was the best way to make sure that the power of the
  reunified Germany was contained and controlled within
  the Community"
  1995: Sweden, Finland and Austria join - membership 15
  1997 Amsterdam; 2001 Nice; euro introduced in 12
  members on 1 January 2002

  PURPOSE 4: THE INSTITUTIONAL SETTLEMENT OF THE     2004-09
             COLD WAR'S END
  ---------------------------------------------------------
  1 May 2004: ten new members - the Czech Republic,
  Cyprus, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta,
  Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia
  2004 Constitutional Treaty rejected by Dutch and
  French voters in 2005; Lisbon signed by 27 members in
  December 2007, rejected by Irish voters in June 2008,
  approved in October 2009, in force 1 December 2009
```

### 1. The learner's problem: a treaty list is not an answer

⚠️ Most candidates can recite some of the dates above and almost none can say what changed between them. ⚠️ The organising question is therefore: **what was integration for, at each stage, and who wanted it for that reason?** ⚠️ Answer that and the treaties arrange themselves.

⚠️ The canonical formulation to carry: ⚠️ European integration was neither an inevitable federal march nor a mere free-trade arrangement; it was a repeatedly renegotiated bargain among peace, prosperity, national sovereignty and geopolitical anchoring.

### 2. The founding purpose, and why it was economic

✅ "The ECSC was set up in 1951, and was the brainchild of Robert Schuman." ✅ "The agreements setting up the full EEC were signed in Rome in 1957" by the six founder members - France, West Germany, Italy, the Netherlands, Belgium and Luxembourg - creating the European Economic Community, popularly the Common Market. ✅ "In 1967 the EEC, the ECSC and EURATOM formally merged."

⚠️ The design logic is worth stating explicitly because it is the topic's cleanest example of institutional thinking: ⚠️ pooling the coal and steel of the two states most likely to fight each other makes renewed war materially harder, not merely politically embarrassing. ⚠️ That is integration as **security policy by economic means**, and it explains why the first institution covered two industries rather than the whole economy.

✅ Britain's position illustrates the alternative model: it "decided not to sign the 1957 Treaty of Rome" and instead took "the lead in organizing a rival group, the European Free Trade Association", founded in 1960, which differed because "there was no question of common economic policies and no supranational authority". ✅ Within less than four years of Rome the British had changed course and applied, and ✅ "eventually, on 1 January 1973, Britain, along with Eire and Denmark, was able to enter."

⚠️ The 1960 free-trade association is the control case for the whole topic: two designs were tried, and the one with common policies and a supranational authority grew while the one without did not.

### 3. The middle period: widening, redistribution and the first sovereignty row

| ✅ Development | ⚠️ What it reveals |
|---|---|
| ✅ Lome Convention, 1975: duty-free entry for goods from over 40 African and Caribbean states, mostly former European colonies, plus promised aid; other poor countries added later; "many critics argued that it was too little" | ⚠️ The Community's external relations were shaped by colonial history, and the criticism recorded here is the bridge to the Global South material of Lesson 20 |
| ✅ First direct elections, June 1979: 410 members; turnout under a third in Britain, over 80 per cent in Italy and Belgium, 57 per cent in West Germany where voting was voluntary | ⚠️ The legitimacy gap is measurable and was visible from the first election; it is not a later development |
| ✅ Exchange-rate mechanism, 1979, linking currencies to limit fluctuation and control inflation "and lead eventually to a single currency"; Britain joined in October 1990 "when the exchange rate was relatively high" and the account calls it a mistake | ⚠️ Monetary integration preceded Maastricht by more than a decade |
| ✅ Greece 1981; Portugal and Spain 1986 - membership 12 and population over 320 million; they had not been admitted earlier "because their political systems were undemocratic" | ⚠️ Membership was conditional on political form: accession functioned as a democratic anchor, which is exactly its later role in the east |
| ✅ The budget crisis of 1980, when Britain's contribution was £1,209 million against West Germany's £699 million and France's £13 million, resolved by a reduction to £1,346 million over three years | ⚠️ Distributive disputes among members are permanent, and they arise from the rules rather than from ill will |
| ✅ The 1986 changes: a completely free internal market by 1992; more control over health, safety, environment and consumers; encouragement for research; help for backward regions; majority voting on many issues; more powers for the parliament | ⚠️ The last two provoked the sovereignty row: federalists were pleased, and in Britain and Denmark "they stirred up the old controversy about national sovereignty" |

✅ Thatcher's objection is recorded verbatim and is the best short statement of the sovereignty case: "a centralized federal government in Europe would be a nightmare; co-operation with the other European countries must not be at the expense of individuality, the national customs and traditions which made Europe great in the past."

✅ The common agricultural policy supplies the standing criticism: subsidies to keep farmers in business encouraged production "far more than could be sold", until by 1980 "about three-quarters of the entire EC budget was being paid out each year in subsidies to farmers"; ✅ production quotas were introduced in 1984 without solving it; ✅ by 1987 there was "a vast wine 'lake' and a butter 'mountain' of one and a half million tonnes - enough to supply the entire EC for a year", enough milk powder for five years, and storage costs of £1 million a day; ✅ surpluses were sold cheaply to the USSR, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh, distributed free to the poor, turned into animal feed and in part burnt; ✅ the 1987 budget crisis left the Community £3 billion in the red with £10 billion of debts; ✅ production curbs and a price freeze followed and surpluses were shrinking by the end of 1988.

⚠️ The agricultural policy is the point at which European integration meets the Global South, and Lesson 20 uses it: ✅ the account states later that the policy "continues to encourage high production levels at the expense of quality, and causes a great deal of damage to the economies of the developing world".

### 4. Maastricht: deepening, and the German question answered again

✅ "A summit meeting of all the heads of the member states was held in Maastricht (Netherlands) in December 1991, and an agreement was drawn up for 'a new stage in the process of creating an even closer union among the peoples of Europe'." ✅ Its recorded points: more powers for the European parliament; greater economic and monetary union culminating in a common currency shared by all member states around the end of the century; a common foreign and security policy; and a timetable.

✅ The British objection and its outcome: objections to federalism and monetary union, and to the Social Chapter - rules on safe and healthy working conditions, equality at work between men and women, consulting and informing workers, and protecting those made redundant. ✅ "Britain argued that these measures would increase production costs and therefore cause unemployment. The other members seemed to think that proper treatment of workers was more important. In the end, because of British objections, the Social Chapter was removed from the Treaty and it was left to individual governments to decide whether or not to carry them out." ✅ Ratification was completed by October 1993.

✅ The motive that connects this lesson to Lesson 9: "The French, Dutch and Belgian governments supported the Treaty strongly because they thought it was the best way to make sure that the power of the reunified Germany was contained and controlled within the Community."

⚠️ That sentence is the topic's most important single link. ⚠️ The founding purpose of 1951 - containing German power by binding it into shared institutions - was reactivated in 1991 by the same event that ended the Cold War. ⚠️ Integration's first purpose and its post-Cold-War purpose are the same purpose, applied to a changed Germany.

✅ Popular enthusiasm lagged: ✅ Denmark rejected the treaty at first and approved it by a narrow majority in a second referendum in May 1993; ✅ the account records that the Swiss "voted not to join the Community (December 1992), and so did the Norwegians" - ⚠️ though both were votes about **membership** rather than about ratifying Maastricht, the Swiss referendum of December 1992 having rejected membership of the European Economic Area and the Norwegian electorate having rejected accession itself; ✅ the French referendum majority was tiny; ✅ in Britain, where no referendum was allowed, the Conservatives were split and the treaty passed "only by the narrowest of majorities in parliament". ⚠️ The legitimacy gap of 1979 had become a ratification problem.

### 5. Widening east, and the institutional strain it caused

✅ "With the continued success of the European Union, more states applied to join. In January 1995, Sweden, Finland and Austria became members, bringing the total membership to 15." ✅ The Treaty of Amsterdam of 1997, in force 1 May 1999, undertook to promote full employment, better living and working conditions and more generous social policies, gave the Council power to penalise members violating human rights and gave the parliament more powers.

✅ The concerns raised by eastern enlargement are recorded in four numbered anxieties: that most former communist states "were so economically backward that they would be unable to join on equal terms with the advanced members"; that the Union would become too large, slowing decision-making; that closer political integration would become almost impossible in a Union of 25 to 30 states "unless a two-speed Europe emerged"; and that the institutions needed reform to be more open, democratic and efficient - an anxiety sharpened in March 1999 when a report revealed widespread corruption and fraud and "the entire Commission of 20 members was forced to resign."

✅ The Treaty of Nice of December 2000, signed in February 2001, addressed this by extending qualified majority voting while keeping unanimity for taxation and social security, reweighting Council membership and parliamentary seats by population, limiting the largest states to one commissioner each, permitting enhanced cooperation by groups of eight or more, calling a conference on the constitution by 2004, and approving a rapid reaction force of 60,000 "though it was stressed that NATO would still be the basis of Europe's defence system."

✅ The rapid reaction force displeased both the French president, who wanted it independent of NATO, and the United States, which in October 2003 complained of being kept in the dark and claimed the plans "represented one of the greatest dangers to the transatlantic relationship". ✅ The account's inference is recorded: "although the Americans wanted Europe to take on more of the world's defence and anti-terrorist burden, it intended this to be done under US direction, working through NATO, not independently."

✅ Ireland rejected Nice in a referendum in June 2001, resenting the increased power of the larger states, and the Commission president's statement that enlargement could proceed anyway "prompted accusations from across the Union that its leaders were out of touch with ordinary citizens."

✅ The tensions of 2001-03 were sharper still: the divide between those wanting a United States of Europe and those wanting a looser association; the confusion after the terrorist attacks of 11 September 2001, when the Union "was not well equipped to take rapid collective action" and individual leaders acted, which smaller members resented; the split over Iraq in March 2003, with Germany and France opposed to action not authorised by the United Nations while Spain, Italy, Portugal, Denmark and the prospective new members favoured it, and the American defence secretary dismissing German and French opposition as "old Europe"; the budgetary rift when France and Germany breached the three per cent deficit rule and were given an extra year, infuriating smaller members; and the collapse of the December 2003 Brussels summit over voting powers.

✅ Enlargement nonetheless proceeded: "the enlargement of the EU was still able to go ahead as planned on 1 May 2004; the ten new members were the Czech Republic, Cyprus, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia."

✅ A Constitutional Treaty was drawn up in June 2004 - bringing together the previous treaties, allowing a procedure for members to leave, keeping national vetoes on taxation, foreign policy and defence, and requiring for a measure to pass the support of at least 15 countries representing 65 per cent of the Union's total population of 455 million, with four countries holding 35 per cent able to block. ✅ It was rejected by Dutch and French voters in 2005; a "period of reflection" followed; and a new agreement was signed by all 27 members at Lisbon in December 2007, stating its aim as completing the process begun at Amsterdam and Nice "with a view to enhancing the efficiency and democratic legitimacy of the Union". ✅ Irish voters rejected Lisbon in June 2008, approved it in October 2009, and it came into effect on 1 December 2009.

### 6. The strain after 2008, bounded

✅ In September 2008 the worst financial collapse in the United States since 1929 spread to Europe; ✅ by the end of 2008 demand for European exports had contracted alarmingly and member states fell into recession one by one; ✅ Spain and Ireland, which had enjoyed the highest growth rates since the euro's introduction in 2002, were worst affected. ✅ Perry Anderson is quoted on Ireland: output contracted by 8.5 per cent between the first quarters of 2008 and 2009 and the fiscal deficit rose to over 15 per cent of GDP. ✅ Greece had "manipulated" its borrowing figures to qualify for the euro in 2001, and for much of 2011 and 2012 its government appeared close to default. ✅ In February 2012 the German Chancellor said Europe faced its gravest test for decades.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The eurozone crisis is studied in detail as part of the world economy. ⚠️ It appears here only to establish that the enlargement settlement was under severe strain within five years of completion, which bears directly on the order question of Lesson 21.

✅ The account's own balance is worth reproducing: "All these problems should not be allowed to lead to the conclusion that the EU is a failure. Whatever happens in the future, nothing can take away the fact that since 1945, the countries of western Europe have been at peace with each other ... Given Europe's war-torn past, this is a considerable achievement, which must be attributed in large measure to the European movement."

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Paper II on important international institutions, their structure and mandate; ✅ Paper I on the political and economic reorganisation of Europe.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Explain how European integration changed from a Cold-War peace project into a post-Cold-War enlargement project." ⚠️ "Was European integration driven more by peace, economics or geopolitical strategy?"

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying European integration began after the Cold War. ✅ The Coal and Steel Community dates from 1951 and the Treaty of Rome from 1957.
2. ⚠️ Writing a treaty timeline. ⚠️ The question is what each stage was for and who wanted it.
3. ⚠️ Presenting integration as a smooth march to federation. ✅ Danish, Swiss, Norwegian, Irish, Dutch and French rejections are all recorded, as are the Commission's mass resignation in 1999 and the collapse of the December 2003 summit.
4. ⚠️ Confusing the Community and the free-trade association. ✅ The latter had "no question of common economic policies and no supranational authority".
5. ⚠️ Omitting the German motive at Maastricht. ✅ France, the Netherlands and Belgium supported it as the best way of containing reunified German power inside the Community.
6. ⚠️ Turning this into a eurozone-crisis answer. ⚠️ The crisis is studied under the world economy; here it is only evidence of strain.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Coal and Steel Community 1951, Schuman's project; Treaty of Rome 1957 with six founder members; merger of EEC, ECSC and EURATOM in 1967.
2. ✅ Britain declined Rome, organised the European Free Trade Association in 1960 with no common policies and no supranational authority, applied within four years, and joined with Ireland and Denmark on 1 January 1973.
3. ✅ Lome Convention 1975: duty-free entry for over 40 African and Caribbean states plus aid; critics said it was too little.
4. ✅ First direct elections June 1979, 410 members; turnout under a third in Britain against over 80 per cent in Italy and Belgium.
5. ✅ Exchange-rate mechanism 1979, intended to lead eventually to a single currency; Britain joined in October 1990 at a relatively high rate.
6. ✅ Greece 1981, Portugal and Spain 1986, membership 12 and population over 320 million; earlier exclusion was because their political systems were undemocratic.
7. ✅ 1986 changes: free internal market by 1992, majority voting on many issues, more parliamentary powers - and the sovereignty controversy, with Thatcher's "centralized federal government in Europe would be a nightmare".
8. ✅ Agricultural policy: three-quarters of the budget in subsidies by 1980; quotas from 1984; by 1987 a wine lake and a 1.5-million-tonne butter mountain, five years of milk powder, £1 million a day in storage; the 1987 budget crisis of £3 billion in deficit and £10 billion of debts; damage to developing-country economies.
9. ✅ Maastricht, December 1991: parliamentary powers, monetary union towards a common currency, a common foreign and security policy, a timetable; the Social Chapter removed on British objection; ratified by October 1993; supported by France, the Netherlands and Belgium as the best way to contain reunified Germany.
10. ✅ Ratification difficulties: Danish rejection then narrow approval in May 1993; a tiny French majority; the narrowest of parliamentary majorities in Britain. ⚠️ The Swiss and Norwegian votes belong in a different column: they declined **membership**, not ratification.
11. ✅ 1995 Sweden, Finland, Austria - membership 15; Amsterdam 1997 in force 1 May 1999; the entire Commission of 20 forced to resign in March 1999; Nice agreed December 2000 with qualified majority voting, reweighting and a 60,000-strong rapid reaction force under NATO's primacy; Irish rejection of Nice in June 2001.
12. ✅ 1 May 2004: ten new members - the Czech Republic, Cyprus, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia. The euro had been introduced in 12 members on 1 January 2002.
13. ✅ The 2004 Constitutional Treaty required 15 countries representing 65 per cent of a population of 455 million; rejected by Dutch and French voters in 2005; Lisbon signed by 27 states in December 2007, rejected by Ireland in June 2008, approved in October 2009, in force 1 December 2009.
14. ✅ After September 2008 the American financial collapse spread; Spain and Ireland were worst hit; Anderson records an Irish output contraction of 8.5 per cent and a deficit over 15 per cent of GDP; Greece had manipulated its figures to join the euro in 2001; in February 2012 Merkel called it Europe's gravest test for decades.
15. ⚠️ The master formulation: integration was neither an inevitable federal march nor a mere free-trade arrangement, but a repeatedly renegotiated bargain among peace, prosperity, national sovereignty and geopolitical anchoring.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the same underlying purpose links the Coal and Steel Community of 1951 to the Maastricht Treaty of December 1991, and why recognising it changes the way European integration should be described.

**Model answer:** The underlying purpose is the containment of German power by binding it into shared institutions rather than by balancing against it. In 1951 the Coal and Steel Community pooled precisely the industries of war, making renewed Franco-German conflict materially harder rather than merely politically embarrassing - integration as security policy by economic means. Forty years later the same problem reappeared in a new form when Germany was reunified in October 1990, and the account records the answer explicitly: the French, Dutch and Belgian governments supported the Maastricht Treaty strongly because they thought it was the best way to make sure that the power of the reunified Germany was contained and controlled within the Community. Recognising this changes the description in two ways. First, it shows that integration is not a single ascending process towards federation but a device repeatedly re-purposed as circumstances change - security in the 1950s, a market bargain in the 1960s and 1970s, deepening and German containment around 1991, and the institutional settlement of the Cold War's end by 2004. Second, it explains why the resistance is equally persistent: a device for constraining states will always be contested by states, which is why the record contains Danish, Swiss, Norwegian, Irish, Dutch and French rejections rather than a smooth ratification history.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the European Union as a purely economic body that gradually acquired political ambitions. The first institution was created for a security purpose, membership was withheld from Greece, Portugal and Spain until their political systems ceased to be undemocratic, and the 1986 reforms that provoked the sovereignty row were adopted to complete a market. Economics was the instrument throughout; politics was the objective from the beginning.

---

## Lesson 19 - NATO enlargement: the other half of the institutional settlement

Progress: 19/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: why one alliance survived when the other dissolved; the enlargement rounds of 1999 and 2004; the alliance's shift from territorial defence to crisis management; the recorded transatlantic friction over European defence; the arguments for and against enlargement stated fairly; and the boundary at which this topic hands the subject to current international relations

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of the November 1990 declaration, the Bosnian and Kosovo operations in which NATO took over running the operation, and the Nice provisions on a European rapid reaction force with the stated primacy of NATO and the American reaction to European defence plans
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 9 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two alliances, one outcome, and the question it leaves

```text
   1990-91            THE SYMMETRY THAT DID NOT HAPPEN
   ==================================================
   Warsaw Pact                        NATO
   - members' communist               - members' governments
     governments fall 1989-90           unchanged
   - the Nov 1990 declaration         - the same declaration
     ends the adversarial               ends the adversarial
     relationship                       relationship
   - DISSOLVED 1 July 1991            - CONTINUES, and then
                                        ENLARGES

   WHY THE ASYMMETRY?
   - the eastern alliance was an instrument of control over
     its members; when control ended, its reason ended
   - the western alliance was, in its members' own account,
     a voluntary association for defence; its members chose
     to keep it
   - and the new democracies of the east ASKED TO JOIN it

   THE ENLARGEMENT ROUNDS OWNED HERE
   1999: Czech Republic, Hungary, Poland
   2004: Bulgaria, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Romania,
         Slovakia, Slovenia
   (the same year as the European Union's ten-member
    enlargement of 1 May 2004 - the two settlements move
    together)

   THE FUNCTIONAL SHIFT, RECORDED IN THIS SESSION
   Bosnia 1995: "the UN position was weakened because NATO
                was now running the operation"
   Kosovo 1999: an air campaign against a sovereign state
                without express Council authorisation
   => from territorial defence of members to crisis
      management beyond them

   THE QUESTION THIS TOPIC STATES BUT DOES NOT SETTLE
   Was enlargement the consolidation of a democratic peace,
   or the extension of one bloc into the space vacated by
   the other?
```

### 1. The learner's problem: why did one alliance survive?

⚠️ The question is not rhetorical, and the answer is structural.

⚠️ The eastern alliance was, on the evidence of this session, an instrument of control as much as of defence. ✅ The account states the difference plainly: "the countries of eastern Europe were forced into it by the USSR ... whereas the members of the EC joined voluntarily." ⚠️ An alliance whose function is to bind its members to a hegemon has no purpose once the hegemon stops enforcing the binding, which is why the Warsaw Treaty Organisation was dissolved on 1 July 1991 by its own members.

⚠️ The western alliance had a different relationship with its members, and - decisively - **demand from outside**. ⚠️ The new democracies of central and eastern Europe applied to join both western institutions, not one. ⚠️ Understanding enlargement as demand-driven as well as supply-driven is what separates a considered answer from a slogan in either direction.

### 2. The rounds this topic owns

⚠️ Two enlargement rounds fall inside this topic's period and are held as part of the declared connecting set.

| ⚠️ Round | ⚠️ New members | ⚠️ Significance for this topic |
|---|---|---|
| 1999 | Czech Republic, Hungary, Poland | Three former Warsaw Pact states join the alliance they had been organised against, ten years after their regimes fell. The same year as the Kosovo campaign, which is why the alliance's purpose and its membership changed simultaneously |
| 2004 | Bulgaria, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Romania, Slovakia, Slovenia | Includes three former Soviet republics. Occurs in the same year as the European Union's ten-member enlargement on 1 May 2004, so that the security settlement and the economic-political settlement of the Cold War's end are completed together |

⚠️ The parallel timing is the analytically important fact. ⚠️ For the states concerned, accession to both organisations was a single project: a guarantee against reversal, an anchor for domestic reform, and a statement about which Europe they belonged to. ⚠️ Lesson 18 showed that European Union membership had been withheld from Greece, Portugal and Spain until their political systems ceased to be undemocratic; the same conditionality logic operated in the east.

### 3. The functional shift, and where this session's own evidence shows it

⚠️ Enlargement is only half the story; the other half is that the alliance's task changed, and two cases in this session document it.

- ✅ **Bosnia, 1995.** After the London conference and the shelling of Sarajevo, "a massive NATO bombing of Bosnian Serb positions" followed, "More UN troops were sent, though in fact the UN position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation", and the Dayton settlement provided for "60 000 NATO troops ... to police the settlement."
- ✅ **Kosovo, 1999.** "NATO forces carried out controversial bombing attacks against Serbia", and after the June agreement "a UN and NATO force of over 40 000 arrived to keep the peace."

⚠️ Read together, the two cases show an alliance built for the territorial defence of its own members conducting coercive operations against a non-member state on behalf of a population that was not its own. ⚠️ That is a change of function, and it is inseparable from the change of membership: an alliance that is enlarging its area is also enlarging the range of contingencies it may be called on to handle.

⚠️ The account also records the friction this produced with the European Union's own defence ambitions: ✅ the Nice rapid reaction force of 60,000 was approved "though it was stressed that NATO would still be the basis of Europe's defence system", which displeased the French president, who wanted it independent; ✅ and the United States complained in October 2003 that it was being kept in the dark and that the plans "represented one of the greatest dangers to the transatlantic relationship". ✅ The account's inference: the Americans wanted Europe to carry more of the burden, but "under US direction, working through NATO, not independently."

⚠️ That is a useful corrective to a simple picture of a western bloc moving as one. ⚠️ The surviving alliance was itself contested from inside by its European members.

### 4. The argument, stated fairly on both sides

⚠️ This is a live dispute in contemporary international relations, and the boundary rule for this session is strict: state the historical facts, state the arguments, and hand the present-day controversy to the study of current international relations.

| ⚠️ The case for enlargement | ⚠️ The case against |
|---|---|
| The applicants asked. Sovereign states that had spent four decades inside an imposed alliance chose a different association, and refusing them would have been a decision imposed on them by others | Expanding a military alliance towards a former adversary's borders is read by that adversary as a security threat regardless of intention, and intentions are not what security policy responds to |
| Membership functioned as an anchor for domestic reform, exactly as European Union accession had for Greece, Portugal and Spain, which had been excluded while undemocratic | Conditionality could have been supplied by the economic union alone; adding a military guarantee was a separate choice with separate consequences |
| A security vacuum between two former blocs would have invited competition rather than prevented it | ✅ The November 1990 declaration stated that the signatories were "no longer adversaries"; a subsequent expansion of one side's alliance sits uneasily with that declaration |
| The alliance's new tasks - Bosnia, Kosovo - were undertaken because no other body could or would act, as the safe-areas failure showed | Those same tasks show an alliance acquiring a licence to use force beyond its treaty area, which was not what its members had committed to |

⚠️ **The honest verdict this session supports.** ⚠️ Enlargement is best described as the security half of the institutional settlement of the Cold War's end, adopted for reasons that were partly defensive, partly about anchoring democracy and largely driven by applicant demand, and carrying consequences that were contested at the time and remain contested. ⚠️ Whether it was wise is a judgement about the decades after this topic ends, and is properly settled in the study of contemporary international relations.

### 5. The boundary, stated explicitly

⚠️ This session stops here on the alliance question, and the stopping point is a rule rather than an omission.

```text
  COVERED HERE                   HANDED ON
  ------------                   ---------
  - why the Warsaw Pact          - every enlargement round after
    dissolved and NATO             2004
    survived                     - the present-day security
  - the 1999 and 2004              architecture of Europe
    rounds as part of the        - contemporary alliance
    settlement                     politics and India's own
  - the functional shift           positions on groupings
    evidenced by Bosnia and        -> International Relations
    Kosovo                       - the detailed institutional
  - the recorded transatlantic     design and mandate of the
    friction to 2003               United Nations
                                   -> studied under the UN
```

⚠️ An answer that respects this boundary reads as disciplined; one that does not reads as a current-affairs essay with dates attached.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Paper II on important international institutions, their structure and mandate; ✅ Paper I on international relations transformed.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Questions on this material normally reach a candidate through the international-relations papers rather than through world history, which is precisely why the historical foundation taught here matters: it supplies the depth that a purely contemporary answer lacks.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating enlargement as imposed. ⚠️ The applicants applied, and the parallel European Union accession of the same states shows a single national project.
2. ⚠️ Treating it as uncontroversial. ⚠️ The tension with the November 1990 declaration is real and should be stated.
3. ⚠️ Forgetting the functional shift. ✅ Bosnia and Kosovo are in this session's own evidence.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the West as monolithic. ✅ The rapid reaction force dispute and the October 2003 American complaint are recorded.
5. ⚠️ Carrying the answer into present-day alliance politics. ⚠️ That is studied under international relations.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Asymmetry: the Warsaw Treaty Organisation was dissolved on 1 July 1991; NATO continued and enlarged.
2. ✅ The account's explanation of the original difference: eastern European states "were forced into it by the USSR", whereas Community members "joined voluntarily".
3. ⚠️ Enlargement rounds owned here: 1999 - Czech Republic, Hungary, Poland; 2004 - Bulgaria, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Romania, Slovakia, Slovenia.
4. ⚠️ The 2004 round coincided with the European Union's ten-member enlargement of 1 May 2004: the security and economic-political settlements were completed together.
5. ✅ The functional shift is evidenced in this session: in Bosnia "the UN position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation" and 60,000 NATO troops policed Dayton; in Kosovo the alliance conducted the air campaign and then formed part of a force of over 40,000.
6. ✅ Transatlantic friction: the Nice rapid reaction force of 60,000 was approved with NATO stated as the basis of European defence, displeasing the French president; in October 2003 the United States said European defence plans "represented one of the greatest dangers to the transatlantic relationship".
7. ⚠️ Four arguments for: applicant demand; democratic anchoring on the Greek, Portuguese and Spanish precedent; avoidance of a security vacuum; and the absence of any other body able to act in the Balkans.
8. ⚠️ Four arguments against: the security dilemma regardless of intention; the availability of economic conditionality alone; the tension with the November 1990 "no longer adversaries" declaration; and the acquisition of a licence to act beyond the treaty area.
9. ⚠️ The boundary: everything after 2004, the present-day security architecture and India's positions are studied under international relations.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Warsaw Treaty Organisation was dissolved in 1991 while NATO survived and enlarged, and state the one tension that the enlargement creates with the way the Cold War was formally ended.

**Model answer:** The two alliances had different relationships with their members. The account states that the eastern European states were forced into their association by the USSR, whereas the members of the western Community joined voluntarily; the eastern alliance therefore functioned as an instrument of control as well as of defence, and once the controlling power stopped enforcing the binding and the members' communist governments had fallen, it had no remaining purpose and its members dissolved it on 1 July 1991. The western alliance was, on its members' own account, a voluntary association whose members chose to continue it, and crucially it faced demand from outside: the new democracies of central and eastern Europe applied to join it, along with the European Union, as a single national project of guarantee, reform anchoring and identity - which is why the 2004 round and the Union's ten-member enlargement of 1 May 2004 fall in the same year. The tension is with the instrument that formally ended the Cold War. In November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were no longer adversaries and that none of their weapons would be used except in self-defence; extending one of those two alliances into the space the other had occupied sits uneasily with a declaration of mutual non-adversity, whatever the intentions of those extending it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Choosing a side and presenting it as the historical finding. The evidence in this session supports the facts of enlargement, the applicants' demand, the functional shift documented in Bosnia and Kosovo, and the recorded friction even among western allies; it does not support a verdict on whether enlargement was wise, because that judgement depends on decades that fall outside this topic and are studied under contemporary international relations.

## Lesson 20 - Globalisation, the Global South and India: convergence claimed, divergence recorded

Progress: 20/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: globalisation as the ideological component of the order claim; the recorded evidence of asymmetric institutional power; the developing world in the Community's own agricultural and trade record; the Global South as an analytical category rather than a bloc; continuing non-Western agency after bipolarity; India's simultaneous strategic and economic recalibration in 1991; and the strict scope boundary

⚠️ Four "no" answers do not amount to a claim that nothing changed. ⚠️ They amount to the finding that what changed was the **distribution of options**, not the arrival of a single destination - which is the formulation this session defends.

### 1. The boundary, stated before the content

⚠️ This lesson is deliberately short on economic history, and the reason is scope rather than lack of material.

```text
  STUDIED UNDER THE WORLD ECONOMY       USED HERE
  --------------------------------      ---------
  Bretton Woods and its institutions    Only as an instrument
  The North-South divide as economic    whose control is evidence
  history                               about the order claim
  Oil, debt and adjustment              Only the ideological
  Globalisation as economic process     component of the claim
  The 2008 crisis                       that markets, elections
                                        and institutions were
  STUDIED UNDER THE ECONOMY AND         expanding together
  INTERNATIONAL RELATIONS
  -----------------------
  India's 1991 reform package           Only the changed systemic
  Strategic autonomy as doctrine        context India faced
  Present-day groupings and diplomacy
```

⚠️ An answer that turns this topic into a globalisation essay has strayed into another part of the syllabus and will be marked accordingly.

### 2. Institutional asymmetry, on the record

✅ The account records the mechanism plainly: "The US economy was so powerful that decisions taken in Washington and New York had worldwide repercussions. With the increasing globalization of the world's economy, American companies had interests all over the world. The Americans kept firm control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund, so that states applying for loans had to make sure that their internal policies were acceptable to the USA."

✅ It gives a dated instance: "In 1995 the new president of the World Bank, James Wolfensohn, announced that he wanted the Bank to do more to promote debt relief, good government, education and health in the Third World. But Washington opposed this, arguing for strict austerity."

✅ And an attributed judgement: Will Hutton, quoted, that "the international financial system has been shaped to extend US financial and political power, not to promote" other ends.

⚠️ Two disciplines apply. ⚠️ First, the Hutton judgement is a quotation and must be attributed. ⚠️ Second, the point being established is narrow and therefore strong: it is that the institutions through which the liberal order operated were not neutral arbiters between equally placed states. ⚠️ That claim is supported by the record; a broader claim that the institutions did nothing useful is not, and should not be made.

### 3. Europe's own record towards the developing world

⚠️ It is a mistake to test the order claim only against American practice, and the European material supplies a second test from the same source.

- ✅ **Access on terms.** The Lome Convention of 1975 allowed goods from over 40 African and Caribbean states, mostly former European colonies, into the Community free of duty and promised economic aid, in response to criticism that the Community was "too inward-looking and self-centred"; ✅ "many critics argued that it was too little."
- ✅ **Structural damage.** The account's own later judgement is unambiguous: the common agricultural policy "continues to encourage high production levels at the expense of quality, and causes a great deal of damage to the economies of the developing world; this needs attention, as does the whole system of food standards regulation."
- ✅ **Surplus disposal as policy.** Efforts to clear the 1987 surpluses "included selling it off cheaply to the USSR, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh, distributing butter free of charge to the poor within the Community, and using it to make animal feed", with some of the oldest butter burnt in boilers.

⚠️ The analytical point is precise and worth making in one sentence: ⚠️ the same institution that anchored democracy in southern and eastern Europe operated an agricultural regime that its own historian says damaged developing economies. ⚠️ Both are true; both belong in an assessment; and holding both is what "critically examine" requires.

### 4. The Global South as a category, and continuing agency

⚠️ Two points have to be held firmly.

1. ⚠️ **"Global South" is an analytical category, not a uniform bloc or a passive zone.** ⚠️ The states grouped under it differ in size, capability, resources and alignment, and treating them as a single actor is an error of the same kind as treating "the East" as one actor in 1989.
2. ⚠️ **The end of bipolarity did not make postcolonial states passive recipients of a Western order.** ⚠️ They continued to bargain through regional organisations, the United Nations, commodity and trade coalitions and issue-based partnerships.

⚠️ The evidence in this session supports the second point from an unexpected direction: ✅ the account's own record of repeated lone American negative votes - 130 to 1 in 1985 on human rights, 140 to 1 in 1987 on Third World communication services - is evidence that the General Assembly majorities on those questions were formed elsewhere. ⚠️ A body in which the strongest state is regularly outvoted 140 to 1 is not a body in which non-Western states lack a voice; it is a body in which their voice is not decisive on matters the strongest state treats as important. ⚠️ Stating the distinction that way is more accurate and more useful than either "the South had no power" or "the South was heard".

⚠️ The account offers a further datum on the plural character of the post-1991 world, this time from inside the West: ✅ "Many people hope that the EU will become strong and influential enough to provide a counterbalance to the USA, which in 2004 seemed in a position to dominate the world and convert it into a series of carbon copies of itself." ⚠️ Recorded as a hope, and labelled as one, it is nonetheless evidence that even the strongest state's closest partners did not treat unipolarity as a settled or desirable permanent condition.

### 5. India in 1991: two shocks, one recalibration

⚠️ The scope here is deliberately narrow, and this session stays inside it.

✅ For India, 1991 combined two shocks: the loss of the familiar Soviet strategic setting, and a severe balance-of-payments crisis. ⚠️ The analytical relevance is **recalibration** - economic liberalisation, diversified major-power relations, and movement from non-alignment's bipolar setting towards strategic autonomy.

⚠️ Three disciplines govern how this is written.

1. ⚠️ **Establish the systemic change, not the policy detail.** ⚠️ The world-history point is that the external environment within which Indian foreign and economic policy had been formed since the 1950s ceased to exist within eighteen months.
2. ⚠️ **Do not write the economics answer.** ⚠️ The 1991 reform package, its sequencing, its instruments and its results are studied under the economy; naming the crisis as a simultaneous shock is the limit here.
3. ⚠️ **Do not write the international-relations answer.** ⚠️ Strategic autonomy as a doctrine, contemporary partnerships and present-day groupings are studied there.

⚠️ What this session can legitimately add is the **comparative** point, which is a world-history contribution rather than an Indian-policy one: ⚠️ India's response to the end of bipolarity - diversification while retaining decision-making autonomy - is one of several post-1991 trajectories, and setting it beside the eastern European states' choice of institutional anchoring in the European Union and NATO, and beside Russia's shock therapy, shows three different answers to the same systemic change. ⚠️ That comparison is what a world-history answer can offer that a policy answer cannot.

### Criticism and reply: is scepticism about convergence just hindsight?

⚠️ **The objection.** In 1991 the convergence claim was reasonable: communist regimes were falling, markets were opening, the European Union was deepening and widening, and the Security Council had just functioned as designed. Criticising the claim from the 2020s is unfair to those who made it.

⚠️ **The reply.** The evidence against convergence is contemporary, not retrospective. ✅ Within months of the Gulf coalition, the Yugoslav wars began and the same institutions did not act. ✅ Within a year of the Soviet dissolution, Russian prices were thirty times higher and over a million people had lost their jobs. ✅ The account records that in eastern Europe generally "the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism" and that "very little help was forthcoming from the west". ⚠️ These are 1991-95 facts, available to anyone making the claim at the time.

⚠️ **The concession that must be made.** ⚠️ Something real did happen: the number of states holding competitive elections rose; ✅ ten states joined the European Union on 1 May 2004; ✅ integration and market opening did expand. ⚠️ The accurate finding is not that convergence was an illusion but that it was **partial, uneven and reversible**, and that its distribution followed prior capability, geography and institutional access.

⚠️ **The residual.** ⚠️ This session cannot say how much of the divergence was caused by policy choice and how much by inherited endowment. ⚠️ It records the three candidate variables - an organised political counterpart, external resources and an external anchor, and the design of the economic programme - and declines to rank them.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Political philosophies and their effect on society; ✅ and Paper II on international institutions, through the record of how the financial institutions operated.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ "Assess the US unipolar moment and its limits from a Global South perspective." ⚠️ Any question asking whether the post-1991 order was genuinely universal.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing a globalisation essay. ⚠️ Economic globalisation is studied under the world economy; here it is only the ideological component of the order claim.
2. ⚠️ Writing India's 1991 reform answer. ⚠️ That is studied under the economy; establish the changed systemic context and stop.
3. ⚠️ Treating the Global South as one actor. ⚠️ It is an analytical category, not a bloc.
4. ⚠️ Asserting that non-Western states had no agency. ✅ The 130-1 and 140-1 votes show majorities formed elsewhere; the accurate claim is about decisiveness, not voice.
5. ⚠️ Testing the order claim only against American practice. ✅ The European agricultural record supplies a second test from the same source.
6. ⚠️ Stating the Hutton judgement as an unowned fact. ✅ Attribute it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ "The Americans kept firm control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund, so that states applying for loans had to make sure that their internal policies were acceptable to the USA."
2. ✅ In 1995 the new World Bank president wanted more debt relief, good government, education and health for the Third World; Washington opposed this and argued for strict austerity.
3. ✅ Will Hutton, quoted: the international financial system "has been shaped to extend US financial and political power".
4. ✅ Lome, 1975: duty-free access for over 40 African and Caribbean states plus promised aid, answering the charge that the Community was too inward-looking; critics said it was too little.
5. ✅ The common agricultural policy "causes a great deal of damage to the economies of the developing world" on the account's own judgement; surpluses were sold cheaply to the USSR, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh, given free to the poor, fed to animals and in part burnt.
6. ⚠️ "Global South" is an analytical category, not a uniform bloc; the end of bipolarity widened options without removing hierarchy.
7. ✅ Evidence of non-Western majorities: 130 to 1 in 1985 on human rights and 140 to 1 in 1987 on Third World communication services. The accurate claim concerns decisiveness, not voice.
8. ✅ Even inside the West, unipolarity was not treated as settled: many hoped the European Union would become a counterbalance to a USA that in 2004 seemed able to dominate the world.
9. ⚠️ India in 1991 faced two simultaneous shocks - the loss of the Soviet strategic setting and a severe balance-of-payments crisis - and the world-history point is recalibration, not the reform package.
10. ⚠️ Three post-1991 trajectories to compare: institutional anchoring in the European Union and NATO for central and eastern Europe; shock therapy and its consequences in Russia; and diversification with retained decision-making autonomy for India.
11. ⚠️ The finding on convergence: partial, uneven and reversible, distributed according to prior capability, geography and institutional access - not a destination.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using evidence from this session rather than from later decades, show that the claim of universal liberal convergence was already testable and already failing between 1991 and 1995.

**Model answer:** Four pieces of contemporary evidence test it. First, institutional neutrality: the account records that the Americans kept firm control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund so that states applying for loans had to ensure their internal policies were acceptable to the USA, and that when the Bank's new president proposed in 1995 to do more for debt relief, good government, education and health, Washington opposed it and argued for strict austerity. Second, the terms of trade offered by the other great liberal institution: the account's own judgement is that the common agricultural policy causes a great deal of damage to the economies of the developing world, and the 1987 surpluses were cleared by selling cheaply abroad, giving butter away, making animal feed and burning the oldest stock. Third, the conduct of the order in practice: within months of the Gulf coalition the Yugoslav wars began and the same institutions did not act, while safe areas were declared without the troops to defend them. Fourth, the record of transition itself: within a year of the Soviet dissolution Russian prices were on average thirty times higher and over a million people had lost their jobs, while across eastern Europe the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism and very little help was forthcoming from the west. All four were visible by 1995 to anyone making the claim at the time.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that nothing changed after 1991. Something real did: the Council authorised effective enforcement in the Gulf, integration deepened and widened, and ten states joined the European Union on 1 May 2004. The defensible finding is that convergence was partial, uneven and reversible, and that its distribution followed prior capability, geography and institutional access - which is a statement about distribution, not a denial of change.
