### 1. The learner's problem: how to sound decisive without being teleological

⚠️ The examiner wants a verdict. The evidence forbids a destination. ⚠️ Resolving that tension is the last skill this topic teaches, and it has a technique.

⚠️ **The technique: make the verdict about mechanisms, not about directions.** ⚠️ "History was moving towards liberal democracy" is a direction and is unsupportable. ⚠️ "Systems that lose performance legitimacy and then reform faster than they deliver become vulnerable to organised alternatives, and whether they survive depends on whether their coercive apparatus will still act" is a mechanism, is decisive, and is supported by every case in this session including the ones that went the other way.

⚠️ **Why teleology loses marks specifically.** ⚠️ It is not merely a philosophical preference. ⚠️ A teleological answer cannot explain China in June 1989, Bulgaria in June 1990, the Serbian Radicals in 2003, or Russia between 1992 and 1995 - all of which are in this session's own evidence, and all of which an examiner can use to test the answer.

### 2. The five interpretations, set against each other

| ⚠️ Interpretation | ⚠️ Its best evidence in this session | ⚠️ What it cannot account for |
|---|---|---|
| **Western triumph** | The arms competition; the 1987 treaty; the strategic-defence programme; economic superiority visible through increasing contact with the West | The Soviet decisions that the same source records: the non-intervention choice, the engineered replacement of satellite leaders, the Afghan withdrawal on reciprocal terms, and Reagan's own responsiveness to Gorbachev's initiatives |
| **Structural failure** | The command economy's inability to convert resources into welfare; Gorbachev's own "absurd situation"; the federal design's ready-made exit units | Timing. The structures had been failing for years, and China's survival under comparable pressure shows the structures did not determine the outcome |
| **Leadership agency** | The 1985 accession; glasnost's dated measures; the non-coercion decision; Yeltsin's rival authority; Milosevic's and Tudjman's mobilisation | The constraints: Ligachev controlled the apparatus; the scale arithmetic made regional coercion impractical; Gorbachev's own reforms produced results he neither wanted nor foresaw |
| **Society from below** | Solidarity's August 1988 strikes; the churches; Charter 77 and the national strike; half a million miners and 42 demands; "a triumph of 'people power'" | The variation. Romania had no equivalent movement and still fell; Bulgaria had one and the renamed ruling party still won |
| **Postcolonial critique** | Institutional asymmetry in the financial bodies; the agricultural policy's damage to developing economies; the record of selective intervention | It is a critique of the **order claim** rather than a causal account of 1989-91, and using it as the latter overreaches its evidence |

⚠️ **How to deploy this in an answer.** ⚠️ Choose one as the organising frame, concede the strongest point of one rival, and use a third to supply the qualification. ⚠️ Three interpretations in a 20-mark answer is optimal; five is a catalogue.

### 3. The boundary map, in the form to memorise

```text
  TOPIC 15  Cold War structure and long chronology
            -> containment, Berlin 1948 and 1961, Korea, Cuba,
               Vietnam, deterrence, non-alignment, detente
            HANDOVER SENTENCE: "The rivalry whose terminal
            phase this answer examines is treated in full
            elsewhere; here it appears only as the condition
            that was being wound up."

  TOPIC 16  United Nations design and institutional performance
            -> Charter, organs, veto, peacekeeping as an
               institutional question, reform logic, Iraq 2003
            HANDOVER SENTENCE: "The Council appears here only
            to fix the legal category of particular actions."

  TOPIC 17  China's revolution, transformation and reform
            -> 1911 to 1949, Maoist campaigns, post-1978 reform
            USE HERE: only the June 1989 comparison, which the
            account's own end-of-chapter question invites.

  TOPIC 20  Thematic world economy and population
            -> Bretton Woods, North-South, oil, debt,
               globalisation as economic history, 2008
            USE HERE: only globalisation as the ideological
            component of the order claim.

  CURRENT IR AND ECONOMY
            -> India's 1991 reform package; strategic autonomy
               as doctrine; present-day groupings, alliances
               and diplomacy; everything after 2004 on
               enlargement
            USE HERE: only the changed systemic context.

  PERIOD CEILING
            -> the core account's narrative horizon is its 2013
               edition; any later claim must be a separately
               dated current item, and exactly one is used in
               this session, in Lesson 9.
```

### 4. Directive and demand map

| ⚠️ If the question says | ⚠️ It is really testing | ⚠️ Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Analyse the causes of the collapse of communism* | Structural decay **plus** reform choices **plus** the decision not to coerce | "Communism failed" |
| *Explain why the Cold War came to an end* | The four endings, and which one the question means | A narrative of the whole Cold War |
| *Show how this affected international relations* | The new problems the account lists: nationalism-driven conflict, weapons supervision, transition economics, allied disunity, and the single-superpower fact | A list of events after 1991 |
| *Explain how German reunification combined revolution and diplomacy* | Four stages: popular, electoral, great-power, legal | "The Wall fell and Germany was united" |
| *Discuss the breakup of Yugoslavia* | Nationalism as a political product, with named agents and minority arithmetic | "Ancient hatreds" |
| *Did a "new world order" emerge?* | The three tests - capability, rules, legitimacy - applied to the Gulf, the Balkans and the institutions | A post-2001 current-affairs narrative |
| *Explain European integration's change* | From security by economic means to the institutional settlement of the Cold War's end | A treaty timeline |
| *Redrawal of boundaries after 1991* | Soviet successors, Yugoslav successors **and** German reunification | Only the USSR |
| *Assess the order from a Global South perspective* | Institutional asymmetry, uneven terms, continuing agency, and the refusal to treat the South as one actor | A grievance essay without evidence |

### 5. Mark-scaled structures

| ⚠️ Marks | ⚠️ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Qualified thesis → **two** causes of collapse **or two** features of the order → one qualification drawn from a named case → verdict |
| **15** | Qualified thesis → why communist rule collapsed → what replaced it in Europe → the contested order → verdict with an explicit non-teleology clause |
| **20** | All of the above → **plus** German reunification, the Gulf, Yugoslavia and Kosovo, European and alliance enlargement, Global South agency and India's recalibration → and an explicit statement of which interpretation is being used and which rival is being conceded → verdict |

### 6. Qualified thesis templates

- ⚠️ **On collapse:** "Soviet communism was not defeated; it was dismantled from above by leaders who tried to reform it and then declined to shoot in order to save it - and the refusal was a decision, not a law of history."
- ⚠️ **On agency:** "Moscow's refusal to coerce and Poland's capacity to mobilise are the two halves of one explanation; the first supplied the opportunity and the second supplied the agency."
- ⚠️ **On Germany:** "The street opened the German question and the treaty closed it: without 9 November 1989 there would have been nothing to settle, and without 12 September 1990 there would have been nothing settled."
- ⚠️ **On Yugoslavia:** "Yugoslavia did not break up because its peoples could not live together; it broke up because, once the federal and communist framework dissolved, political entrepreneurs found ethnic mobilisation the cheapest route to power - and Slovenia shows what the same act produced where the minority arithmetic differed."
- ⚠️ **On the order:** "The post-1991 order was unipolar in capability but plural in sovereignty and legitimacy: the Gulf coalition showed unusual alignment, while Yugoslavia exposed weak consensus over civil war and intervention."
- ⚠️ **On Europe:** "European integration changed from a device for containing Germany into a device for absorbing the post-communist east - and both functions were pursued without resolving the sovereignty question either raised."
- ⚠️ **On non-teleology:** "The collapse of Soviet communism was not proof that every state would converge on one political-economic model; outcomes depended on institutions, social coalitions, state capacity and external constraints, and this period's own record of divergence is the evidence."

### 7. Verdict scaffolds

- ⚠️ **"Why did communism collapse?"** → "Economic failure made the system indefensible, reform made criticism legal, nationalism supplied the alternative loyalty, and the refusal to use force made the outcome irreversible - and the last of these was a decision, as China's contrary decision in June 1989 demonstrates."
- ⚠️ **"Yugoslavia?"** → "A state failure converted into an ethnic war by leaders who found nationalism the most available political resource, and prolonged by external hesitation and by recognition granted without minority guarantees."
- ⚠️ **"New world order?"** → "Real as a brief concentration of capability and Council alignment, but incomplete as an order: Yugoslavia, uneven globalisation and continuing postcolonial sovereignty exposed the limits of universal convergence."
- ⚠️ **"European integration?"** → "It changed from a device for containing Germany into a device for absorbing the post-communist east, and by 2009 it had completed that absorption without settling either the sovereignty question or the distributive one."
- ⚠️ **"Continuity or rupture?"** → "A structural rupture in the distribution of power and in the map of Europe, inside a substantial continuity in the logic by which power was used."

### 8. Factual-risk control list for this topic

⚠️ These are the errors that cost marks most often on this material. ⚠️ Check an answer against them before submitting it.

1. ❌ Do not say Gorbachev intended to destroy communism. ✅ He tried to reform and save it, inside the one-party framework.
2. ❌ Do not treat glasnost and perestroika as synonyms. ✅ Openness and restructuring had different mechanisms and different speeds.
3. ❌ Do not merge the Berlin Wall's breach, the Two Plus Four settlement, German reunification and Soviet dissolution into one event. ✅ 9 November 1989; 12 September 1990; 3 October 1990; December 1991.
4. ❌ Do not omit the August 1991 coup from the dissolution sequence. ✅ It failed, discredited the Party and accelerated republican exits.
5. ❌ Do not explain Yugoslavia by ancient hatreds. ✅ Leadership choices, institutional failure, minority arithmetic and external hesitation all appear in the record, and the account says war was not inevitable.
6. ❌ Do not say European integration began after the Cold War. ✅ Its roots are the Coal and Steel Community of 1951 and the Treaty of Rome of 1957.
7. ❌ Do not call the Gulf War ordinary United Nations peacekeeping. ✅ It was Council-authorised coalition enforcement by member states cooperating with Kuwait.
8. ❌ Do not say the United Nations bombed Serbia in 1999. ✅ NATO carried out the campaign without express Council authorisation; the Council authorised the subsequent presences.
9. ❌ Do not invent casualty, refugee, membership or economic figures. ✅ Use only the figures recorded in this session, each with its qualifier and its source.
10. ❌ Do not date the sequence loosely. ✅ Gorbachev 1985; Poland's opening 1988-89; Wall 9 November 1989; Malta 2-3 December 1989; Two Plus Four 12 September 1990; reunification 3 October 1990; the Gulf authorisation 29 November 1990 with a deadline of 15 January 1991; the "no longer adversaries" declaration November 1990; Comecon 28 June 1991; Warsaw Pact 1 July 1991; failed coup 19-21 August 1991; Gorbachev's resignation 25 December 1991; Maastricht agreed December 1991 and ratified by October 1993; Dayton November 1995; Kosovo campaign 24 March to 10 June 1999; NATO rounds 1999 and 2004; European Union enlargement 1 May 2004.
11. ❌ Do not extend the narrative past the core account's 2013 horizon. ✅ Exactly one separately dated current item is used in this session, in Lesson 9.
12. ❌ Do not write that the collapse of communism proved socialism failed. ⚠️ What collapsed was a **regime type** in particular states; the doctrinal distinctions between socialism, communism and social democracy are a separate study, and the record in this session contains communists who won free elections in Bulgaria in June 1990 and a former communist elected president of Poland in December 1995.
13. ❌ Do not treat "the Global South" as one actor, or as a passive zone.
14. ❌ Do not adopt attributed judgements in your own voice. ✅ Glenny, Volkogonov, Guyatt, Blum, Chomsky, Byers, Hutton, Beer, Anderson, Chubarov and Rothkopf are quoted authors in this session.
15. ❌ Do not present the post-1991 transitions as uniform. ✅ Poland recovering by 1994; Russia with prices thirty times higher within a year; Czechoslovakia separating peacefully in 1992; Yugoslavia at war; ten states acceding in 2004.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This lesson is the answer-writing apparatus for every stem the topic can generate in either paper.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Any evaluative directive - assess, critically examine, discuss, comment, elucidate - on the end of the Cold War, the collapse of communism, the new world order, the redrawal of boundaries or European integration.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Adopting an interpretation without naming it. ⚠️ Name the frame, concede a rival, qualify with a third.
2. ⚠️ Writing a verdict about direction rather than mechanism. ⚠️ Mechanisms survive the counter-examples; directions do not.
3. ⚠️ Straying into a neighbouring topic. ⚠️ Use the handover sentences.
4. ⚠️ Running out of qualification. ⚠️ Every thesis template above has its qualification built in; use it as the closing clause.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Rupture: the bipolar alliance structure, the Soviet state, the map of eastern Europe, the legitimacy of one-party socialist rule in Europe, and the suppression of some conflicts.
2. ⚠️ Continuity: great-power interest as the driver of intervention, asymmetry inside institutions, nationalism as a mobilising resource, inequality of capacity, and contested sovereignty as a central value.
3. ⚠️ The verdict: a structural rupture in the distribution of power and in the map, inside a substantial continuity in the logic by which power was used.
4. ⚠️ Five interpretations with their strongest evidence and their limits: Western triumph, structural failure, leadership agency, society from below, postcolonial critique.
5. ⚠️ Technique against teleology: make the verdict about mechanisms, not directions, because mechanisms survive the counter-examples.
6. ⚠️ Four counter-examples that defeat teleology: China in June 1989, Bulgaria in June 1990, Russia from 1992 to 1995, and the Serbian Radicals in 2003.
7. ⚠️ Boundary handovers: Topic 15 for the long Cold War; Topic 16 for United Nations design; Topic 17 for China; Topic 20 for the world economy; international relations and the economy for India's policy and for everything after 2004.
8. ⚠️ Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20, with the 20 requiring an explicit statement of the interpretation used and the rival conceded.
9. ⚠️ Seven qualified thesis templates and five verdict scaffolds, each carrying its own qualification.
10. ⚠️ Fifteen factual-risk controls, of which the date list in control 10 is the single most examinable item in the topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** An examiner asks you to "critically examine whether the end of the Cold War represented a rupture or a continuity in international politics". Give the thesis, the two supporting movements and the closing qualification you would use, in about eight sentences.

**Model answer:** The thesis: the end of the Cold War was a structural rupture in the distribution of power and in the political map, inside a substantial continuity in the logic by which power was used. The first movement establishes the rupture with dated evidence: the Warsaw Pact was dissolved on 1 July 1991 after its members had declared in November 1990 that they were no longer adversaries; the Soviet state dissolved into separate republics in December 1991, ending communist rule in Russia after 74 years; Germany was reunified on 3 October 1990, Czechoslovakia separated in 1992, Yugoslavia broke into five states, and ten states joined the European Union on 1 May 2004. The second movement establishes the continuity from the same source base: intervention continued to follow great-power interest, since the Gulf coalition acted where oil supplies were threatened while no comparable action followed East Timor in 1975 and reluctance in Rwanda in 1994 preceded a massacre of some half a million people; institutional asymmetry persisted, with firm American control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund so that borrowers had to make their internal policies acceptable; and nationalism remained the most available mobilising resource, in Yugoslavia and afterwards, the extreme nationalist Serbian Radicals emerging as Serbia's largest single party at the end of 2003. The closing qualification refuses teleology explicitly: the same rupture produced a European Union accession in one place and a war in another, a negotiated German settlement and a violent Romanian overthrow, an agreed Czech-Slovak separation and a Bosnian catastrophe, which is why the period is better described by the mechanisms that produced its divergent outcomes than by any direction it is supposed to have taken.

**Misconception to avoid:** Balancing the two sides so evenly that no position emerges. "Critically examine" asks for a graded verdict, not neutrality. The verdict here is graded and specific - rupture in distribution and map, continuity in logic - and each half is carried by named, dated evidence, which is what distinguishes a considered judgement from a refusal to make one.

## Probable framings recorded in the topic's study material and in the core account

⚠️ Since no question is owned, the best available guide to how this topic would be examined is the set of framings recorded in the topic's own study material and by the account itself. ⚠️ All of them are covered by the original Mains practice below or by the lesson-level examination-use sections above.

✅ **Framings recorded for the core narrative of this topic:** analyse the causes of the collapse of communism in the USSR and eastern Europe; explain how German reunification combined popular revolution and great-power diplomacy; the statement for discussion that "the Cold War ended through interaction between structural decay and political choice"; whether the Gulf War confirmed a new world order or only a brief alignment of power and institutions; the breakup of Yugoslavia as a post-Cold-War crisis of nationalism and state failure; an assessment of the United States unipolar moment and its limits from a Global South perspective; and how European integration changed from a Cold-War peace project into a post-Cold-War enlargement project.

✅ **Framings recorded for the debate and evaluation material:** the statement for comment that "Gorbachev tried to reform communism but ended by dissolving it"; whether the Cold War was ended more by Soviet weakness, Western pressure or political choice; why German reunification was both a popular revolution and a diplomatic settlement; why the breakup of Yugoslavia became the most violent crisis of post-Cold-War Europe; a critical examination of the idea of a new world order after 1991; whether the Gulf War demonstrated a durable post-Cold-War order; an assessment of the post-1991 order from a postcolonial and Indian perspective; and whether European integration was driven more by peace, economics or geopolitical strategy.

✅ **From the core account's own end-of-chapter questions for the chapters used in this session:** explain why the Cold War came to an end, and show how this affected international relations; consider the view that if Gorbachev had followed different policies, the USSR could have survived, in the same way that communism survived in China; the statement for assessment that "it was Gorbachev's reluctance to commit himself to sufficiently radical changes that led to the break-up of the Soviet Union"; explain why the collapse of the USSR was followed by serious economic and political problems; and the comment invited on whether "Putin's Russia may well have been a police state, but at least he rescued the country from the chaos of the Yeltsin years."

## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return over nine years should change preparation rather than reduce it, and three things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through other papers and other syllabus areas.** ✅ Every question in the span that touches this topic's subject matter is routed to international relations. ⚠️ The material taught here is the historical foundation of answers written in Paper II, and a candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip the topic will meet NATO expansion, post-Cold-War nationalism and European Union membership elsewhere without the depth that makes an answer good.
2. ⚠️ **The Paper I syllabus clause covers it directly.** ✅ The syllabus names world history from the eighteenth century onward, including the redrawal of national boundaries and political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ This topic is the syllabus's single best case of both clauses operating together: the largest boundary redrawal of the late twentieth century, and the terminal test of one of the three named philosophies in the states that had institutionalised it.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 21 lists seven qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the four-endings thesis - that rivalry, bloc, state and order ended separately and by different mechanisms - and the rupture-and-continuity thesis, that the distribution of power and the map changed while the logic by which power was used largely did not.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the system and its reform, Lessons 1 to 6

### Cumulative check A1 - from stagnation to loss of control, in one causal chain

**Question:** Construct a single causal chain from the condition of the Soviet system in the early 1980s to the miners' strike of July 1989, naming the mechanism at each step.

**Model answer:** The chain has six links. First, a command economy that was over-centralised, based almost completely on state ownership and control and weighted towards defence and heavy industry left consumer goods in short supply, which removed the performance legitimacy that had replaced revolutionary legitimacy - Gorbachev conceded the paradox himself, calling it an absurd situation that the world's biggest producer of steel, fuel and energy suffered shortages through waste and inefficiency. Second, increasing contact with the West in the 1980s supplied a comparison, a trend and, decisively, an attribution: it showed that the leaders and the system were the cause. Third, a reforming leadership arrived in March 1985 intending to save the system rather than to end it, and deployed two instruments of very different speeds - glasnost, which worked by permission and took effect at once, and perestroika, which required institutional redesign and could only show results over years. Fourth, the permissions accumulated fast and visibly: dissidents released, the Sakharovs returned from Gorky in December 1986, Bukharin declared innocent, the January 1988 law against confinement in mental institutions, cultural unions electing new leaderships in May 1986, and the 1988 Party Conference and May 1989 Congress televised. Fifth, the economic instrument mis-specified its measure: the Law on State Enterprises tied wages to output measured in roubles, so enterprises shifted to expensive goods and cut cheap ones, wages rose, money was printed, inflation and the deficit soared, and basic goods - soap, washing powder, razor blades, cups and saucers, television sets and food - disappeared. Sixth, the two speeds collided in a coalfield: in July 1989 Siberian miners found no soap at the end of a shift, asked what kind of regime it was if they could not even get washed, struck, were joined across Siberia, Kazakhstan and the Donbass until half a million were out in the first major strike since 1917, and escalated their 42 demands from food and conditions to independent trade unions and an end to the Party's privileged position.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the chain as a story of inevitable decline. Each link is a mechanism that could have been broken: a different measure of output would not have emptied the shops; a different sequencing of permission and delivery would not have accumulated criticism faster than results; and a different decision about force, as in China in June 1989, would have produced a different ending to the same chain.

### Cumulative check A2 - the two halves of the 1989 explanation

**Question:** Why does the account's own narrative require both Moscow's restraint and eastern European organisation, and what happens to an answer that supplies only one?

**Model answer:** Because the account records both and each is insufficient alone. Restraint is recorded directly: critics became more daring as they realised Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them, none of the communist governments except Romania was prepared to use sufficient force once no help could be expected from Moscow, and the reason was partly arithmetical, since holding down the whole of eastern Europe simultaneously would have required a huge commitment that a leadership just extracted from Afghanistan did not want. Organisation is recorded equally directly: the process began in Poland in August 1988 when Solidarity organised huge anti-government strikes that eventually forced the government to allow free elections, and the account calls the regional outcome a triumph of people power by demonstrators too numerous to shoot. An answer that supplies only restraint makes 1989 a decision taken in Moscow and cannot explain why Poland moved first, why Romania's route was violent or why Bulgaria's renamed party won. An answer that supplies only organisation cannot explain 1953, 1956 or 1968, when comparable movements met force, or Polish martial law in 1981, when the same union was banned and thousands arrested because the army still feared Russian intervention.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating this as a balanced-sounding formula rather than an argument. The two halves are testable against the cases: Poland in 1981 is restraint absent, organisation present; Romania in 1989 is restraint present, organisation absent; and the different outcomes in each are what make the conjunction an explanation rather than a compromise.

## Block B - the collapse and the new map, Lessons 7 to 12

### Cumulative check B1 - five routes, two variables

**Question:** Sort Poland, East Germany, Czechoslovakia, Romania and Bulgaria by the presence of an organised opposition counterpart and of a divided ruling party, and state what each combination produced.

**Model answer:** Poland and Czechoslovakia had both: an organised counterpart - Solidarity with the Church behind it, and Charter 77 under Havel - and a leadership that had either already tried force and knew its cost or had lost its reformist credibility. Both produced transfer of power without mass violence, Poland through the negotiated April 1989 agreement and the June elections, Czechoslovakia through a demonstration on 17 November 1989, a national strike and Havel's election on 29 December. East Germany had a movement in New Forum around the Protestant Church but its decisive division was inside the elite: Honecker wanted the army to fire, his colleagues refused, they dropped him, Krenz conceded, and the Wall was breached on 9 November 1989 - elite collapse under exit pressure, since thousands were already escaping through Hungary's opened Austrian frontier. Bulgaria had a divided ruling party but no comparable counterpart: the Politburo voted Zhivkov out on 10 November 1989, the day after the Berlin Wall was breached, though the account's wording gives December 1989; the party renamed itself the Bulgarian Socialist Party and won the free elections of June 1990, probably because its propaganda argued that capitalism would bring economic disaster - managed continuity. Romania had neither: opposition had been so ruthlessly crushed that there was no equivalent of Solidarity or Charter 77, and the result was violence at Timisoara and in Bucharest, the execution of the Ceausescus on 25 December 1989, and a National Salvation Front full of former communists under Ion Iliescu, who won the presidency in May 1990.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing the grid is a causal law. Albania does not fit it - communism there was installed in 1945 by an indigenous resistance movement rather than by the Soviet army, and its collapse came later, in 1991-92 - and East Germany's route was dominated by an additional variable, an adjacent co-national state that made exit a political weapon. The grid is an answer-writing device that generates comparisons; it is not a substitute for the cases.

### Cumulative check B2 - why a state ended rather than a government

**Question:** Name the four conditions of Soviet dissolution, attach a dated fact to each, and explain why the fourth is a trigger rather than a cause.

**Model answer:** The first condition is units capable of acting as states: the Soviet Union was a federal state of fifteen republics each with its own parliament, kept under tight control since Stalin's time until glasnost and perestroika encouraged them to hope for more powers and more independence. The second is a centre unable or unwilling to prevent exit: national income fell in 1990 and by about 15 per cent in 1991, and Moscow's refusal to recognise Lithuania's declaration of March 1990 changed nothing. The third is a rival authority to rally to: Yeltsin, excluded from the Supreme Soviet by conservatives, was elected president of the parliament of the Russian republic in May 1990, creating two authorities with different sources of legitimacy in the same city. The fourth is the trigger: the coup of 19-21 August 1991, mounted by a self-declared State Committee on the State of Emergency, which failed within three days, destroyed much of the remaining legitimacy of the party and accelerated republican exits, after which republican leaders agreed on 8 December 1991 that the union had ceased to exist and created a commonwealth, eight further republics joined at Alma-Ata on 21 December, and Gorbachev resigned on 25 December 1991. It is a trigger rather than a cause because the first three conditions were already in place and would have continued to operate; what the coup destroyed was the last version of the union that still had a plausible chance, a smaller voluntary association of willing republics.

**Misconception to avoid:** Saying that the USSR simply became Russia. Fifteen union republics became independent states; Russia was the largest successor but not the continuation of the whole, and the Baltic states stood outside even that framework, because their claim rested on the illegitimacy of the 1940 incorporation and was therefore a restoration of statehood rather than a secession.

### Cumulative check B3 - what the transition did not deliver

**Question:** Using the record of Russia and of eastern Europe generally, state what the removal of communist rule did not by itself produce, and show that the finding is one of divergence rather than uniform failure.

**Model answer:** It did not by itself produce a functioning market. Shock therapy from January 1992 removed price controls from about 90 per cent of goods; by the end of the year prices were on average thirty times higher, wages did not keep pace, over a million people lost their jobs, thousands lived in tents and many relied on food parcels from abroad. It did not produce broad ownership: vouchers worth 10,000 roubles were the equivalent of about £35, most workers could not afford shares, managers accumulated vouchers, and by the end of 1995 most former state industry had passed to a small group of financiers whose profits came from reintroduced subsidies rather than from the market, with total investment falling by two-thirds. It did not produce settled democratic institutions: the constitution was a Soviet leftover unclear on the division of powers, Yeltsin had no supporting party, and in the December 1995 Duma elections the Communist Party and its allies were the largest grouping at 186 of 450 seats. And across the region generally the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism, with very little help forthcoming from the west. But the finding is divergence, not failure: Poland showed clear signs of recovery by 1994, East Germany had the resources of the former West Germany behind it, Czechoslovakia separated peacefully in 1992, and ten states entered the European Union on 1 May 2004.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting this into nostalgia for the previous system. The same body of evidence records shortages, queues, a calculation in early 1990 that about a quarter of the Soviet population was below the poverty line, and a social record that a named correspondent called atrocious. The claim is conditional - removing a system does not install another - not comparative.

## Block C - the order and its tests, Lessons 13 to 17

### Cumulative check C1 - three tests, three cases

**Question:** Apply the capability, rules and legitimacy tests to the Gulf, Bosnia and Kosovo, and state what the three cases together establish about the post-1991 order.

**Model answer:** On capability all three cases pass: a coalition of over 600,000 from more than thirty nations reversed Iraq's occupation of Kuwait within four days of ground fighting; NATO's air power ended the Bosnian Serb bombardment of Sarajevo in 1995; and eleven weeks of air attacks forced Milosevic to accept terms in June 1999. On rules the three diverge sharply. The Gulf action rested on a Security Council resolution of 29 November 1990 authorising member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means after 15 January 1991 - a clear basis. Bosnia exposed the gap between mandate and means: UNPROFOR had no supporting artillery or aircraft, Srebrenica, Zepa and Gorazde were declared safe areas without enough troops to defend them, and command effectively shifted, since the account records that the United Nations position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation. Kosovo had no express Council authorisation for the use of force at all, which is why the account calls the bombing controversial, though the Council did authorise the subsequent civil and security presences on 10 June 1999. On legitimacy all three are contested: the Gulf response is undercut by the same powers having armed Iraq and ignored the Kurds in 1988 and East Timor in 1975; Bosnia by international hesitation before atrocity; Kosovo by an immediate escalation in which hundreds of thousands of Albanians were driven out after the bombing began. Together they establish that the post-1991 arrangement was unipolar in capability and plural in rules and legitimacy - an order in the first sense only.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Gulf as the rule and the Balkans as the exception. Chronologically the Balkans came almost immediately after, and the account's own list of post-Cold-War problems predicts exactly this: a conflict not directly affecting the interests of East or West would probably be left to find its own solution, bloody or otherwise.

### Cumulative check C2 - attribution under pressure

**Question:** Write four sentences about mass violence in this topic, each containing a figure or a characterisation, each correctly attributed. Then state the rule they illustrate.

**Model answer:** First: the account read for this session records that Croat and Muslim supporters of the fascist regime set up by the Italians to rule Croatia and Bosnia during the Second World War were responsible for the murder of some 700,000 Serbs. Second: the same account records that atrocities were committed by all sides in Bosnia but that it seemed the Bosnian Serbs were the most guilty, that they carried out ethnic cleansing by driving Muslim civilians from Serb-majority areas into camps and in some cases murdering all the men, and that two million refugees had been driven from their homes. Third: at Srebrenica in July 1995 the account records the killing of about 8,000 Muslims, and the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia characterised that killing as genocide in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004. Fourth: after the NATO campaign began in March 1999 the account records that Milosevic ordered a campaign of ethnic cleansing which drove hundreds of thousands of ethnic Albanians into Albania, Macedonia and Montenegro. The rule they illustrate is that a figure without an owner is not evidence: the qualifier, the source and the period must travel with the number, and responsibility must be attributed to named governments, leaders and forces rather than to peoples.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that attribution weakens an answer by making it hedged. It does the opposite. "Hundreds of thousands died in the Balkans" is unfalsifiable and therefore worth nothing; "the account records about 8,000 Muslims killed at Srebrenica in July 1995, judicially characterised as genocide in 2004" is checkable, precise and demonstrates exactly the evidential discipline a 20-mark answer is marked for.

## Block D - the settlement and the verdict, Lessons 18 to 21

### Cumulative check D1 - two enlargements, one settlement

**Question:** Explain why the European Union enlargement of 1 May 2004 and the NATO enlargement of the same year are best treated as one settlement, and state the strongest objection to that settlement.

**Model answer:** They are one settlement because for the acceding states they were one project. Ten states joined the Union on 1 May 2004 - the Czech Republic, Cyprus, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia - and seven joined the alliance in the same year, after three had joined it in 1999. Both memberships served the same three purposes: a guarantee against reversal of the 1989-91 changes, an external anchor for domestic reform on the precedent of Greece, Portugal and Spain, who had been excluded from the Community while their political systems were undemocratic, and a statement about which Europe these states belonged to. Both were driven by applicant demand rather than imposed, and both completed in the same year the process that the dissolution of Comecon on 28 June 1991 and of the Warsaw Pact on 1 July 1991 had opened. The strongest objection is that the security half sits uneasily with the instrument that ended the Cold War: in November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were no longer adversaries and that none of their weapons would be used except in self-defence, and extending one of the two alliances into the space the other had vacated is read by the former adversary as a security development regardless of the intentions of those extending it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Resolving the objection in either direction and presenting the resolution as a historical finding. What this topic can establish is the fact of the two enlargements, the demand that drove them, the functional shift documented in Bosnia and Kosovo and the friction recorded even among western allies over European defence plans in 2003. Whether the settlement was wise depends on decades outside this topic's period, and is a question for the study of contemporary international relations.

### Cumulative check D2 - the whole topic in one verdict

**Question:** State the topic's central verdict in one sentence, then defend each of its two clauses with three pieces of dated evidence.

**Model answer:** The verdict: the end of the Cold War was a structural rupture in the distribution of power and in the political map, inside a substantial continuity in the logic by which power was used. For the first clause: the Warsaw Treaty Organisation was dissolved on 1 July 1991 and Comecon on 28 June 1991, ending the institutional architecture of one bloc; the Soviet state dissolved into separate republics in December 1991, with Gorbachev resigning on 25 December and communist rule in Russia ending after 74 years; and the map of Europe was redrawn by German reunification on 3 October 1990, the Czech-Slovak separation of 1992, the break-up of Yugoslavia into five states, and the accession of ten states to the European Union on 1 May 2004. For the second clause: intervention continued to follow great-power interest, since the account states that the primary concern of the West and the great powers in the Gulf was their own self-interest and supplies East Timor in 1975 as the comparison, while reluctance in Rwanda in 1994 preceded the massacre of some half a million people; institutional asymmetry persisted, with firm American control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund so that borrowers had to make their internal policies acceptable, and Washington opposing the Bank president's debt-relief priorities in 1995; and nationalism remained the most available mobilising resource, with the extreme nationalist Serbian Radicals the largest single party in Serbia at the end of 2003 and the account recording western and European disappointment at the result.

**Misconception to avoid:** Allowing the continuity clause to slide into the claim that nothing changed. Fifteen new states, a reunified Germany, five Yugoslav successors, a dissolved alliance and a twenty-five-member Union are not nothing. The claim is about the logic of power, not about the furniture of the international system, and keeping the two apart is what makes the verdict defensible.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions below are **original practice**, written for this session. ⚠️ None is a previous-year question, and none should be cited as one. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to its stated word ceiling and carries a scoring note that applies only to that question.

## Question 1 - 10 marks - Answer in 150 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** "Gorbachev's reforms failed because they moved at two incompatible speeds." Examine.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** The claim is largely sound. Glasnost operated by permission and took effect immediately: dissidents released, the Sakharovs returned from Gorky in December 1986, Bukharin declared innocent, the January 1988 law against psychiatric confinement, and the 1988 Party Conference and May 1989 Congress televised. Perestroika required redesigning the Party, economy and government, and could only work slowly; growth was unchanged in 1988-89 and national income fell about 15 per cent in 1991. Expectations therefore outran delivery, and criticism acquired legality, organisation and a national audience - the account notes glasnost turning against the Party itself. But speed alone is incomplete. The Law on State Enterprises failed because its measure was wrong, tying wages to output valued in roubles, which emptied shops of soap and razor blades and produced the July 1989 miners' strike. Design error, not merely pace, converted disappointment into the first major strike since 1917.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 1.** ⚠️ Full marks require the two-speed mechanism **and** the qualification that a design error, not pace alone, produced the decisive shortage. ⚠️ An answer that lists reforms without naming the permission-versus-delivery asymmetry cannot exceed half marks, however accurate its dates.

## Question 2 - 10 marks - Answer in 150 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** Distinguish, with one example each, between Security Council-authorised coalition enforcement, United Nations peacekeeping, and alliance action without express authorisation. Bring out why the distinction matters.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** Coalition enforcement: the Gulf, 1991. The resolution of 29 November 1990 authorised member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means after 15 January 1991; over 600,000 troops from more than thirty nations served under national and coalition command, not United Nations command. Peacekeeping: UNPROFOR in Bosnia from February 1992, 13,000 troops deployed with the parties' consent, distributing aid with, as the record states, no supporting artillery or aircraft, and declaring safe areas it could not defend. Alliance action without express authorisation: NATO's Kosovo air campaign of 24 March to 10 June 1999, which the account calls controversial precisely because the Council had not authorised force, although it authorised the subsequent presences on 10 June. The distinction matters because the order claim of 1990 rested on collective security. Only the first case supports it; the second shows mandate without means, and the third shows capability without authority.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 2.** ⚠️ This question is marked on category precision, not on narrative. ⚠️ Award nothing for describing the wars; award fully only where command, legal basis and purpose are separated for each category and the consequence for the order claim is drawn. ⚠️ Writing "the UN fought Iraq" or "the UN bombed Serbia" caps the answer at one third.

## Question 3 - 15 marks - Answer in 250 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** "The breakup of Yugoslavia was produced by politics, not by history." Critically examine.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The statement is defensible with one qualification. Against inherited hatred the record is decisive. Under Tito the nationalities lived peacefully, having apparently put behind them wartime memories that included, on the account's estimate, the murder of some 700,000 Serbs by supporters of the Italian-installed fascist regime. Grievances far worse than any available in 1990 had been neutralised for decades, so hatred had to be reactivated, not released. The reactivators are named. Milosevic, president of Serbia from 1988, bears much responsibility: he deliberately stirred Serbian nationalism using Kosovo, claiming terror against the Serbian minority though there was no definite evidence, and won the 1990 elections as a nationalist. Tudjman, a former communist jailed under Tito, stirred Croatian nationalism and refused guarantees to Croatia's Serbs, which supplied Serbia's stated excuse. The account states the counterfactual: war was not inevitable, and statesmanlike leaders making concessions could have found peaceful solutions. The qualification is that politics operated on structure. Inflation reached 90 per cent in 1986 with over a million unemployed, 13 per cent of the workforce; Slovenia was prosperous while parts of Serbia were poverty-stricken; a rotating collective presidency could not impose a distributive settlement; about 600,000 Serbs lived in Croatia and about 1.3 million in Bosnia, which was 44 per cent Muslim, 33 per cent Serb, 17 per cent Croat; and the Community recognised Bosnia without minority guarantees, the same mistake made with Croatia. Slovenia, with few Serbs, escaped war after the same declaration. Politics decided; structure supplied the materials.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 3.** ⚠️ The differentiator is Slovenia. ⚠️ An answer that names Milosevic and Tudjman but omits the control case cannot show that the minority arithmetic mattered, and therefore cannot exceed two thirds. ⚠️ Any unattributed mortality figure is penalised regardless of the rest of the answer's quality.

## Question 4 - 15 marks - Answer in 250 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** Explain why German reunification required four distinct kinds of act, and assess what the sequence shows about how the Cold War ended.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** Four acts were required because four different things were unsettled. The popular act came first: the Berlin Wall was breached on 9 November 1989 after Honecker, who wanted the army to fire, was dropped by colleagues unwilling to cause widespread bloodshed. It ended enforced separation and settled nothing legal, because borders, sovereignty and alliance status remained open - and Poland was suspicious about the territory east of the Oder and Neisse. The electoral act converted demand into mandate: West German parties moved east, Kohl toured, the East German Christian Democrats won overwhelmingly in March 1990, and though de Maiziere wanted gradual union, nearly everybody wanted immediate union. The great-power act supplied consent: the USSR and the USA agreed, Gorbachev promised all Russian troops out by 1994, and France and Britain, less happy, went along - consent obtained in a context where Gorbachev had visited Kohl in June 1989 seeking German financial help. The legal act completed it: the Two Plus Four settlement, formally the Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany, was signed at Moscow on 12 September 1990 and entered into force on 15 March 1991, while reunification took effect at midnight on 3 October 1990. The sequence shows that the Cold War ended by negotiation as much as by revolution. Streets opened questions that only states could close; the closing had a price, and one instrument was integration itself: France, the Netherlands and Belgium supported Maastricht as the best way to contain reunified German power.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 4.** ⚠️ Marks concentrate on the second half of the final sentence. ⚠️ An answer that narrates four stages accurately but does not use them to argue that the ending was negotiated as well as revolutionary has answered "explain" and not "assess", and loses the assessment band entirely.

## Question 5 - 20 marks - Answer in 250 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** "Unipolar in capability, plural in legitimacy." Critically examine the post-1991 international order in the light of this statement.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The statement is the most defensible summary. On capability it is confirmed: the account records that the overriding fact was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower, and the Gulf demonstrated it, over 600,000 troops from more than thirty nations reversing Iraq's occupation within four days of ground fighting. On rules the record divides. The Gulf rested on the resolution of 29 November 1990 authorising member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means after 15 January 1991 - a genuine change from Cold War deadlock. Against that the account records the Panama operation of December 1989 with at least 2,000 civilians killed and an Organization of American States regret resolution carried 20 to 1; the rejection of an International Court judgment on the mining of Nicaraguan harbours and refusal of the ordered compensation; lone negative votes of 130 to 1 in 1985 and 140 to 1 in 1987; and, before September 2001, rejection of the 1972 missile treaty and withdrawal from the 1997 climate protocols. On legitimacy the account's own framing concedes the point, asking how an era so full of hope became so full of hatred, and quoting Guyatt on resentment grounded in a real perception of the imbalance of power. Capacity failed too: Bosnia produced safe areas without troops, Kosovo force without Council authorisation. The qualification is that plurality is not impotence: majorities formed elsewhere outvoted the strongest state repeatedly, and even inside the West many hoped the European Union would counterbalance it.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 5.** ⚠️ This question is marked on the separation of the three tests and on attribution. ⚠️ Every critical item must carry its source - Guyatt, the Organization of American States vote, the Court judgment - and an answer that reproduces them as its own opinion loses a full band. ⚠️ The closing qualification about plurality without impotence is required for the top band.

## Question 6 - 20 marks - Answer in 250 words

> ⚠️ **Original practice.** "The end of the Cold War changed the distribution of power without changing the logic by which power was used." Discuss with reference to Europe and the Global South.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** Distribution changed decisively. Comecon was dissolved on 28 June 1991 and the Warsaw Pact on 1 July 1991 after both blocs declared in November 1990 that they were no longer adversaries; the Soviet state dissolved in December 1991 into separate republics and Gorbachev resigned on 25 December; Germany was reunified on 3 October 1990; Czechoslovakia separated in 1992; Yugoslavia became five states; and ten states joined the European Union on 1 May 2004 while NATO enlarged in 1999 and 2004. Logic changed far less. Intervention still followed interest: the account judges that the primary concern in the Gulf was self-interest over oil, contrasts it with the world ignoring East Timor in 1975, and records reluctance in Rwanda in 1994 where no direct interests were involved, after which some half a million people were massacred. Institutions remained asymmetric: the Americans kept firm control over the World Bank and the Fund, so borrowers had to make their internal policies acceptable, and Washington opposed the Bank president's debt-relief priorities in 1995 in favour of austerity. Europe's own record is indicted too: the account says the common agricultural policy causes a great deal of damage to developing economies, and critics judged the Lome access of 1975 too little. Yet the Global South was not passive: American isolation in votes of 130 to 1 and 140 to 1 shows majorities forming elsewhere. The verdict is rupture in distribution and map, continuity in logic, and a redistribution - not abolition - of options.

⚠️ **Scoring note for Question 6.** ⚠️ The required move is symmetry of evidence: the continuity clause must be supported from both the American and the European record, since testing only one side reads as polemic. ⚠️ An answer that omits the Global South's continuing agency and presents the South as passive loses the top band even if every other element is present.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Five error families account for almost every avoidable mark lost on this topic. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, states the correction with its evidence, and gives the sentence that repairs it.

## Family 1 - Date and sequence collapse

| ⚠️ Error | ✅ Correction | ⚠️ Repair sentence |
|---|---|---|
| The Berlin Wall's breach ended the Cold War | ✅ The rivalry ended by the November 1990 declaration; the breach was 9 November 1989 | "The breach of the Wall on 9 November 1989 ended enforced separation; the adversarial relationship was formally terminated in November 1990." |
| The Wall's breach reunified Germany | ✅ Signature 12 September 1990; reunification 3 October 1990; entry into force 15 March 1991 | "Reunification required an electoral mandate in March 1990, great-power consent, the Two Plus Four settlement of 12 September 1990 and the legal act of 3 October 1990." |
| The USSR collapsed in 1989 | ✅ Dissolution December 1991; Gorbachev resigned 25 December 1991 | "The Soviet state dissolved in December 1991, two years after the Wall." |
| Maastricht created the euro in 1992 | ✅ Maastricht was agreed in December 1991 and ratified by October 1993; the euro was introduced in 12 states on 1 January 2002 | "Maastricht set the path to monetary union; the currency itself appeared in twelve members on 1 January 2002." |
| The Gulf authorisation and the war are the same date | ✅ Resolution 29 November 1990; deadline 15 January 1991; ground phase from 24 February | "Authorisation came on 29 November 1990, the deadline fell on 15 January 1991, and the ground attack began on 24 February." |
| Dayton ended the Yugoslav wars | ✅ Dayton, November 1995, signed in Paris in December, ended the Bosnian war and left Kosovo unaddressed | "Dayton ended the war it was convened for and ignored the one that followed in 1998-99." |

## Family 2 - Category confusion

| ⚠️ Error | ✅ Correction | ⚠️ Repair sentence |
|---|---|---|
| "The UN fought Iraq" | ✅ Member states cooperating with Kuwait were authorised to use all necessary means | "The Council authorised member states to act; command was national and coalition, not United Nations." |
| "The UN bombed Serbia" | ✅ NATO conducted the campaign without express Council authorisation | "NATO acted on its own decision; the Council authorised only the civil and security presences on 10 June 1999." |
| UNPROFOR failed because peacekeeping is useless | ✅ It had no supporting artillery or aircraft and safe areas were declared without troops to defend them | "The failure was of means and mandate, not of the peacekeeping concept as such." |
| The Warsaw Pact ended in 1989 | ✅ It signed the November 1990 declaration and was dissolved on 1 July 1991 | "The Pact outlived the regimes that composed it by up to two years." |
| Glasnost meant democracy | ✅ It was permission to speak, granted inside a one-party framework | "Glasnost legalised criticism; it did not initially question the Party's monopoly." |

## Family 3 - Attribution failure

| ⚠️ Error | ✅ Correction | ⚠️ Repair sentence |
|---|---|---|
| "About 90,000 Iraqis died" | ✅ "Some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies" | "On the estimates recorded in the account, Iraqi dead numbered around 90,000 against fewer than 400 allied dead." |
| "8,000 died at Srebrenica" stated flatly | ✅ The account gives "about 8000"; the tribunal characterised the killing as genocide in 2004 | "The account records about 8,000 Muslims killed at Srebrenica in July 1995, judicially characterised as genocide in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004." |
| "Half a million died in Rwanda because of American inaction" | ✅ The account records reluctance because no direct interests were involved and that "because of the delays, some half a million people were massacred" | "The account attributes delay to the absence of direct interests and records that some half a million people were massacred." |
| "The Serbs committed the atrocities" | ✅ "Atrocities were committed by all sides, but it seemed that the Bosnian Serbs were the most guilty" | "Atrocities were committed by all sides, with the Bosnian Serb forces the most guilty on the account's assessment." |
| Quoting Chomsky, Blum, Hutton or Guyatt as your own view | ✅ All are authors quoted by the account | "As Chomsky puts it, quoted in the account, the UN is dismissed when it fails to serve American unilateralism." |
| "700,000 Serbs were murdered in the Second World War" stated flatly | ✅ The account gives "some 700 000" and names the perpetrators as supporters of the Italian-installed fascist regime | "The account estimates that some 700,000 Serbs were murdered by supporters of the fascist regime installed by the Italians." |

## Family 4 - Causal over-reach

| ⚠️ Error | ✅ Correction | ⚠️ Repair sentence |
|---|---|---|
| "The West won the Cold War" | ✅ The account credits Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response together, and records the Soviet decisions | "Western pressure narrowed Soviet options; Soviet and eastern European decisions determined what followed." |
| "Economic failure caused the collapse" | ✅ It removed performance legitimacy; China survived comparable pressure by using troops in June 1989 | "Economic failure explains urgency and vulnerability; the outcome turned on a decision about force." |
| "Nationalism destroyed the USSR" | ✅ Nationalism strengthened because authority and ideology were already weakening | "National mobilisation was the form the crisis took, using federal institutions as ready-made vehicles." |
| "Ancient hatreds destroyed Yugoslavia" | ✅ War "was not inevitable"; leaders reactivated national feeling and Slovenia escaped | "Nationalism was produced by named political entrepreneurs operating on a particular distribution of minorities." |
| "The coup caused the Soviet dissolution" | ✅ The conditions pre-existed; the coup destroyed the last viable alternative | "The coup did not create the outcome; it eliminated the smaller voluntary union that might still have been available." |
| "Communism failed, proving socialism wrong" | ✅ A regime type collapsed in particular states; renamed communists won in Bulgaria in June 1990 and a former communist won Poland's presidency in December 1995 | "What collapsed was a regime type in particular states, which is a different proposition from a verdict on a doctrine." |

## Family 5 - Boundary violation

| ⚠️ Error | ✅ Correction | ⚠️ Repair sentence |
|---|---|---|
| Narrating the whole Cold War | ⚠️ The long rivalry is studied as the Cold War topic | "The rivalry whose terminal phase this answer examines is treated in full elsewhere." |
| Explaining United Nations structure and the veto at length | ⚠️ Design and institutional performance are studied under the United Nations | "The Council appears here only to fix the legal category of particular actions." |
| Writing India's 1991 reform package | ⚠️ That is studied under the economy | "India faced two simultaneous shocks in 1991; the reform package itself is a question in economic history." |
| Writing a globalisation essay | ⚠️ That is studied under the world economy | "Globalisation appears here only as the ideological component of the order claim." |
| Extending into the war on terrorism or post-2004 alliance politics | ⚠️ Both are studied under later topics and under current international relations | "This answer stops at the point where the order claim can be tested against the record of the 1990s." |
| Using undated post-2013 claims as history | ⚠️ The core account's horizon is 2013; exactly one separately dated current item is used in this session | "Any later claim must be separately dated and attributed, not folded into the narrative." |

```text
            1 Dec 2009                                    [deepening]
```

## Map 3 - The order claim, tested case by case

| ⚠️ Case | ✅ Capability | ✅ Rules | ✅ Legitimacy | ⚠️ Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gulf, 1990-91 | Over 600,000, more than thirty nations, four days of ground fighting | Resolution of 29 November 1990; deadline 15 January 1991 | Undercut by prior arming of Iraq, inaction over the Kurds in 1988 and East Timor in 1975 | ⚠️ The order claim's best case, and still conditional on interest alignment |
| Bosnia, 1992-95 | UNPROFOR had no supporting artillery or aircraft; NATO air power decisive only from August 1995 | Mandate without means; safe areas without troops; command shifts to the alliance | Two million refugees; the account's phrase "no real winners" | ⚠️ Mandate without capacity; the order's clearest failure of design |
| Kosovo, 1998-99 | Eleven weeks of air attack forced acceptance of terms | No express Council authorisation for force; Council resolution of 10 June 1999 for the presences | Hundreds of thousands expelled during the campaign; 20,000 peacekeepers still present at the end of 2003 | ⚠️ Capability without authority; effective and contested |
| Rwanda, 1994 | Not deployed | Not engaged | ✅ Reluctance because no direct interests were involved; "some half a million people were massacred" | ⚠️ The consistency test the order fails outright |
| European enlargement, 1995-2004 | Economic and institutional rather than military | Accession conditionality, applied earlier to Greece, Portugal and Spain | Ratification refused by voters in Denmark, Ireland, the Netherlands and France at various points; and, separately, membership itself declined by the Swiss and Norwegian electorates | ⚠️ The order's most durable achievement, and still contested by its own electorates |

## Map 4 - Five transitions compared

| ⚠️ Case | ✅ Route | ✅ Violence | ✅ First free election outcome | ⚠️ What it proves |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Poland | Negotiated: April 1989 agreement, June elections | None recorded in 1988-89 | Solidarity sweeps the contestable seats; Jaruzelski president; Mazowiecki prime minister | ⚠️ Organised counterpart plus a leadership that had tried force once |
| Hungary | Reform-communist concession | None recorded | March 1990: renamed communists crushed; Democratic Forum wins | ⚠️ Internal party reform reaches the same wall as in the USSR |
| East Germany | Exit pressure plus elite split | None; Honecker's order refused | March 1990: East German CDU wins overwhelmingly | ⚠️ An adjacent co-national state makes exit a political weapon |
| Romania | Violent overthrow | Timisoara and Bucharest: many killed; the Ceausescus shot | May 1990: Iliescu and the National Salvation Front win | ⚠️ Thorough repression determines the replacement, not the survival |
| Bulgaria | Managed continuity | None recorded | June 1990: renamed Bulgarian Socialist Party wins | ⚠️ A ruling party can survive its own leader's removal |
