## Map 7 - Answer spines

```text
  SPINE A - "Why did communism collapse?" (10/15)
  Thesis: dismantled, not defeated
    -> performance legitimacy lost (the "absurd situation")
    -> reform legalised criticism faster than it delivered
    -> organised society converted the opening (Aug 1988)
    -> the centre declined to coerce (the decisive omission)
  Qualification: China, June 1989
  Verdict: the last step was a decision, not a law of history

  SPINE B - "Was there a new world order?" (15/20)
  Thesis: unipolar in capability, plural in legitimacy
    -> capability: the Gulf coalition
    -> rules: the 29 Nov 1990 authorisation against Panama,
       the Court judgment, the lone votes
    -> legitimacy: the account's own question; Guyatt
    -> capacity: Bosnia's safe areas; Kosovo's authorisation gap
  Qualification: plurality is not impotence (130-1; 140-1)
  Verdict: an order in the first sense only

  SPINE C - "Yugoslavia" (15/20)
  Thesis: nationalism produced by politics, on a structure
    -> structure: 90% inflation 1986; 13% unemployment;
       regional inequality; a rotating presidency
    -> agents: Milosevic from 1988; Tudjman; the unevidenced
       Kosovo claim; refused minority guarantees
    -> arithmetic: 600,000 in Croatia; 1.3 million in Bosnia;
       44/33/17
    -> external: recognition without guarantees, twice
  Control case: Slovenia
  Verdict: war was not inevitable

  SPINE D - "Europe" (15/20)
  Thesis: a repeatedly renegotiated bargain, not a march
    -> 1951-57: security by economic means
    -> 1973-86: widening against deepening; the market bargain
    -> 1991: deepening, and containing a reunified Germany
    -> 1995-2004: the institutional settlement of the Cold
       War's end
  Qualification: six national refusals; a Commission resigning
  in 1999; a summit collapsing in 2003
  Verdict: absorption completed without resolving sovereignty
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ These are compressed revision notes, not a second teaching pass. ⚠️ Every subtopic is represented, with priority given to definitions, chronology, evidence, causal logic, comparisons, debates, traps, answer routes and rapid-recall facts.

## 1. Four endings inside one phrase

- ✅ Rivalry: terminated November 1990, when NATO and Warsaw Pact countries signed a treaty agreeing they were "no longer adversaries" and that weapons would be used only in self-defence.
- ✅ Bloc: swept away between August 1988 and December 1991.
- ✅ State: fifteen republics; dissolution December 1991; Gorbachev resigned 25 December 1991; communist rule in Russia over "after 74 years".
- ⚠️ Order: announced on 11 September 1990, not achieved; assessing it is the topic's central analytical task.
- ⚠️ Best organising word: **dismantling** - taken apart by people inside it, partly by intention and partly by consequence.
- ⚠️ Boundaries: Topic 15 for the long Cold War, Topic 16 for United Nations design, Topic 17 for China, Topic 20 for the world economy, international relations and the economy for India and for the present.

## 2. Late-Soviet structural pressures

- ✅ Gorbachev's 1988 diagnosis: over-centralised, no room for local initiative; a command economy almost wholly state-owned and controlled; weighted to defence and heavy industry; consumer goods in short supply.
- ✅ His own phrase: "an absurd situation" that the world's biggest producer of steel, fuel and energy suffered shortages through waste and inefficiency.
- ✅ Western contact in the 1980s supplied comparison, trend **and** attribution to the leaders and the system.
- ✅ Misha Glenny, quoted: the communist record on health, education, housing and other social services "has been atrocious".
- ✅ Brezhnev advocated disarmament while expanding forces, especially the navy and the SS-20s; aid stepped up to Cuba and offered to Angola, Mozambique and Ethiopia.
- ✅ Afghanistan: over 100,000 troops; guerrillas impossible to subdue; "a drain on their resources and a blow to their prestige".
- ✅ Andropov (Nov 1982-Feb 1984) had already begun modernisation, anti-corruption and decentralisation; Volkogonov on Chernenko: his rise symbolised "the deepening of the crisis in society, the total lack of positive ideas in the party, and the inevitability of the convulsions to come".
- ⚠️ Analytical conversion: stagnation removed **performance legitimacy**, the successor to revolutionary legitimacy.

## 3. Legitimacy, nationalities and federal design

- ✅ Fifteen union republics, each with its own parliament; the Russian republic one of them, its parliament in Moscow alongside the federal bodies.
- ✅ Kept under tight control since Stalin's time; glasnost and perestroika "encouraged them to hope for more powers".
- ✅ Nagorno-Karabakh, February 1988: a Christian autonomous republic inside Muslim Azerbaijan asked to join Armenia; refused for fear of the conservatives; fighting followed; "Moscow had clearly lost control".
- ✅ Baltic republics taken over against their will in 1940; movements denounced as "national excesses"; Lithuania declared independence March 1990; Latvia and Estonia followed more gradually; Moscow refused recognition.
- ✅ The Baltic declarations were "encouraged by what was happening in the satellite states of eastern Europe".
- ✅ Yeltsin elected president of the Russian republic's parliament, May 1990.
- ⚠️ Mechanism: federal design made exit **available**; ideological and performance failure made it **attractive**; non-coercion made it **achievable**.
- ⚠️ Trap: nationalism was a consequence of weakening authority as much as a cause.

## 4. Gorbachev I - glasnost and perestroika

- ✅ March 1985, aged 54; determined to transform and revitalise after "the sterile years following Khrushchev's fall".
- ✅ He "did not want to end communism"; he wanted humane, democratic socialism inside the Marxist-Leninist one-party framework.
- ✅ Glasnost measures: dissidents released; Sakharovs return from Gorky December 1986; Bukharin declared innocent; *Pravda* criticises Brezhnev's treatment of dissidents; law of January 1988 against psychiatric confinement; May 1986 cultural unions elect new leaderships; banned anti-Stalin films and novels released; Mandelstam, who died in a labour camp in 1938, prepared for publication; April 1986 Chernobyl "killing hundreds of people" discussed openly; 1988 Conference and May 1989 Congress televised.
- ✅ Perestroika results: growth unchanged 1988-89; national income fell 1990 and by about 15 per cent in 1991; an attributed comparison with the American crisis of the early 1930s.
- ⚠️ The formula: speed of permission exceeds speed of delivery.
- ✅ The transferable sentence: "The most dangerous time for any repressive regime is when it begins to try and reform itself by making concessions."

## 5. Gorbachev II - new thinking abroad

- ✅ 1987 treaty: 436 American and 1,575 Soviet warheads; strict verification; all Russian missiles out of East Germany and Czechoslovakia; at most 4 per cent of stocks; first destruction of weapons.
- ✅ Afghan settlement signed at Geneva on **14 April 1988**, conditional on the end of American aid to the resistance; withdrawal begun **15 May 1988**, completed 15 February 1989; the account's 1 May 1988 is the agreed timetable, not the start. ✅ Reagan in Moscow June 1988.
- ✅ China: 1984 grievances were Afghanistan, Kampuchea and the frontier build-up; 1985 agreements; formal reconciliation May 1989 in Beijing.
- ✅ The decisive omission: critics grew bolder "as they realized that Gorbachev would not send Soviet troops in to fire on them"; no satellite government except Romania used sufficient force; the arithmetic made region-wide coercion impractical after Afghanistan.
- ⚠️ Standard designation: the "Sinatra Doctrine", used by the Soviet spokesman Gennadi Gerasimov in October 1989.
- ✅ Moscow actively engineered leadership change in Czechoslovakia and was "probably involved" elsewhere - preserve the hedge - hoping progressive leaders would save communism.
- ✅ The inversion: satellite leaderships faced a Moscow more progressive than themselves.
- ⚠️ Malta 2-3 December 1989; formal termination November 1990; credit distributed between Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response.

## 6. Why the reforms failed at home

- ✅ Radicals (Yeltsin) against conservatives (Ligachev), who controlled the apparatus; May 1989 Supreme Soviet packed with conservatives; Moscow protests.
- ✅ Law on State Enterprises: wages tied to rouble-valued output → expensive goods favoured, cheap goods cut → higher wages → money printed → inflation and deficit → soap, washing powder, razor blades, cups and saucers, TV sets and food vanish → longer queues.
- ✅ July 1989: Siberian miners without soap; "What kind of a regime is it, if we can't even get washed?"; half a million out across Siberia, Kazakhstan and the Donbass; first major strike since 1917; 42 demands escalating to independent unions and an end to Party privilege under Polish influence.
- ✅ Early 1990: about a quarter of the population calculated to be below the poverty line; worst affected large families, the unemployed and pensioners.
- ✅ Gorbachev-Yeltsin: voluntary union against feared disintegration; Party privilege against Party transformation; rapid marketisation against caution about unemployment and prices.
- ⚠️ Transferable principle: a system stuck between plan and market may have neither coordinating mechanism working.

## 7. 1989 I - Poland and Hungary

- ✅ The process "began in Poland in August 1988" with Solidarity's strikes.
- ✅ 1981: martial law, Solidarity banned, thousands arrested; the army obeyed "because everybody was still afraid of Russian military intervention"; lifted by July 1983.
- ✅ 1988: subsidy cuts, food prices up, protest strikes; Jaruzelski "decided not to risk using force" - no backing from Moscow and he needed opposition support on the economy.
- ✅ April 1989 agreement: Solidarity legalised; two chambers; 65 per cent of lower-house seats reserved; a freely elected senate; the two houses jointly electing a president who chooses a prime minister.
- ✅ June 1989: 92 of 100 senate seats; 160 of 161 contestable lower-house seats.
- ✅ August 1989: Jaruzelski president on the guaranteed seats; Mazowiecki the first non-communist leader in the bloc; a mixed government.
- ✅ December 1990: guaranteed seats removed; Walesa president; "the peaceful revolution in Poland was complete".
- ✅ Hungary: Kadar's 1985 admission; May 1988 he and eight supporters voted off the Politburo; opposition parties the Alliance of Free Democrats and the Democratic Forum; March 1990 free elections crush the renamed Hungarian Socialist Party; Antall prime minister; the leadership acted "following the example of the Poles".
- ⚠️ Two preconditions for negotiated exit: an organised counterpart, and a divided or reform-minded ruling party.

## 8. 1989 II - the other four routes

- ✅ East Germany: June 1989 Bonn bargain; August-September escapes via Hungary's opened Austrian frontier; New Forum around the Protestant Church; October demonstrations; Honecker wanted to fire, colleagues refused, he was dropped, Krenz conceded; Wall breached 9 November 1989.
- ✅ Czechoslovakia: Husak resigned 1987; Jakes no reformer; 17 November 1989 Prague demonstration and police brutality; Charter 77 under Havel; Dubcek's first public speech since 1968; national strike; Havel president 29 December 1989.
- ✅ Romania: Ceausescu since 1965; the Securitate "responsible for many deaths"; Timisoara 17 December 1989, many killed; the Bucharest rally and "murderers of Timisoara"; the army fired, then refused; the Ceausescus tried and shot 25 December 1989; "no equivalent of the Polish Solidarity and Czech Charter 77"; the National Salvation Front full of former communists; Iliescu president May 1990.
- ✅ Bulgaria: Zhivkov since 1954; the Politburo removed him on **10 November 1989**, the day after the Berlin Wall was breached, the account's wording giving December 1989; the renamed Bulgarian Socialist Party won June 1990 over the Union of Democratic Forces, "probably" through propaganda about capitalist disaster.
- ✅ Albania: communist since 1945 by indigenous resistance, as in Yugoslavia; Hoxha then Alia; poorest in Europe; Adriatic escapes in the winter of 1991; Berisha president 1992.
- ✅ After communism: uncompetitive industry with no bloc markets; stocked shops at unaffordable prices; "the standard of living was even lower than under the final years of communism"; "very little help was forthcoming from the west"; East Germany subsidised with resentment both ways; Poland recovering by 1994 but electing a former communist in December 1995; Czechoslovakia separating peacefully in 1992; Romania, Bulgaria and Albania slowest.

## 9. German reunification

- ✅ 9 November 1989: Wall breached - enforced separation ends, nothing legal settled.
- ⚠️ Three open questions: borders (Oder-Neisse and Polish suspicion), sovereignty (four-power rights), alliance status and foreign forces.
- ✅ March 1990: East German CDU wins overwhelmingly; de Maiziere wanted gradual union; the population wanted immediate union.
- ✅ Great-power consent: USSR and USA agreed; all Russian troops out of East Germany by 1994; France and Britain less happy and went along.
- ⚠️ Two Plus Four - the Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany - signed at Moscow 12 September 1990; in force 15 March 1991.
- ✅ "Germany was formally reunited at midnight on 3 October 1990."
- ✅ December 1990 all-German elections: CDU/CSU with FDP win; the renamed communists take 17 of 662 seats; Kohl the first Chancellor of all Germany since the Second World War.
- ✅ Consequences: Polish suspicion; at least 16,000 refugees a month entering by October 1992; neo-Nazi violence; east-west resentment over transfers, taxes, interest rates and jobs.
- ⚠️ 1871 and 1990 share a word and nothing else.
- 📰 The session's single dated current item sits here: the United States Department of State's National Museum of American Diplomacy states that it received the Berlin Wall "Signature Segment" in October 2015 and will open to the public in October 2026.

## 10. The institutional termination, 1990-91

- ✅ November 1990 declaration: "no longer adversaries"; weapons only in self-defence; "The Cold War was over."
- ⚠️ The instruments: the conventional forces treaty and the Joint Declaration of Twenty-Two States, 19 November 1990; the Charter of Paris for a New Europe, 21 November 1990.
- ⚠️ Comecon dissolved 28 June 1991; the Warsaw Treaty Organisation 1 July 1991.
- ✅ Six new problems recorded, four owned here: nationalism-driven conflicts left to find their own solutions "bloody or otherwise" - Czechoslovakia peacefully, Azerbaijan-Armenia by war, Georgia, and "most tragic of all" Yugoslavia, broken into Serbia with Montenegro, Bosnia-Herzegovina, Croatia, Slovenia and Macedonia; weapons supervision as the superpowers became less willing to police; transition economics with the recorded fear of a right-wing backlash "especially in Russia itself"; and western allied disunity, including the American refusal of troops for the Bosnian peacekeeping force.
- ✅ "The overriding fact now was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower."
- ⚠️ Three definitions, three defensible dates: alliance rivalry November 1990; bipolar structure December 1991; ideological contest unevenly and not everywhere.

## 11. Soviet dissolution

- ⚠️ Four conditions: units able to act as states; a centre unable or unwilling to prevent exit; a rival authority to rally to; and a trigger.
- ✅ Dual power from May 1990: two authorities in one city with different sources of legitimacy.
- ⚠️ Coup 19-21 August 1991 by a self-declared State Committee on the State of Emergency; Gorbachev detained; resistance from the Russian republic; failure within three days.
- ✅ It destroyed much of the party's remaining legitimacy and accelerated republican exits: coercive restoration destroyed the authority it sought to save.
- ⚠️ 8 December 1991: Russian, Ukrainian and Belarusian leaders agree the union has ceased to exist and create a commonwealth; 21 December: eight more join at Alma-Ata.
- ✅ Gorbachev resigned 25 December 1991; the union was wound up the following day.
- ⚠️ The Baltic claim was restoration of statehood, not secession; the Baltic states did not join the commonwealth.
- ⚠️ What did not end: the economic crisis, the territorial conflicts, the custody of nuclear weapons, and communist party-states elsewhere.
- ⚠️ Transferable principle: a failed restoration is worse for an old order than no attempt at all.

## 12. The aftermath in Russia

- ✅ Shock therapy from January 1992: price controls off about 90 per cent of goods; subsidies ended; prices on average thirty times higher by year-end; goods present but unaffordable; wages lagging; over a million jobs lost; thousands in tents; food parcels from abroad.
- ✅ Vouchers worth 10,000 roubles, "about £35"; workers could not afford shares; managers accumulated vouchers; by end-1995 most former state industry in a small group of hands; profits from reintroduced subsidies rather than the market; funds moved abroad; total investment down by two-thirds.
- ✅ Attributed judgements: the 1993 "savage era" report; Daniel Beer on plunder by robber barons; a 1994 report estimating criminal mafia control of 70-80 per cent of business and banking; Chubarov's "deformed capitalism".
- ✅ Institutions: a Soviet-era constitution unclear on the division of powers; the Communist Party relegalised November 1992; Yeltsin without a supporting party; April 1993 referendum 53 per cent.
- ✅ December 1995 Duma: Yeltsin's supporters 65 of 450; the Communist Party 157, with allies 186.
- ✅ 1996 presidential: advisers urged cancellation and force; Yeltsin allowed the vote; first round 35 to 32 per cent with **Gorbachev barely 1 per cent**; second round 54 per cent; the account attributes the result to dislike of the alternative and Zyuganov's open admiration for Stalin.
- ✅ Later record: oil from about £28 a barrel in 2000 to over £60 by end-2006; largest gas producer and second largest oil exporter; growth over 6 per cent a year; software exports $1.5 billion in 2006; National Priority Projects from 2005. Against it: a reserve fund at the cost of investment; a $90 billion cushion in 2008-9; higher-education funding at 12 per cent of the 1989 level and not restored by 2008; inferior free health care; pensioners worst affected; nostalgia for post-Stalinist Soviet times.
- ✅ Attributed named cases: Politkovskaya shot October 2006; Litvinenko killed in London November 2006 by polonium 210, extradition refused, "a clear inference" but no direct governmental attribution by the UK; Anderson on the liberal intelligentsia's self-compromise.
- ⚠️ Master sentence: the collapse of communism did not automatically create a healthy democracy or a fair market economy; the later period restored state authority more effectively than liberal credibility.

## 13. Explaining 1989-91

- ⚠️ Three questions: vulnerability (structure), timing (reform, mobilisation, diplomacy), character (the non-coercion decision, scale arithmetic, elite splits).
- ⚠️ Six families with their limits: stagnation (not outcome); burdens (not timing or peacefulness); agency (inside constraints); society (varied by country); nationality (partly an effect); Western pressure (cannot carry triumphalism).
- ⚠️ Four readings and their errors: triumphalist, structuralist, agency-centred, social-historical; plus the postcolonial critique, which belongs to the order rather than to causation.
- ✅ Contingency test one: China, June 1989 - troops used, at least a thousand students killed and many later executed, worldwide condemnation, and the system survived.
- ✅ Contingency test two: Romania - force used, many killed, and the regime still fell once the army refused to continue.
- ⚠️ Balanced verdict: structural weakness made reform urgent; reform released opposition; societies and republics supplied alternatives; diplomacy and the refusal to use mass force made reversal increasingly difficult.

## 14. The unipolar moment

- ⚠️ The phrase was used by President George H. W. Bush before Congress on 11 September 1990, during the Kuwait crisis, as an objective.
- ✅ The claimed content: peace, cooperation, international justice, liberty, human rights, the eradication of poverty, the spread of education, health and democracy.
- ✅ Rothkopf, 1997, quoted: the United States is "the most just, the most tolerant and the best model for the future".
- ✅ Guyatt, 2000, quoted: resentment "grounded not in fundamentalism or insanity but in a real perception of the imbalance of power".
- ✅ Recorded instances: Panama December 1989 with at least 2,000 civilians killed and an Organization of American States regret resolution 20 to 1; suppression of left-wing movements in Mexico, Colombia, Ecuador and Peru in the 1990s; intervention in Nicaraguan elections in 1989 and 2001; Rwanda 1994 with some half a million massacred; rejection of the International Court judgment on the mining of Nicaraguan harbours and refusal of compensation; lone votes 130-1 in 1985 and 140-1 in 1987 and the 1996 food-summit refusal; and, before September 2001, rejection of the 1972 missile treaty and withdrawal from the 1997 climate protocols.
- ✅ Quoted judgements to attribute: Blum on the American Empire; Chomsky on the UN being dismissed; Byers on exceptional rules; Hutton on the financial system.
- ⚠️ Master distinction: unipolarity is a distribution of capability; order additionally requires rules, legitimacy, institutional consent and the ability to manage different kinds of conflict.
- ⚠️ Two named contemporary theses: Fukuyama 1989 and Huntington 1993 - claims, not descriptions.

## 15. The Gulf War

- ✅ Iraq invaded Kuwait in August 1990; motives probably the wealth of Kuwait, a historical claim refuted by Kuwait's separate existence as a British protectorate since 1899 against Iraq's post-1918 creation, and an expectation of no outside action grounded in prior American support.
- ⚠️ Authorisation 29 November 1990: member states cooperating with Kuwait to use all necessary means after 15 January 1991.
- ✅ Sanctions cut Iraq's oil exports; Saudi Arabia, Syria and Egypt supported the action; over 600,000 assembled with more than thirty contributing nations.
- ✅ Desert Storm in two phases; ground attack from 24 February; Iraq driven out within four days; "some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies"; Bush called a ceasefire fearing loss of Arab support.
- ✅ Aftermath: Kurdish and Shia risings crushed; Russia, Syria and Turkey feared Kurdish contagion and the USA feared Iranian gains; no-fly zones with UN backing; Saddam Hussein remained in power.
- ✅ The account's verdict: the primary concern was self-interest, not international justice; East Timor in 1975 supplies the comparison; the United Nations chapter records the same criticism and adds that intervention succeeded most where its forces actively supported one side.
- ⚠️ The order verdict: alignment made enforcement possible; nothing guaranteed the alignment would recur.

## 16. Yugoslavia

- ✅ Formed after the First World War; six republics and two provinces; under Tito nationalist feelings "kept strictly under control"; a rotating collective presidency after 1980.
- ✅ Attributed wartime figure: "some 700 000 Serbs" murdered by supporters of the Italian-installed fascist regime - evidence that worse memories had been neutralised.
- ✅ Mid-1980s: inflation 90 per cent in 1986; over a million unemployed at 13 per cent; Slovenia prosperous, parts of Serbia poverty-stricken.
- ✅ Milosevic from 1988 "bears much of the responsibility", stirred Serbian nationalism using an unevidenced Kosovo terror claim, won 1990 as a nationalist, wanted a united federation dominated by Serbia.
- ✅ Tudjman, a former communist jailed under Tito, stirred Croatian nationalism and refused guarantees to Croatia's Serbs.
- ✅ Arithmetic: about 600,000 Serbs in Croatia, about 15 per cent; about 1.3 million in Bosnia, roughly a third; Bosnia 44 per cent Muslim, 33 per cent Serb, 17 per cent Croat.
- ✅ June 1991 Slovene and Croat declarations; Slovenia escaped war largely for lack of Serbs and through Community mediation; Serbian forces took about a third of Croatia by end-August 1991; UNPROFOR, 13,000 troops, February 1992.
- ✅ Bosnia declared independence March 1992 under Izetbegovic; recognition without minority guarantees was "the same mistake as it had done with Croatia".
- ✅ Ethnic cleansing, camps, the siege of Sarajevo, two million refugees; UNPROFOR without artillery or aircraft; safe areas at Srebrenica, Zepa and Gorazde without troops to defend them.
- ✅ 1995: May shelling; peacekeepers taken hostage; Srebrenica and Zepa captured in July with about 8,000 Muslims killed - characterised as genocide by the tribunal in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004; the Markale market shelling of 28 August, for which the account records 27 killed and the standard verified toll is 43 killed and 75 wounded - both stated, neither merged; massive NATO bombing with the alliance now running the operation; Karadzic and Mladic indicted; Milosevic seeking sanctions relief; Croat-Muslim recapture of western Slavonia and the Krajina.
- ✅ Dayton November 1995, signed in Paris in December: one state, two entities, unified Sarajevo, a corridor to Gorazde, indicted war criminals banned from public life, over two million refugees with the right to return, 60,000 NATO troops, sanctions relief understood; "no real winners".
- ⚠️ Control cases: Slovenia within Yugoslavia, and Czechoslovakia's peaceful separation of 1992.

## 17. Kosovo 1999

- ✅ Albanian majority resented hardline policies and the loss of provincial autonomy; non-violent protest under Rugova from 1989; Kosovo "largely ignored" at the 1995 negotiations.
- ✅ Peaceful protest made no impression; the Kosovo Liberation Army formed; civil war by 1998; a full-scale Serb offensive with atrocities in spring 1999, widely reported abroad.
- ✅ NATO's "controversial bombing attacks against Serbia"; ⚠️ Operation Allied Force, 24 March to 10 June 1999, without express Council authorisation for force.
- ✅ Immediate effect: Milosevic "only made him more determined"; hundreds of thousands of Albanians expelled into Albania, Macedonia and Montenegro.
- ✅ June 1999: agreement worked out by Russia and Finland; Serb troops withdrew and many Serb civilians left; most Albanian refugees returned; a UN and NATO force of over 40,000; the UN mission supervised the administration; ⚠️ the Council resolution of 10 June 1999 framed the presences.
- ✅ End of 2003: still 20,000 peacekeepers amid complaints of poverty, unemployment and corruption.
- ✅ Milosevic indicted May 1999; lost September 2000; the result annulled; demonstrations and the storming of parliament; conceded; arrested 2001; conducted his own defence from July 2001; died March 2006 with no verdict.
- ✅ Serbia after him: empty treasury, sanctions-ruined economy, inflation, fuel crisis; the extreme nationalist Serbian Radicals largest at the end of 2003, disappointing the USA and the EU; Karadzic arrested July 2008 after thirteen years.
- ⚠️ Three questions to separate: objective achieved; immediate cost; consistently applied rule.

## 18. European integration

- ✅ Coal and Steel Community 1951, Schuman's project; Treaty of Rome 1957 with six founders; merger of the three communities in 1967.
- ✅ Britain's free-trade association of 1960 had "no question of common economic policies and no supranational authority"; Britain, Ireland and Denmark joined on 1 January 1973.
- ✅ Lome 1975: duty-free access for over 40 African and Caribbean states plus aid; critics said too little.
- ✅ First direct elections June 1979, 410 members; turnout under a third in Britain against over 80 per cent in Italy and Belgium; exchange-rate mechanism 1979; Britain joined it in October 1990 at a high rate.
- ✅ Greece 1981; Portugal and Spain 1986 - twelve members, over 320 million people; earlier exclusion because their systems were undemocratic.
- ✅ 1986 changes: free internal market by 1992, majority voting, more parliamentary powers - and the sovereignty row, with Thatcher's "nightmare" formulation.
- ✅ Agricultural policy: three-quarters of the budget by 1980; quotas from 1984; by 1987 a wine lake and a 1.5-million-tonne butter mountain, five years of milk powder, £1 million a day in storage; a £3 billion deficit and £10 billion of debts in 1987; damage to developing economies on the account's own judgement.
- ✅ Maastricht December 1991: parliamentary powers, monetary union towards a common currency, a common foreign and security policy, a timetable; the Social Chapter removed on British objection; ratified by October 1993; supported by France, the Netherlands and Belgium as the way to contain reunified German power.
- ✅ Ratification difficulties: Danish rejection then narrow approval May 1993; a tiny French majority; the narrowest British parliamentary majority. ⚠️ Separately, the Swiss and Norwegian electorates declined membership - a different act from refusing ratification.
- ✅ 1995 Sweden, Finland, Austria; Amsterdam 1997, in force 1 May 1999; the whole Commission of 20 resigned March 1999; Nice December 2000 with qualified majority voting, reweighting and a 60,000-strong rapid reaction force under NATO's stated primacy; Irish rejection June 2001; the Iraq split of 2003 and the "old Europe" remark; the deficit-rule rift; the December 2003 summit collapse.
- ✅ 1 May 2004: ten new members; the euro had entered 12 states on 1 January 2002; the 2004 Constitutional Treaty required 15 states with 65 per cent of 455 million and was rejected by Dutch and French voters in 2005; Lisbon signed by 27 in December 2007, rejected by Ireland June 2008, approved October 2009, in force 1 December 2009.
- ✅ After September 2008 the American collapse spread; Spain and Ireland worst hit; Anderson's figures for Ireland; Greece's manipulated entry figures of 2001; Merkel in February 2012 on Europe's gravest test for decades.
- ⚠️ Master formulation: a repeatedly renegotiated bargain among peace, prosperity, sovereignty and geopolitical anchoring.

## 19. NATO enlargement

- ⚠️ Asymmetry: the Warsaw Pact dissolved 1 July 1991; NATO continued and enlarged.
- ✅ The original difference: eastern European states "were forced into it by the USSR" while Community members "joined voluntarily".
- ⚠️ Rounds owned here: 1999 - Czech Republic, Hungary, Poland; 2004 - Bulgaria, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Romania, Slovakia, Slovenia; the 2004 round coincides with the Union's enlargement of 1 May 2004.
- ✅ Functional shift evidenced in this session: in Bosnia "the UN position was weakened because NATO was now running the operation" and 60,000 alliance troops policed Dayton; in Kosovo the alliance conducted the campaign and then formed part of a force of over 40,000.
- ✅ Transatlantic friction: the Nice rapid reaction force with NATO stated as the basis of European defence displeased the French president; in October 2003 the United States called European defence plans "one of the greatest dangers to the transatlantic relationship".
- ⚠️ Four arguments each way; the tension with the November 1990 "no longer adversaries" declaration is the strongest objection.
- ⚠️ Boundary: everything after 2004 is studied under international relations.

## 20. Globalisation, the Global South and India

- ✅ "The Americans kept firm control over the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund, so that states applying for loans had to make sure that their internal policies were acceptable to the USA"; Washington opposed the Bank president's 1995 debt-relief priorities in favour of austerity; Hutton quoted on the financial system extending American power.
- ✅ Europe's own record: Lome access judged too little; the agricultural policy "causes a great deal of damage to the economies of the developing world"; surpluses sold cheaply to the USSR, India, Pakistan and Bangladesh, given free to the poor, fed to animals and in part burnt.
- ⚠️ "Global South" is an analytical category, not a uniform bloc; the end of bipolarity widened options without removing hierarchy.
- ✅ Evidence of non-Western majorities: 130 to 1 in 1985 and 140 to 1 in 1987; the accurate claim concerns decisiveness, not voice.
- ✅ Even inside the West unipolarity was not treated as settled: many hoped the Union would counterbalance a USA that in 2004 seemed able to dominate the world.
- ⚠️ India 1991: two simultaneous shocks - the loss of the Soviet strategic setting and a severe balance-of-payments crisis; the world-history point is recalibration; the reform package is studied under the economy and strategic autonomy under international relations.
- ⚠️ Three post-1991 trajectories to compare: institutional anchoring for central and eastern Europe; shock therapy in Russia; diversification with retained autonomy for India.

## 21. Continuity, rupture and the answer architecture

- ⚠️ Rupture: the bipolar alliance structure, the Soviet state, the European map, the legitimacy of one-party socialist rule in Europe, and the suppression of some conflicts.
- ⚠️ Continuity: interest-driven intervention, institutional asymmetry, nationalism as a mobilising resource, inequality of capacity, and contested sovereignty.
- ⚠️ Verdict: rupture in the distribution of power and in the map, inside continuity in the logic by which power was used.
- ⚠️ Five interpretations with evidence and limits; deploy three in a 20-mark answer, never five.
- ⚠️ Technique against teleology: verdicts about mechanisms, not directions.
- ⚠️ Counter-cases: China June 1989; Bulgaria June 1990; Russia 1992-95; the Serbian Radicals in 2003.
- ⚠️ Directive map, mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20, seven thesis templates, five verdict scaffolds, fifteen factual-risk controls - control 10, the date list, is the single most examinable item in the topic.

## Discrepancies found in the evidence base and how they were handled

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The naming of the November 1990 instrument.** The account refers to "a treaty" signed by the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact agreeing that they were "no longer adversaries", without naming it | ⚠️ Lesson 10 teaches the account's sentence verbatim as the anchor and then names the three Paris instruments of 19-21 November 1990 separately, marking them as material-fact-pass additions rather than as the account's own wording. ⚠️ No claim is made that the account names them |
| **The date of the Soviet Union's formal end.** The account gives the end of December 1991 and Gorbachev's resignation without distinguishing the republican agreements, the resignation and the legislature's formal act | ⚠️ Lesson 11 teaches the three stages separately - 8 and 21 December 1991, 25 December and the following day - with the first two and the last drawn from the declared material-fact pass, and states in the lesson that the account's own summary is the end-of-December formulation |
| **The characterisation of the Srebrenica killings.** The account calls it "perhaps the ultimate act of barbarism" and gives "about 8000 Muslims", without a legal characterisation | ⚠️ Lesson 16 reproduces the account's wording and figure exactly, and separately records the judicial characterisation as genocide in the tribunal's 2004 Krstic appeal judgement, drawn from the declared material-fact pass. ❌ Neither is merged into the other |
| **The authorisation status of the Kosovo campaign.** The account calls the bombing "controversial" without stating the legal basis | ⚠️ Lesson 17 teaches the account's word and then states, from the declared material-fact pass, that there was no express Security Council authorisation for the use of force and that the Council authorised the subsequent presences on 10 June 1999. ⚠️ The distinction between contested force and authorised aftermath is presented as this session's analysis, not as the account's |
| **European Community membership figures.** The 1986 accession passage gives a Community population of "over 320 million" while the 2004 Constitutional Treaty passage gives a Union population of 455 million | ⚠️ Not a contradiction: the two figures describe twelve members in 1986 and twenty-five in 2004. ⚠️ Lesson 18 states both with their membership counts attached so that no reader can mistake one for a revision of the other |
| **The Maastricht date.** The section heading dates the Maastricht agreement to 1991 while the canonical Basic owner's stage table gives "Maastricht, 1992-93" and its must-know facts give signature in 1992 and entry into force in 1993 | ⚠️ Both are correct about different acts: the summit and the agreement were of December 1991, the treaty was signed in February 1992 and ratification was completed by October 1993. ⚠️ Lesson 18 and register note 18 teach the December 1991 summit and the completion of ratification by October 1993, which are the two facts the account states directly, and the owner's signature year is preserved in the date-control list of Lesson 21 |
| **The Gulf casualty estimates.** The account gives "some estimates put Iraqi dead at 90 000 compared with less than 400 for the allies" | ⚠️ Taught with the qualifier intact in Lesson 15, in register note 15 and in remediation family 3. ❌ No alternative figure is introduced and no total is computed |
| **The Romanian December figures.** The account gives "many people were killed" at Timisoara and "many were killed and wounded" in Bucharest, with no numbers | ⚠️ Lesson 8 reproduces the wording and states expressly that no numerical substitute is made. ⚠️ The dispute over Romanian December casualties is recorded here as an open uncertainty rather than resolved |
| **Albania's leadership dates.** The extraction gives Hoxha as leader "since 1946 until his death in 1985" in one clause while describing Albania as communist since 1945 | ⚠️ Lesson 8 teaches the structure - communism installed in 1945 by an indigenous resistance movement, Hoxha as a Stalin admirer, Alia as successor - and does not assert the precise start year of Hoxha's leadership, which the extraction renders inconsistently |
| **The date of Zhivkov's removal.** The account states that the Bulgarian Politburo "voted to remove him (December 1989)" | ✅ **Corrected in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** Zhivkov was removed on **10 November 1989**, the day after the Berlin Wall was breached. ⚠️ The corrected date is now carried in Lesson 8's sorting visual, in its Bulgaria section with an explicit correction note, in its revision notes, in cumulative check B1, in Map 1 and in register note 8, and the account's December wording is disclosed at each teaching point rather than silently replaced |
| **The start of the Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan.** The account states that it was agreed that the Russians "would begin withdrawing their troops from Afghanistan on 1 May 1988" | ✅ **Corrected in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** That sentence reports the **agreed timetable**. The settlement was signed at **Geneva on 14 April 1988**, and the withdrawal began on **15 May 1988**, finishing on 15 February 1989. ⚠️ Lessons 2 and 5, their revision notes, the Lesson 5 progress line, Map 1 and register note 5 now carry the Geneva signature and the 15 May start, with the account's 1 May explicitly labelled as the timetable. ❌ No sentence in this session now asserts that the withdrawal began on 1 May |
| **The toll of the Markale market shelling of 28 August 1995.** The account records that "27 people were killed by a single mortar shell on 28 August" | ✅ **Handled in the repair pass of 29 September 2026 by disclosure rather than substitution.** The standard verified toll for that shelling is **43 killed and 75 wounded**. ⚠️ Lesson 16, its trap list, its revision notes, Map 1 and register note 16 now state both figures side by side and say explicitly that they are not merged. ❌ The account's figure is not deleted and the verified figure is not silently substituted for it |
| **The Swiss and Norwegian votes of the early 1990s.** The account states that "the Swiss people voted not to join the Community (December 1992), and so did the Norwegians", in a passage otherwise about Maastricht ratification | ✅ **Clarified in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** Both were votes about **membership**, not about ratifying Maastricht: the Swiss referendum of December 1992 rejected membership of the European Economic Area, and the Norwegian electorate rejected accession itself. ⚠️ Lesson 18, register note 18 and Map 3 now separate membership refusal from ratification refusal, and Map 3 no longer lists Switzerland or Norway among the states whose voters refused ratification |
| **The width of the eastern European collapse window.** The account gives August 1988 to December 1991 for eastern Europe as a whole, while its country narratives complete most of the regime changes during 1989 | ✅ **Made precise in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** Lesson 1 now distinguishes the full window from the narrower fact that the communist monopoly was broken in the six non-Soviet Warsaw Pact states during 1989, with several first free elections following in 1990, and notes that Albania's multi-party elections came only in the spring of 1991 and the union dissolved in December 1991. ⚠️ Both the explanatory paragraph and the Lesson 1 concept-check model answer now carry the precise subset |
| **The spelling of transliterated names.** The extraction renders several Slavic names with damaged diacritics and one, the Polish Solidarity leader, in a corrupted form in two places | ⚠️ This session renders Slavic names without diacritics throughout and uses the standard English forms - Milosevic, Tudjman, Ceausescu, Walesa, Jaruzelski, Mazowiecki, Kwasniewski, Izetbegovic, Karadzic, Kostunica - which are the forms the account itself uses where its text is undamaged |

## The single bounded material-fact pass

⚠️ One verification pass was conducted for this session on 29 September 2026, limited to a finite list of standard connecting names and dates fixed **before** searching. ⚠️ The list was declared as: the formal title, place and entry into force of the German settlement; the standard designation of the Soviet renunciation of intervention; the standard designation and dates of the August 1991 coup body; the December 1991 republican instruments and the date of the union's formal end; the dissolution dates of the two bloc institutions; the names and dates of the three Paris instruments of November 1990 that the account leaves unnamed; the Malta meeting's date and status; the author, forum and date of the "new world order" formulation; the two named contemporary theses about the post-1991 world; the two NATO enlargement rounds falling inside this topic's period; the dates and authorisation status of the Kosovo air campaign together with the Council resolution framing its aftermath; and the judicial characterisation of the Srebrenica killings. ⚠️ Each was needed because the bounded base either omits the item entirely or is damaged at exactly the point where it would appear, and its omission would leave a real syllabus gap in the topic's owned scope. ⚠️ The minimum reliable source set was used, the pass stopped when the declared list was resolved, and no other item was pursued.

| Item verified | Where used | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany, signed at Moscow on 12 September 1990 by the two German states and the four former occupying powers, in force 15 March 1991 | Lesson 9 sections 4 and 5; Lesson 21 date control; register note 9; remediation family 1 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the canonical owner gives the signature date and the reunification date but neither the formal title, the place nor the entry into force, and a close question turns on the three distinct moments |
| The "Sinatra Doctrine" as the standard designation of the Soviet renunciation of intervention in the bloc, used by the Soviet foreign-ministry spokesman Gennadi Gerasimov in October 1989 | Lesson 5 section 4; register note 5 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the account describes the non-intervention mechanism at length without naming it, and the designation is how a question would refer to the reversal of the earlier doctrine of limited sovereignty |
| The August 1991 attempt: 19-21 August 1991, mounted by a self-declared State Committee on the State of Emergency, with Gorbachev detained and resistance organised from the Russian republic's leadership | Lesson 11 sections 3 and 5; cumulative check B2; register note 11 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the pages of the core account covering the coup are blank in the copy held for this session, and the canonical owners record the event without its dates or the body's designation |
| The agreement of 8 December 1991 by the Russian, Ukrainian and Belarusian leaders that the union had ceased to exist and that a Commonwealth of Independent States was created; the Alma-Ata Protocol of 21 December 1991 by which eight further republics joined; and the winding up of the union by its own legislature on the day after Gorbachev's resignation | Lesson 11 section 4; cumulative check B2; register note 11 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed for the same reason: the canonical owner records republican exit and the resignation of 25 December 1991 but not the instruments, and a dissolution taught without its instruments cannot support the constitutional point the lesson makes |
| The dissolution of the Council for Mutual Economic Assistance on 28 June 1991 and of the Warsaw Treaty Organisation on 1 July 1991 | Lesson 10 section 3; Lesson 19 sections 1 and 2; Map 1; register notes 10 and 19 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the account records the bloc's institutions and their function but not their termination dates, and the sequence rivalry-then-institutions-then-state is the lesson's central claim |
| The three Paris instruments of November 1990: the Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe and the Joint Declaration of Twenty-Two States, both of 19 November 1990, and the Charter of Paris for a New Europe of 21 November 1990 | Lesson 10 section 2; Map 1; register note 10; Mains question 6 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the account quotes the "no longer adversaries" language without naming the instrument that carries it, and the lesson's argument requires the distinction between a declaration, a force treaty and a normative charter |

Bush in an address to a joint session of Congress on 11 September 1990 during the Kuwait crisis, as a stated objective of the response | Lesson 14 section 1; Lesson 1 visual; register note 14 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the account attributes the claim only to "various American spokesmen", and the lesson's method - testing a programme against a record - requires the programme to have an author and a date |
| Francis Fukuyama's essay "The End of History?" of 1989 and Samuel Huntington's article "The Clash of Civilizations?" of 1993 as the two named contemporary theses about the post-1991 world | Lesson 14 section 5; Lesson 21 section 2; register note 14 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the advanced owner refers to the claim that one Western model had become history's universal endpoint without attribution, and the non-teleology discipline requires the thesis to be named in order to be rejected |

| The Kosovo air campaign, Operation Allied Force, 24 March to 10 June 1999, conducted without an express Security Council authorisation for the use of force; and the Council resolution of 10 June 1999 authorising the international civil and security presences | Lesson 17 sections 3 and 5; Lesson 15 category table; Map 3; register note 17 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the account calls the bombing controversial without stating why, and the whole humanitarian-intervention debate the owned scope requires turns on the authorisation status |
| The judicial characterisation of the Srebrenica killings of July 1995 as genocide by the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia in the Krstic appeal judgement of 2004 | The opening attribution discipline; Lesson 16 section 5; cumulative check C2; register note 16 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the owned scope requires sensitive conflict and mortality to be strictly attributed, and a judicial characterisation is the only attribution available that is neither the account's own adjective nor a contested estimate |

## Open gaps recorded rather than filled

1. ⚠️ **The core account's own narrative of the August 1991 coup and the immediate dissolution.** The pages carrying that narrative are blank in the copy held for this session. Lesson 11 therefore teaches the mechanism from the canonical owners, which record the failed hard-line coup, the destruction of party legitimacy and the acceleration of republican exits, supplemented by the declared material-fact pass for the dates, the body's designation and the December instruments. ❌ No detail is reproduced from the account for this episode, and the lesson's pre-teach line records the gap.
2. ⚠️ **The second page of the account's glasnost record.** One page of the Gorbachev section is blank, immediately after the Chernobyl sentence. Lesson 4 uses only the material legible on the surrounding pages, and the glasnost table is complete for those items alone. ❌ No inference is made about what the missing page contained.
3. ⚠️ **The 1993 constitutional conflict in Russia.** The pages carrying the account's narrative of the confrontation between president and parliament are blank. Lesson 12 therefore teaches the structural condition - a Soviet-era constitution unclear on the division of powers, a president without a supporting party, and the April 1993 referendum result of 53 per cent - which are legible, and does not narrate the confrontation itself.
4. ⚠️ **Part of the 1997-99 Russian economic narrative and part of the 2011-12 material.** Further blank pages affect both. Lesson 12 uses only the legible record on either side, which is why its later section moves from the 1997 Asian financial effect to the 2000s oil and growth record without a continuous narrative between them.
5. ⚠️ **Romanian December casualties.** The account gives no figures and the numbers in circulation were contested at the time. Lesson 8 reproduces the account's wording and records the uncertainty. ❌ No figure is supplied.
6. ⚠️ **The account's post-2013 material.** The fifth edition's narrative horizon is its publication year, and this session does not extend the historical narrative beyond it. Everything after that point in this session is either a dated current item - of which exactly one is used - or a standard connecting fact from the declared pass with a date inside the topic's period.
7. ⚠️ **The eurozone crisis after 2009.** Deliberately bounded. Lesson 18 records the strain on the enlargement settlement and hands the economic history to the world economy.
8. ⚠️ **The war on terrorism, Iraq 2003 and the Middle East after the Gulf War.** Deliberately bounded. The account's chapters on these subjects were read only far enough to establish the boundary, and their substance belongs to their own owners and to current international relations.
9. ⚠️ **The relative weight of Western pressure in ending the Cold War.** The account credits Gorbachev's initiative and Reagan's response together and supplies no basis for weighting them. Lesson 13 records this as an unresolved question rather than assigning a weight.
10. ⚠️ **The counterfactual for Kosovo.** What would have happened without the air campaign is the crux of the intervention debate and is unavailable from any source held here. Lesson 17 states this rather than implying an answer.
11. ⚠️ **The ranking of the three variables behind divergent transition outcomes.** Lessons 12 and 20 name an organised political counterpart, external resources and an external anchor, and the design of the economic programme, and decline to rank them on the evidence held here.
